#3010. Stock Market Today, Nov. 23: Micron Surges 19% as Nvidia CEO Change Calms AI Spending Fears

Today, Nov. 23, 2026, memory chipmaker Micron's rally came on volume well above its three-month average.

By RJ Fulton – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:00PM EST

Micron Technology (MU +19.04%) surged 19.04% on Monday, November 23, closing at $707.70 as semiconductor stocks staged a remarkable recovery following last week's steep sell-off. Nvidia's decision to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO helped restore confidence across the artificial intelligence (AI) industry, while commentary from Citi suggesting concerns over AI infrastructure spending may be overblown provided additional support for memory-chip stocks. Trading volume reached 49.1 million shares, approximately 72% above its three-month daily average of 28.6 million, highlighting strong investor participation in the rebound.

Led by semiconductor stocks, Wall Street staged a broad rebound following last week's steep sell-off. The S&P 500 (^GSPC +4.73%) surged 4.74% to close at 7,238, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +6.51%) jumped 6.51% to 24,090, reflecting a sharp improvement in investor sentiment.

The rally extended across the semiconductor industry, particularly among companies exposed to growing demand for AI infrastructure. Western Digital (WDC +13.84%) climbed 13.84% to $297.25, while NXP Semiconductors (NXPI +8.88%) gained 8.93% to $256.14. The widespread gains suggest investors are reassessing the outlook for chipmakers following a period of intense selling pressure and concerns surrounding AI-related capital spending.

Micron's sharp rebound highlights the market's continued sensitivity to developments surrounding artificial intelligence infrastructure spending. Although uncertainty around Nvidia's leadership has eased, the underlying demand for advanced memory remains the more important factor for Micron's long-term outlook. As AI models become increasingly complex and data centers require greater computing capacity, demand for high-performance memory could provide a substantial growth opportunity.

However, the memory semiconductor industry remains highly cyclical, with profitability heavily influenced by supply, demand, and pricing conditions. Following Monday's 19% rally, investors should be particularly mindful of whether Micron's earnings growth can justify its valuation, especially if major technology companies begin moderating their AI capital expenditures.

Looking ahead, Micron's appearance at the UBS Global Technology and AI Conference and its December 17 earnings report will provide important opportunities to assess these risks. Investors should focus on management's commentary regarding memory pricing, production capacity, and expected demand from AI customers. Sustained pricing strength and improving profitability would provide stronger evidence that Micron's recovery is supported by fundamentals rather than simply a rebound in semiconductor stocks.

#3009. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#3008. The Flipper One is more than a Zero 2.0 – it’s a $399 Linux computer with a $13M Kickstarter

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Linux

Backers are going big on the $759 Ultimate Bundle, but Flipper warns that rising RAM prices could affect production.

The Flipper One crowdfunding campaign has broken the $13-million mark, smashing the funding raised for previous Flipper devices and demonstrating strong interest in the successor to the Flipper Zero. The $399 Flipper One is a pocket Linux computer focused on networking, with delivery estimated for July 2027. But it isn’t simply an updated version of the Flipper Zero. It’s a very different animal and a much more serious bit of kit.

The thing that made the Flipper Zero so popular was how easy it was to use. You could fire it up and turn the volume down on the TV at your parents’ place, see the radio waves coming from your car’s key fob, and copy hotel room key cards.

Also: The Flipper One may be the ultimate Linux PC for hackers in 2026 – and I’m excited

It could do a lot, there wasn’t a steep learning curve, and you didn’t need to buy anything else. Did the fake videos on TikTok from people claiming they could change the prices at gas stations (I wish!) or start cars remotely help make the Zero more popular?

Unfortunately, yes. But what made it successful was that it packed a lot of power into a package that was small, fun, affordable, and easy to use.

If the Flipper Zero, with its NFC, RFID, sub-GHz, and IR, was kindergarten, the Flipper One is a college degree: a pocket Linux computer focused on networking.

This is reflected in the price. You can pick up a Zero for $169, while the Flipper One is $399.

What this buys you is the Flipper One base computer with a 256 × 144 grayscale LCD, Rockchip RK3576 8-core ARM SoC with NPU, a low-power MCU for handling the LCD and user interface, 8GB of LPDDR5 RAM, 64GB of UFS 2.2 storage, and a microSD slot.

If that $399 feels like a lot, it’s worth keeping in mind that, according to Flipper, the price only covers production costs and that the RAM and UFS storage account for at least half of the total hardware cost.

If you think this is a lot, you could spend a lot more. Adding a LoRa, SDR, or 5G module will push the total up a lot higher. And if you want all three modules, you’re looking at $759 for the Flipper One Ultimate Bundle.

Also: The Flipper One is the Linux cyberdeck I wish my Raspberry Pi could be

The reception has been strong enough that pledges have flown past the $300,000 goal that Flipper initially set, with over 24,000 backers pledging over $13 million. Close to 5,300 backers have gone for the Ultimate Bundle, compared to just over 4,300 choosing the bare Flipper One.

This suggests that backers really want the radio capabilities that this new platform offers.

If you want one, this crowdfunding run is a good time to get one because it’s a Kickstarter Special slashing 25% off an estimated retail price of $550.

The response over on Reddit has been a little more subdued, with some being taken aback by the price of the Zero’s bigger sibling.

Also: 7 useful things I can do with my Flipper Zero – as someone who’s used it for years

As someone who homebrews a lot of electronics, I’m going to say that the price is justified, and this project goes far beyond something you could put together with a Raspberry Pi and some code.

The Flipper One campaign runs until Dec. 17, 2026, with delivery estimated for July 2027.

Keep in mind that this is a Kickstarter pledge, not a purchase, and that rewards aren’t guaranteed. Flipper also says on the Kickstarter page that component shortages are the biggest risk to the project and that they “could cause significant delays to production and delivery.” It also says that if RAM prices continue to rise, it cannot “rule out adjusting some product specifications, including reducing the amount of RAM.”

Also: Is the Flipper Zero the best multi-tool for hacking?

And the good news here is that I’ve seen several analysts predicting that RAM prices will begin to go down in 2027 as demand eases off.

#3006. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh Just Laid Out 3 Scenarios for "Super Intelligence." 2 of Them Are "Less Positive," Including 1 Scenario That Outright Disappoints

The Federal Reserve has begun modeling the projected impacts of what the Trump administration calls "super intelligence" on the economy.

By Bram Berkowitz – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:00PM EST

As chair of the Federal Reserve's Board of Governors, Kevin Warsh has a powerful job. His words can move markets, and his view of the economy can play a big role in shaping the policy decisions that follow.

That's why his thinking on artificial intelligence, or what Warsh refers to as “super intelligence,” matters. Super intelligence (SI) is the term adopted by President Donald Trump and his administration to describe AI, and has become a driving force of both corporate spending and stock market gains. Whether it delivers on its promise could impact growth, jobs, and inflation for years. These are the very concerns the Fed is charged with managing, so it has to try to figure out what's coming before it arrives.

On Nov. 16, at a conference for the Fed's Productivity and Jobs Task Force hosted by the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco, Warsh said the Fed is modeling three possible paths for the economy as AI continues to develop.

Two of these scenarios are "less positive," including one where artificial intelligence outright disappoints.

Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.

During his opening remarks, Warsh said Fed staff had built three illustrative scenarios over the next several years, stressing that they are not forecasts and that he was not there to offer predictions.

The first is the one that every investor hopes for. Productivity rises, and the gains are shared evenly between workers and those who manage and own capital. In this scenario, growth remains above its long-run average, and the economy remains near full employment.

The second scenario is interesting because it doesn't require artificial intelligence to fail. It requires it to work.

"An SI-driven expansion could bring substantial labor market disruption as some jobs are displaced, rather than complemented, by technology," Warsh said. "GDP growth would still rise above its long-term average. But widespread automation, combined with labor market frictions, could push the jobless rate above levels consistent with full employment."

The third is a letdown, where "SI could disappoint." After heavy investment and the strong market rally we've seen, returns on capital are lower than expected, a concern some investors already share. This leads to a decline in valuations, and the economy slows before eventually recovering without the productivity gains.

Again, these aren't predictions from Warsh, who has tried to avoid any kind of forward guidance since becoming chair. Warsh also noted, "many other outcomes -- probably including one that will prove most accurate -- could be drawn."

Still, if the Fed is beginning to model these three scenarios, it's likely at least somewhat on the minds of Warsh and the rest of the Fed's board. Interestingly, the first and third scenarios are easier to understand because they are more black-and-white. Either AI works, benefiting the economy, or it doesn't, and the economy and likely the stock market struggle.

But it's the second that I find most interesting, and likely the one most investors are not factoring in.

A successful AI boom that leaves workers behind is harder to see coming and likely harder to model. It's also one that could make it difficult for the Fed to manage its two goals of stable prices and maximum employment, because this scenario could push them in different directions.

If AI delivers on its promise, companies will use it to do more with fewer people. This would lift margins and productivity in the near term, which is exactly what bulls are counting on, and may already be happening this year.

But the people who lose their jobs or are forced to switch to lower-paying jobs are also consumers. Consumer spending accounts for more than two-thirds of the U.S. economy.

Fewer or lower paychecks, or less confidence in keeping them, means less spending power. Over time, that could hit the revenue of the same companies that saved money by cutting costs.

AI investment is already a major source of growth. Business equipment spending jumped nearly 21% in the third quarter, helping lift GDP growth to 3.4%, according to Reuters. But this doesn't replace the spending of millions of households.

Nobody knows what will happen. Everything may work out, but investors should remember that forecasts rarely pan out as expected. The best case for AI isn't necessarily the best case for the consumers who power the economy.

Long-term investors should remain invested, but it's important to consider these scenarios and make sure your portfolio is prepared.

#3005. Your Google AI mode searches can stay private now with ‘temporary threads’ – here’s how

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

With temporary threads, any searches you run in Google’s AI Mode won’t be associated with your account or saved to your chat history.

Most of the major AIs offer a temporary chat or incognito option in which your conversation remains private and vanishes as soon as your chat ends. Now Google has jumped on board this bandwagon with its AI Mode searches.

Also: Google is making it easier to opt out of Gemini AI training

New to Google’s AI Mode is an option known as temporary threads. By choosing this setting, any searches you conduct in AI mode are detached from your own Google account and history. Your temporary searches won’t be affected by your past preferences or chats. They won’t be impacted by any personalization you’ve set up through your account. And they won’t be saved to your chat history. The goal here is to prevent anyone else, including Google, from accessing your conversations in this mode.

This option is currently rolling out around the world in all languages to Google search on both desktop and mobile browsers. You can run a temporary thread on any device and in any browser.

Try this for yourself: Head to the regular Google search page and click the button on the search field for AI Mode. You should then see an icon with a pen hovering over a dotted circle. If you don’t see it, you may have to run a search to get started. On the results page, you should then spot the icon.

Hover over the icon, and the tooltip will say: “Start temporary thread.” Click the icon on the left to start a new thread. Now, any conversation you kick off will be temporary and unconnected to your account. But you don’t have to stick with just plain old text. Select the plus icon to upload an image or file. Select the microphone icon to speak instead of type.

Also: Goodbye, Gemini Gems: How to use Google’s new Skills

Certain limitations are in effect with a temporary thread. Specifically, you can’t do any of the following:

Are these temporary threads truly private? Here’s what Google says:

“Temporary threads will not be saved to your Google account and disappear after 24 hours,” Google said on a support page. “They won’t be used to train Google’s generative AI models, unless you submit feedback or it’s necessary for safety purposes. Google will still collect data and use data that is not associated with your account, including to keep our services working smoothly and safely and to support business operations.”

Google also warns that your browser may still save your history if you visit a website within a temporary thread. Data could still be collected by the sites you visit and the services those sites use, including Google services like Google Analytics.

Also: I asked Gemini Notebook to ‘make me a site’ from my notes

ChatGPT, Gemini, and Microsoft Copilot all offer a temporary chat mode that also promises to keep your chats private and unconnected to your account. Claude and Perplexity provide the same feature but call it incognito mode.

#3004. Jensen Huang Is Back as Nvidia CEO With the US Government’s Blessing

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The decision was made with "input from senior levels of the United States government," according to the board.

Nvidia announced on Monday that ousted cofounder Jensen Huang would return to his role as CEO after he resigned earlier this month when multiple extramarital affairs became public, including one with a woman that the US intelligence community believes is a Chinese intelligence asset.

“The board believes Jensen is the right person to lead Nvidia, and that his reinstatement is in the best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers,” said Harvey C. Jones, chair of Nvidia’s board. “We have every confidence in Jensen and look forward to working with him as he resumes his leadership of the company.”

The stunning news comes after weeks of reporting on alleged malfeasance in Huang’s personal and professional lives. The scandal broke when The New York Times reported that Huang had a close, personal relationship with a woman suspected of being a Chinese intelligence asset. Then came news of a second extramarital affair, leading to Huang’s eventual resignation.

Previously, the board denied it would bring Huang back, despite Huang saying he would return if the board allowed it. Many names were floated as a possible replacement for Huang, including Arm CEO Rene Haas. But many tech experts said it was difficult to imagine Nvidia without Huang. Apparently, the board agreed.

Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress temporarily took over as interim CEO, telling investors at its recent earnings call that everything is “business as usual.” But additional reporting revealed that Huang regularly “humiliated” his direct reports, raising serious questions about his management style and the corporate culture of Nvidia, which Huang helped shape more than any other person over the past 20 years.

Nvidia’s stock price rose dramatically on Monday morning with the news of Huang’s return, dragging with it other chipmakers like AMD and Qualcomm and market indices including the S&P 500, Nasdaq and Dow, which all jumped up on Monday. It’s another sign that Nvidia, as a key company in the AI industry, is a driving force behind the US and global economy.

The board added that it received input from “senior levels of the United States government” on the decision. President Donald Trump said in a Truth Social post that Nvidia made the “correct and proper decision” to reinstate Huang, adding, “The Attorney General of the United States has confirmed that the US Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct.”

The board’s independently commissioned investigation into Huang will continue, but he will regain his seat on the board. Huang is still set to testify before the Senate’s banking committee on Dec. 3, and the SEC’s investigation into Nvidia is also still set to continue.

Calling into CNBC’s Squawk Box on Monday morning, Trump reiterated that Huang’s return is good news for the country. He called Huang a “genius,” saying, “We have to protect our geniuses … The bigger threat is losing the brainpower of a person like Jensen.” In a rambling interview, Trump talked about the importance of Nvidia’s industry-leading chips.

Trump previously floated the idea of former Apple CEO Tim Cook taking over Nvidia’s top job, which gained zero traction. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has been pushing for further scrutiny of Huang and Nvidia’s compliance with US export control rules. While Trump loosened the rules around how US chipmakers can do business with Chinese companies, there are still bans on selling the most advanced chips.

But we need only look to the stock market to see why the Trump administration may be changing its tune. Since the beginning of the scandal, Nvidia’s stock price has fallen dramatically, wiping out billions in market value. Monday morning’s news of Huang’s return saw premarket trading hike its share price by 30%. With Nvidia, so goes the entire stock market. And after last week’s Anthropic IPO failed to quell concerns over a growing “AI bubble” — where Nvidia is smack-dab in the center — the rebound points to investors’ confidence in Huang, Nvidia and the AI industry, spies and affairs be damned.

Within Silicon Valley, some have continually pushed for Huang’s return. Elon Musk posted an ominous “It is done” on Monday morning; Epic CEO Tim Sweeney posted a link to AC/DC’s Back in Black, a nod to Huang’s affinity for expensive black leather jackets.

The tech industry, perhaps more than any other, thrives on the narrative of the god-like startup founder. It’s why Jensen Huang was synonymous with Nvidia, Sam Altman with OpenAI and Dario Amodei with Anthropic. And this is not the first time a major tech CEO’s departure has caused such a commotion.

“[Huang’s] return may help restore investor confidence, but the upheaval echoes Sam Altman’s brief ouster and reinstatement at OpenAI,” Emarketer analyst Jacob Bourne said, “raising similar questions about corporate governance and the outsized influence of individual leaders over the future of AI.”

Altman was fired and reinstated within a chaotic five-day period in 2023. The OpenAI board accused Altman of lying to them, but he was ultimately welcomed back and has kept a firm grip on the now-huge AI company ever since. This episode is the basis for Luca Guadagnino’s upcoming movie, Artificial, starring Andrew Garfield as Altman, coming out next month.

Those tech founders have become increasingly intertwined with government power, particularly since Trump’s return to office. Many Big Tech executives, including Huang, have donated billions in personal funds for Trump’s projects, such as his inauguration fund and plans for a White House ballroom. It’s led many tech critics to argue that Big Tech is buying influence to avoid regulation and oversight.

#3003. Colgate-Palmolive vs. McDonald's: Which Consumer Goods Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Two consumer goods company giants, one leaning on Walmart and the other on thousands of franchisees, offer very different routes to steady returns.

By Lawrence Rothman, CFA – Updated Nov 23, 2026 at 5:11PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Colgate-Palmolive ›

When market volatility rises, many investors turn toward large and established companies for stability. Choosing between Colgate-Palmolive (CL -0.79%) and McDonald's (MCD +1.15%) requires weighing a household goods leader against a global fast-food powerhouse.

Colgate-Palmolive focuses on essential household items like toothpaste and soap, offering stability through products people buy regardless of the economy. McDonald's dominates the quick-service restaurant industry by leveraging a massive franchise network that generates recurring rent and royalty income. While one provides daily necessities and the other offers affordable convenience, both represent mature businesses that investors often compare for long-term consistency.

Colgate-Palmolive operates as a global leader in segments like oral care, home care, and pet nutrition through brands such as Colgate and Hill's. These essential products are sold in over 200 countries, making the company a prominent name among consumer staples stocks. Investors should note that nearly 11% of its 2025 sales came from Walmart (WMT +1.27%), creating a customer concentration that gives the retailer significant bargaining power over trade discounts.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, revenue reached $21.3 billion, which represents a growth rate of approximately 5.9% compared to the prior 12-month period. The company reported net income of $2.2 billion during this same period as it navigated a complex global environment. This performance reflects top-line expansion across its various geographic regions even as it faces competition from smaller, digital-first brands.

McDonald's utilizes a franchise-heavy business model where roughly 95% of its 45,000 restaurants are managed by independent owners. These franchisees provide the company with steady streams of rent and royalties, which support a high net margin.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, revenue reached $27.8 billion, representing a growth rate of roughly 5.8% over the previous 12-month period. The company recorded net income of $9.0 billion. McDonald's reported a net margin of 32.4% during this time frame, illustrating the profitability inherent in its franchise-led structure.

Colgate-Palmolive faces significant risks from its global footprint, as geopolitical instability in regions like the Middle East can disrupt operations. The company also deals with intense competition from private labels. Additionally, evolving global regulations concerning environmental impact and product safety require ongoing investment to ensure compliance.

McDonald's depends on the financial health of its franchisees to execute its expansion plans, and any broad economic downturn could impact its royalty streams. The business is also susceptible to reputational damage from food safety incidents and recent class action lawsuits regarding product labeling and marketing claims.

McDonald's carries a lower Forward P/E, while Colgate-Palmolive appears more attractive based on its P/S ratio, which compares market value to sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

While neither company is a fast grower, they have generated positive sales and earnings gains. That's positive for mature companies.

Additionally, both have made strong commitments to paying dividends to shareholders. That's an important component of their total returns. In fact, Colgate and McDonald's have increased payments annually for 63 and 50 straight years, respectively. That makes them Dividend Kings, or companies that have raised dividends for at least 50 consecutive years.

Both have sufficient earnings to support dividends, too. Colgate-Palmolive has a payout ratio, or dividends divided by earnings, of 82%. Meanwhile, McDonald's has a 59% payout ratio.

Turning to the dividend yields. McDonald's 2.8% yield surpasses Colgate's 2.2% yield.

The decision comes down to the underlying businesses and dividends. With McDonald's higher dividend yield and asset-light business conducive to free cash flow generation, that stock is my choice over Colgate-Palmolive.

#3002. I’ve tested countless Linux distros, and Pond is unlike anything I’ve tried – here’s why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Linux

Feeling adventurous? This Linux distribution is exciting, efficient and totally unique.

Linux is all about choice. From the kernel all the way up to the desktop environment, you get to determine what is used and even how it is used. The staggering number of distributions, desktops, package managers, file managers, and terminal apps provide you with a nearly infinite combination of choices. That choice is and has always been a key aspect of Linux.

Also: My favorite Linux desktop environment for laptops surprised even me

So too is innovation. If there’s one thing that separates Linux from all other operating systems, it’s the willingness to try something different. Linux developers are always pushing the limits of what an operating system is, and this new take on the OS is the epitome of that.

The distribution in question is called Pond. According to the developers, “Pond is an opportunity for us to play with familiar conventions and experiment with new ideas that make a computer more natural and satisfying.”

The developers have achieved something special. Pond takes the standard UI and turns it on its head. Part tiling window manager, part mobile UI, part GNOME, and part something we’ve never before seen, this desktop environment is special. It took me all of five minutes to fall in love with what they’ve created.

Also: The only 4 Linux email clients I recommend

If you’ve ever considered using a tiling window manager, but thought the lack of a mouse made it seem a bit too complicated, Pond is exactly what you want. Here’s how it works:

Pond uses the Niri window manager to arrange windows in columns along an infinite strip from left to right. In multi-monitor setups, each display gets its own strip, so windows never “overflow” from one monitor to the next.

Also: How to install Arch Linux without losing your mind

At the same time, workspaces are dynamic, and there’s always a new workspace automatically created to the right of the farthest-right window. The combination of the Pond sidebar and Niri’s way of handling windows makes this UI truly original.

Pond doesn’t have a traditional desktop menu. Instead, you open the launcher (using the Super+Space key combination), type the name of the app you want to install, and hit Enter when the app name appears on both the display and as an icon in the top portion of the sidebar.

Another thing that sets Pond apart is how you install apps. Yes, you can open a terminal window and install an app using the pacman command, or you can open the launcher, type install, press Enter, and then type the name of the app you want to install. For instance, if you wanted to install LibreOffice, you would type:

Once you hit Enter a second time, the installation will begin. Many apps install via Flatpak, but on the off-chance an app must be installed via the pacman package manager, you’ll be prompted to type your user password before the installation begins.

As I hinted earlier, Pond is based on Arch Linux, which means it’s a rolling-release distribution. One issue that did give me pause was the lack of a GUI update system, which indicates that updates would have to happen in the terminal, like so:

I installed the Shelly GUI frontend, which does have an update component. Personally, I would simply open the terminal window, type sudo pacman -Syu, hit Enter, and let the update run in the background. But if a GUI is your jam, Shelly is what you want.

Also: A super fast distro for anyone ready to move beyond beginner Linux

It would be sweet if the developers allowed updating the system from within the launcher, so it didn’t require opening the terminal first. The Pond launcher does allow running system commands from the launcher, which will then open the terminal window and execute the command. For instance, open the terminal, type a command, hit Enter, and then scroll through the list of available commands that can be run.

Pond ships with kernel 7.3.4-3, so it has outstanding hardware recognition and performance.

You should definitely give Pond a try, especially if you’re adventurous and want to experience something completely different. If, on the other hand, different is not in your stream, then Pond will frustrate you to no end.

Also: Why Nextcloud Office is my new favorite free productivity suite

Pond excited me more than any OS has in a very long time; so much so that I could see this becoming my go-to OS, once it reaches stable release. Until then, I will keep Pond installed on a laptop, keep upgrading, and see what happens next.

Kudos to the Pond OS team for coming up with something truly unique in an already competitive world.

#3001. Charging to 100% Every Night Wears Down Your Phone’s Battery. Try This Instead

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Heat and long stretches at a full charge put the most strain on a phone battery. A few small changes keep both in check.

Apple says iPhone batteries are most comfortable at temperatures between 62 and 72 degrees Fahrenheit, and charging outside that range, whether in a hot car or inside a thick case, speeds up wear. Heat is one of two things that age a phone battery faster than normal use. The other is time spent at 100%. Avoiding both takes very little effort, and the charge limit settings on iPhones, Pixels and Galaxy phones handle part of the job for you. Here’s how to charge in a way that keeps your battery healthier for longer.

Voltage stress at the top of the charge range, heat from fast charging and the cumulative effects of full discharge cycles all accelerate capacity loss. The damage may not appear immediately, but it’s measurable after one year and obvious after two.

Age is part of it, sure, but the overnight charging habit accelerates the process considerably. Lithium-ion batteries lose capacity faster when they spend extended time at the top of their charge range, which is precisely where an overnight charge leaves them. The fix doesn’t require a new phone or even a new charger. It requires a small adjustment to a habit most people have never thought twice about.

Keeping a lithium-ion cell pinned at 100% creates voltage stress, and the heat generated from sitting on a charger could end up meaning the difference between keeping your current phone a little longer or having to buy a new one. So once and for all, does keeping your phone plugged in really hurt the battery? Here’s what the experts suggest.

It isn’t about immediate damage but rather how fast your battery ages over time. Understanding the difference between what will and won’t break your phone is key. Here is the truth about your charging habits.

Battery health is not just about how many times you charge your phone. It is also about how it manages voltage, temperature and maintenance. Lithium-ion batteries age fastest when they are exposed to extreme levels: 0% and 100%.

Keeping these batteries near full charge for long stretches of time puts additional voltage stress on the cathode and electrolyte. This is why many devices use “trickle charging” or temporarily pause at 100%, topping up only when needed.

Still, the biggest threat is not overcharging, but heat. When your phone is plugged in and running demanding apps, it produces heat that accelerates chemical wear inside the battery. If you are gaming, streaming or charging on a hot day, that extra warmth does far more harm than leaving the cable plugged in overnight.

The Apple battery guide describes lithium-ion batteries as “consumable components” that naturally lose capacity over time. To slow that decline, iPhones use Optimized Battery Charging, which learns your daily routine and pauses charging at about 80% until just before you typically unplug, reducing time spent at high voltage.

Apple also advises keeping devices between 0 and 35 degrees Celsius (32 and 95 degrees Fahrenheit) and removing certain cases while charging to improve heat dissipation. You can read more on the official Apple battery support page.

Samsung offers a similar feature called Battery Protect, found in the One UI battery and device care settings. When enabled, it caps charging at 85%, which helps reduce stress during long charging sessions.

Other Android makers like Google, OnePlus and Xiaomi include comparable options — often called Adaptive Charging, Optimized Charging or Battery Care — that dynamically slow power delivery or limit charge based on your habits. These systems make it safe to leave your phone plugged in for extended periods without fear of overcharging.

Even with these safeguards, some conditions can accelerate battery wear. As mentioned before, the most common culprit is high temperatures. Even for a short period of time, leaving your phone charging in direct sunlight, in a car or under a pillow can push temperatures into unsafe zones.

Heavy use, such as gaming or 4K video editing, while charging can also cause temperature spikes which can degrade the battery faster. And cheap, uncertified cables or adapters may deliver unstable current that stresses cells. If your battery is already several years old, it will naturally be more sensitive to this kind of strain.

There is no need to overhaul your habits, but a few tweaks can help your battery age gracefully. Start by turning on the optimization tools on your phone: Optimized Battery Charging on iPhones, Battery Protect on Samsung devices and Adaptive Charging on Google Pixels. These systems learn your routine and adjust charging speed so your phone is not sitting at 100% all night.

Keep your phone cool while charging. According to Apple, phone batteries perform best between 62 and 72 degrees Fahrenheit (16 to 22 degrees Celsius). If your phone feels hot, remove its case or move it to a better ventilated or shaded spot. Avoid tossing your phone under a pillow or too close to other electronics, like your laptop. Skip wireless chargers that trap heat overnight. Use quality chargers and cables from your phone manufacturer or trusted brands. Those cheap “fast-charge” kits you find online often deliver inconsistent current, which can cause long-term issues.

Finally, try not to obsess over topping off the charge. It is perfectly fine to plug in your phone during the day for short bursts. Lithium-ion batteries actually prefer frequent, shallow charges rather than deep full cycles. There is no need to keep it between 20% and 80% all the time, but just avoid extremes when possible.

Keeping your phone plugged in overnight or on your desk all day will not destroy its battery. That is a leftover myth from a different era of tech. Modern phones are smart enough to protect themselves, and features such as Optimized Battery Charging or Battery Protect do most of the heavy lifting for you.

Still, no battery lasts forever. The best way to slow the inevitable is to manage heat, use quality chargers and let the software on your phone do its job. Think of it less as “babying” your battery and more as charging with intention. A few mindful habits today can keep your phone running strong for years to come.

#3000. Meta Muse booked an Airbnb without permission – and that’s not the worst of it

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Also: Don’t use Muse? Meta’s AI agent can still access your private messages

According to a Reuters investigation, Meta employees testing Muse said the agent messaged them about topics they hadn’t asked it about, including career advice, a follow-up on a request for childcare, products they hadn’t purchased, and, crucially, bank account balances they didn’t recognize.

These incidents “prompted unnerved employees to raise concerns it could be mixing up conversations between different accounts and exposing users’ private data,” Reuters said.

Reuters said “an employee told colleagues his Muse had randomly added an extra message to its regular chats about fishing-related weather and tide information, saying: ‘BTW, two bank updates, your Capital One balance is $2,722.58, your Charles Schwab balance is $5,899.57.'” In another instance, Muse booked a non-refundable Airbnb for an employee who hadn’t instructed it to.

Muse notified another employee, who was using the agent on her personal phone, about a message she’d received on Signal, though she said she hadn’t given the agent access to the encrypted messaging app.

Reuters and the AI Now Institute determined, through testing on an Android phone and a Mac, that Muse used “permissions granted during the bot’s initial setup to pull in the message text, leveraging those authorizations in ways a user might not anticipate.” Because Muse is set up with automatic access to Android push notifications, it didn’t need explicit app access to read messages.

The tests did not work on an iPhone, suggesting better protections against Muse’s prying.

Similarly, in September, a user reported Muse had shared his address with a Facebook Marketplace buyer before checking with him. The incident was ultimately a misunderstanding between the terms the user thought he’d consented to and how much access he was actually handing over, which exemplifies that Meta and the user may have explicitly different definitions of access, and that Muse can take advantage of that grey area.

Meta told Reuters these incidents were either isolated or the result of hallucinations, though Reuters could not confirm that.

Reuters’ findings may provide an opportunity for Google’s agentic offering, now called Tasks (formerly Spark), which it made free (with usage limits, like Muse) this week.

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Xiaomi's bonkers dual-screen phone is here and I've tested its camera.

Editor at Large; Lead Photographer, Europe

Editor at Large; Lead Photographer, Europe

Xiaomi has taken the wraps off its latest 18 Pro Max Android phone in Paris. I’ve had the phone for the past few weeks and have been putting the camera to the test in my beautiful home of Edinburgh, Scotland, as well as on the beautiful (and rainy) streets of Paris. You can check out my review of the phone if you’re keen to hear more about that wild rear screen on the back, but here I wanted to share some of my favorite photos I’ve taken with the phone so far.

The images seen here are taken with the phone’s various built-in camera modes, largely the black and white mode, as I love the dramatic, filmic look it produces. They’re presented either as-shot or with minimal adjustments using the phone’s built-in editing tools.

Taken using one of the phone’s built-in color styles, I love the vibrancy in this shot. The reds really pop, while the wider angle of the main camera emphasizes the tunnel effect of the alleyway, drawing the eye to the person and their dog.

I may be spoiling the rest of the images here by saying that this is my absolute favorite shot I’ve taken, but there we go. I positioned the figure between the buildings and had the phone almost on the floor to turn the rain-soaked tiles into a reflective surface to add some drama to this scene. The phone captures excellent black-and-white photos at night, too.

Back in color, this UFO that had presumably crash-landed in this Parisian market looks great in this filmic color mode. The oranges and reds pop against the other tones in this scene.

While this quick street portrait is similarly punchy.

I used manual mode here, taking full control over the white balance and exposure settings to achieve the tones I wanted. It’s a great-looking shot with excellent dynamic range. Xiaomi’s phone doesn’t appear to go too hard with its software processing either, meaning that even fine details look quite natural. Other Android phone-makers could take note.

As with the photo of Toulouse the cat, I exposed for the highlights here, forcing the rest of the scene under this bridge into shadow. As a result, it looks moody and dramatic but crucially it also looks exactly as I hoped it would. Many phones would try to artificially lift the shadows here, which would ruin the vibe I was going for.

I took this with the phone’s telephoto zoom lens, aiming to focus more on the digger as the subject and use the water for reflection. I love how this shot looks, especially in black and white, as the cloud detail behind it is especially noticeable.

While here I used the full wide angle of the main camera in order to create a lot of negative space around the figures, emphasizing the size of the building around them.

That building was Le Grande Arche in Paris and despite it being a miserably cold, wet and cloudy day I think the phone has captured a satisfyingly stark scene here.

I don’t like flying, so I distracted myself on the flight out to Paris by taking photos of things with the Xiaomi 18 Pro Max, including this man having a look out of the window.

And this shot of my plane’s wing somewhere above the UK. Again, in black and white. When it looks this good, why would I choose anything else?

And while we’re still in black and white, a particularly friendly pigeon. Hi, friend!

And to round us off, this dude in shades looking at a menu.

I’m genuinely really thrilled with so many of the shots I’ve taken with this phone. The black-and-white mode in particular is spectacular, and I’m looking forward to taking more shots with it over the coming weeks and months.

The recently renovated Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort offers great views, a well-equipped fitness center and delicious food.

Hilton is making a big play in South Florida with its Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort. The massive 1,000-room property — which underwent an $80 million renovation before opening under the Signia Hilton brand in May 2026 — sits on the beach in Hollywood, Florida, just outside of Fort Lauderdale.

The resort caters primarily to business and group travelers (it’s attached to a massive events space), but it’s also a strong option for vacationers looking for some R&R. The main downside: The location isn’t great if your trip primarily revolves around off-property excursions, dining and nightlife.

Room rates typically run $300 to $600 a night, depending on the season, but you can also use Hilton Honors benefits to make your stay more affordable. There’s also a resort fee of $59 per night — but Hilton waives that when you book using Hilton points.

Hilton covered the cost of my stay for two nights, but the opinions in this review are my own. Here’s what guests can expect at this oceanfront resort.

Since the Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort is part of Hilton Honors, there are several ways to book a stay there.

If you’re using Hilton points, expect to pay 70,000 to 80,000 points a night when standard rooms are available. Hilton elite status — a benefit you can earn by holding any Hilton Honors credit card — gets you the fifth-night-free perk, where you stay for at least five nights but pay for four when booking with points.

For example, if you were to stay five nights at 80,000 points a night, you’d pay 320,000 points total, bringing down your average nightly rate to 64,000 points.

You can also book using Hilton’s generous free night certificates, which can be used at almost any property in the portfolio. If you hold the Hilton Honors American Express Aspire Card, you automatically get a certificate each year. It’s also possible to earn certificates through reaching spending thresholds on the card and on the Hilton Honors American Express Surpass® Card. Terms apply.

Make sure to check the cash rate against the points rate to make sure you’re getting good value from your redemption. NerdWallet values Hilton points at 0.4 cent apiece.

For cash bookings of two nights or more, you might consider using The Hotel Collection via American Express’ travel portal for eligible cardholders. Eligible bookings come with benefits like a $100 property credit and other amenities subject to availability, including a room upgrade, early check-in and late checkout.

Keep in mind that third-party bookings typically don’t earn Hilton points or count toward elite status qualification.

It’s hard to miss the Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort, with its signature H-shaped facade and sleek white-and-glass exterior.

Stepping into the hotel, I was immediately struck by the lush greenery filling the entrance and indoor courtyard. The grand lobby has soaring glass ceilings, giving the space a greenhouse feel, although the strong air conditioning keeps you cool.

Despite its massive size, it doesn’t have a sprawling layout, which I appreciated. Everything is close together, and you can walk from the check-in counter to any of the indoor restaurants in under a minute.

I stayed in an oceanfront suite with a balcony, which was spacious and had a fun beachy vibe. To get to the rooms, you’ll need to use either the north or south tower elevator banks.

In the living room, there was a large sitting area featuring a TV, a couch, an accent chair and a four-person high-top table.

Although I didn’t spend too much time on the balcony — the October balminess was a bit too much for me — I enjoyed stepping out each morning to admire the view.

The bedroom had a king bed with extremely comfortable bedding and an oversized chair that was a great spot to relax and read a book by the window.

The bathroom was spacious, featuring a shower and a separate tub (something every room has).

Even the standard room I toured felt roomy. I liked that it shared similar elements with the suite, including the oversized lounge chair.

This is what you’d normally get when booking a room at the cheapest points price.

There are several dining venues on the property, ranging from a grab-and-go cafe to a high-end steakhouse. It is a luxury resort, so expect the prices to reflect that.

For breakfast, you can head to Palmea Kitchen, which is also open for lunch. I had the breakfast buffet, which offered a nice spread of classics, from hot food items and pastries to cold cuts and smoked salmon.

If you don’t have time for a sit-down breakfast, you can head next door to The Press, which offers made-to-order and grab-and-go food options throughout the day. You can also get smoothies, juices, snacks, pastries and other sweet treats.

The Canteen is another grab-and-go option that also sells snacks, drinks, souvenirs and sundries, similar to a convenience store.

For lunch and dinner, try Solara. It doesn’t have a traditional restaurant feel, as it’s mostly an indoor-outdoor bar space. One side connects to the lobby, while the other side faces the Solara adults-only pool.

It serves a lunch menu, as well as a substantial bar food menu that’s available late into the night.

You can also eat at Playa Beach Bar & Grill, a beachside restaurant serving Latin-inspired cuisine. I enjoyed spending a lazy lunch there and watching the waves crash onto the shore.

For a fancy sit-down dinner, go to Diplomat Prime, the property’s steakhouse. We had a group dinner at the restaurant, and the meats were, of course, the highlight. But don’t sleep on the seafood, which was also delicious.

One of the best parts of the Dip, as it’s affectionately known, is its location right on the beach. Guests can access the stretch of beach directly adjacent to the property. But it’s not a private beach, so you might encounter crowds.

I didn’t spend any time on the beach itself — it was extremely windy while I was there — but the hotel’s resort fee includes amenities like a beach lounge (umbrella not included), a daily one-hour kayak or paddleboard rental, and beachfront yoga classes.

If you don’t want to get sand everywhere, you can head to one of the three pool options: the adults-only Solara pool, the family-friendly lagoon pool and the Verandah Social Club for those 21 and older (requires paid access).

The family-friendly lagoon spans the length of the property and runs beneath the Solara pool. It also has its own hot tub.

I spent an afternoon in a cabana at the Verandah Social Club, which has some smaller plunge pools and a hot tub.

I appreciated the attentive service from the staff there, who constantly checked on whether guests needed anything.

If you’re looking for other ways to stay active, head to the two-story fitness center, featuring a variety of equipment like free weights, exercise bikes, a rowing machine and treadmills.

Next door, you’ll find the spa and wellness center. I had a relaxing massage that incorporated hot stones and aromatherapy.

The locker rooms come with a sauna and steam room, although I wished there were more amenities like a hot tub and cold plunge.

One of the resort fee inclusions is complimentary high-intensity interval training workout classes at the gym across the street. I signed up for an early morning class. The instructor was friendly, and it was a great way to start my day.

Other amenities include a complimentary one-hour e-bike rental each day, as well as a kids’ club with various activities (but drop-off fees apply).

I had a fantastic stay at this resort, but I’d recommend it for a specific traveler.

If you’re planning to stay on-property your entire trip, it’s a great option. That’s probably why it’s such a popular convention hotel, since everything you need is easily accessible.

I loved the hotel’s design, including the jungle-like atmosphere in the lobby, and I found the dining options to be tasty, albeit pricey.

But if your trip revolves around visiting Miami attractions, you’ll likely want to stay closer to the action.

#2997. Why ASML Stock Popped Today

The AI sector soared as Jensen Huang returned to Nvidia.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:16PM EST

Shares of ASML (ASML +9.28%) were among the winners today as the semiconductor sector surged on news that Jensen Huang was returning as the CEO of Nvidia.

The announcement reinjected confidence in the AI boom, lifting sector stocks like ASML, which finished the session up 9.3, while the Nasdaq Composite closed up 6.5%.

ASML is the world's only maker of extreme ultraviolet lithography (EUV) machines for making the world's most advanced chips.

These are highly complex, very expensive machines, and the company sells only dozens of them each quarter.

It's a cyclical business downstream of broader chip demand, including more advanced chips used for AI. Therefore, the strengthening of the AI ecosystem is good news for ASML, and investors believe AI demand and its potential growth are stronger with Jensen Huang back running Nvidia.

Because of the nature of its business, ASML's results can swing quarter to quarter, so the long-term outlook is crucial for the stock. It already stands to benefit from the shortage in memory as orders from memory chipmakers look to expand capacity, and it should benefit from the continued growth of AI demand, which is supported by having Huang back as the Nvidia CEO.

ASML is targeting revenue of 44 billion-60 billion euros for 2030. While the company won't benefit directly from the return of Huang, investors should look for that figure to move higher as a sign that ASML is benefiting from tailwinds from AI growth.

#2994. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.
