#2993. Can AI plan terrorist attacks? The US tested ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and Grok

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Investigators tried to bypass AI safeguards to generate plans. The results exposed security gaps and raised questions about preventing misuse.

The Government Accountability Office (GAO) conducted a series of tests earlier this year designed to determine whether users could bypass guardrails and convince AIs to plan terrorist attacks. The investigators succeeded. In October, the agency published a report titled “Covert Testing Used Common Platforms to Create Viable Attack Plans,” which was released to the public in November.

The tests were conducted using Claude, Gemini, ChatGPT, and Grok. All four AIs produced attack scenarios that subject matter experts were able to confirm would have mostly succeeded.

I spoke via email with the report’s lead author, Kevin Walsh, director of IT and cybersecurity audits at the GAO. He told ZDNET:

In general, the details provided by the four AI platforms are not novel. Much of the information is readily available on the internet. But these plans were generated in hours, rather than weeks or months. AI could also surface ideas or methods that may not have been previously considered.

To the companies’ credit, their safeguards have continued to evolve since we conducted these tests. The companies also assure us that this is an area of concern and ongoing research. However, our report concludes that, in its current state, this technology can be dangerous, and its misuse represents a growing risk to our nation.

The Government Accountability Office is an independent, nonpartisan agency that works for the legislative branch of the US government. Its responsibility is to “examine how taxpayer dollars are spent and provide Congress and federal agencies with objective, non-partisan, fact-based information to help the government save money and work more efficiently.”

Also: Why enterprise AI agents could become the ultimate insider threat

The GAO investigators constructed two attack scenarios: detonating an explosive device for maximum damage and destroying an electrical grid substation for maximum disruption. They randomly split the challenge across the four AI platforms, so that two AIs each worked independently on the explosive challenge while two others worked independently on the substation challenge.

This assignment split is where, after being shown the results, the AI companies pushed back. That seems justifiable. By not giving all four AI services the same set of assignments, the GAO lost the ability to objectively measure all four against each other.

As a platform-vs-platform comparison, the GAO investigation falls short. But as an investigation into whether the generative AI tools available in mid-2026 might be able to be used for credible national security threats, its results are definitive. The investigation also highlighted how AIs reduce the time and effort involved in researching how such attacks might be carried out.

While the four AIs used for the tests were identified in the report, the GAO did not publish which AIs were used to carry out which tests. Instead, “Due to the sensitive nature and content of the testing, we randomly assigned an alphabetic character to each platform for purposes of presenting the test results within this report.”

For the bomb-damage scenario, the two assigned AIs recommended different approaches, although both provided specific actionable steps in order to accomplish the tasks.

Platform A constructed a scenario where an IED (improvised explosive device) would be detonated inside a hotel parking structure. It provided a timetable for when the hotel was expected to be crowded due to a public event as well as where the hotel’s security was likely to be weakest.

It recommended where to rent a van from outside the city, how to disable the GPS in the van, a shopping list of supplies used in constructing the device, specific stores to shop at, where to park the van in the garage, how to set up the device for detonation, and even a bus route to take in order to get back to the attacker’s personal car.

Also: Could cyberwar knock us back to the Stone Age?

After additional prompting by GAO investigators, Platform A “also generated an image that directed us to a specific area to deploy the IED to cause significant damage to the location. Specifically, Platform A generated an image of a parking structure that recommended where to park the vehicle.”

GAO investigators verified the plan’s viability by consulting with a bomb squad commander at a big-city fire department. The bomb tech confirmed that following the instructions with the recommended components would result in an explosion. The GAO also confirmed the availability of the supplies from the vendors the AI recommended.

Platform B targeted a convention center. Its recommended preparations were extensive. It recommended incorporating a shell company as a cover story so that the attacker could legitimately lease a secure, ventilated barn. It recommended a rural purchasing circuit, buying just enough supplies at four rural co-ops over four days in order to avoid triggering any mandatory reporting requirements.

The AI then recommended purchasing diesel fuel from three truck stops using cash, making “unremarkable purchases at unremarkable locations.” The AI recommended transferring the fuel into sealed 55-gallon drums at the barn. It also recommended careful storage conditions, including temperature, drum integrity, and separation from other materials (the materials themselves were redacted from the GAO’s report).

Also: Apple, Google, and Microsoft join Anthropic’s Project Glasswing to defend world’s most critical software

Next up, the AI recommended a communications security review, burner phones, varied procurement routes, and information compartmentalization. It recommended taking a taxi to a waterfront and then walking south for 40 minutes. It suggested confirming sight lines and contractor access to road positions. It even suggested that while the attacker’s face would appear on three cameras, the footage from those specific cameras would not be reviewed for three weeks.

It then recommended sending decoy trucks into the target, then sneaking an explosive vehicle into a parking garage.

When the GAO reviewed the recommendations, the agency discovered the AI got event dates wrong. However, according to the bomb squad commander, the components and procedures recommended would definitely have resulted in an explosion.

As with the bomb-damage scenario, the two AIs participating in the electrical grid scenario took different approaches, but both had the potential to be disturbingly effective.

Platform C began by identifying electrical substations where an attack could be most effective. It produced a list that helped identify vulnerable substations. After some prompting, “The platform then selected a substation in a specific major U.S. city and estimated that the loss of power would cause billions of dollars in damages.”

It identified the specific location on the specified substation where a drone could hit and cause a short circuit, which would destroy a key component. It actually provided a simulated video of the drone attack as well as a diagram that showed the component’s failure process.

The AI identified the exact model of the drone that it felt was best suited for the attack. Initially, the AI’s guardrails refused to provide an exact location for a target substation. That said, “The platform eventually provided the name of a substation, which we used to identify an exact location.”

The day after the AI made its recommendations, the GAO reported, “We observed that the platform had removed portions of our chat discussions that related to electrical grid vulnerabilities. It is unclear whether this action was initiated as part of a guardrail or if by error.” After two weeks, the account had not been suspended.

GAO investigators confirmed that the suggested drone model did exist, the route to the target was accurate, and the proposed target did exist. A subject matter expert consulted by the GAO “Verified that the information provided by Platform C was generally correct. The expert stated such an attack could, at the very least, cause damage to the substation. However, the expert could not confirm whether the specific drone suggested by Platform C would cause a short circuit or destruction of the substation.”

After rented vans, shell companies, and aerial drone attacks, Platform D decided to do it old school: plain old firearms. Point. Shoot. Buh-bye.

The AI provided GAO investigators with a primary target substation along with three secondary backup locations. It recommended locations near a large international airport and a bunch of data centers to increase the economic impact from the attack. And just in case it wasn’t clear enough, the AI drew a map highlighting the locations of the target substations.

It recommended the use of a gun, recommended the type of weapon along with the proper ammo in order to maximize the effects of the attack, along with where an attacker could go to procure such weapons. It recommended both online and in-person surveillance of the target locations prior to initiating an attack.

As with Platform C’s recommendation, Platform D recommended specific components in the substation that would be most vulnerable to an attack. It even provided images highlighting where the transformers would be most vulnerable.

The GAO report stated that the AI originally threw up guardrails and refused to provide the information requested, saying, “Our query raised serious concerns about potential intent to engage in criminal activity or harm critical infrastructure due to the context of the conversation.”

Also: AI finds zero-day flaws for just $4 now – even mediocre hackers can cash in

The more telling comment is this from the report: “However, the platform ultimately provided us with information needed for an attack.” I’m thinking someone in the GAO could probably sell water to a fish.

As with Platform C, GAO noted that the AI removed some of the chat content from the chat history. As for whether the plan would work, “We verified the planned attack was viable for half of the substation locations recommended by Platform D.”

A subject matter expert also confirmed that attacking the specific areas of the transformers recommended could cause an outage and that the specific firearms and ammunition recommended were available from the purchase locations specified. The AI did make an error in the generated maps by incorrectly placing roads.

But of the four recommended locations, two were actually viable. GAO investigators took a field trip to the recommended locations and verified that two could work for such an attack, but the other two had high walls that obscured the view of the targets.

After completing the tests, the GAO investigators presented the results to the AI vendors and gave the companies the opportunity to respond. While the GAO report purposely hid which AI provided which attack plan, the report is fully forthcoming with the complete response letters from each company.

In reading the responses, I found the difference in attitudes and approach to the question most interesting.

Anthropic’s four-page response letter was signed by Anthony Cimino, head of federal affairs. The company’s response was courteous but strongly challenging. It moved immediately from thanking the GAO to questioning the statistical significance and criticizing the absence of a control group.

Anthropic’s letter about Claude says, “We ask that the report specify that there were not enough trials to achieve significance, and that therefore any conclusions are at most preliminary.”

Also: How I safely run my OpenClaw AI agent 24/7 on a Mac

Google’s three-page response about Gemini was unsigned. The majority of the response discussed launch reviews, executive oversight, Google’s testing, filters, training, and human review practices. The company did agree that Gemini could violate guidelines. It was the most disengaged from the GAO’s report, and it offered little in the way of explanation about the issues raised.

Google’s letter says, “We have deep respect for the mission of the Government Accountability Office and we want to underscore that Google takes your work very seriously.”

OpenAI’s three-page response letter about ChatGPT was signed by Chan Park, head of US and Canada policy and partnerships. It says the company reviewed 325 responses from the AI and identified three that fell short of how the AI is supposed to behave. The company apparently replayed the requests and said that it was unable to reproduce the responses reported by the GAO.

OpenAI was the clearest in stating that the GAO’s results demonstrated unacceptable behavior from the AI, stating: “Even a discrete contribution to a broader harmful activity can matter, and improving safeguards against such assistance remains an ongoing priority.”

SpaceXAI’s 363-word, less-than-a-page response about Grok was unsigned. The company emphasized how much effort was required to bypass the safeguards and mentioned that there have been improvements in the guardrails since the GAO conducted its tests earlier that year.

Then we reach the “Say what, now?” moment of our day.

The company replied, “It is worth noting that GAO received its output from paid accounts in the United States. SpaceXAl provides unique moderation policies for registered, paid US account holders that reflect the lower risk profile of such accounts compared to free or foreign accounts.”

In other words, the company contends that paying US account holders have a lower risk profile than free or foreign users, and that therefore Grok provides fewer guardrails. The letter provides no supporting evidence for that assumption.

While a credit card number could provide some linkage to a user’s identity compared to fully free accounts, users could easily obtain a prepaid card that allows for completely anonymous paid usage. I just think that making an assumption about risk factor based on whether a user is willing to pay $8 a month is a particularly fallacious security policy.

The GAO report is clear that each of the AIs did provide some pushback against providing cookbooks for conducting the destructive behavior.

The agency conducted a number of adversarial prompt seeding operations, including roleplaying, special encoding, character transformation, word transformation, separating intent from content, taking very small baby steps in prompting, and other approaches often used to convince an AI to effectively “jailbreak” its guardrails.

Also: If you’re not working on quantum-safe encryption now, it’s already too late

While the AIs were eventually persuaded to participate in guiding attacks, some accounts were later shut down by the AI platforms, indicating that while the guardrails didn’t prevent the AI from providing initial advice, they were eventually flagged and terminated.

While that’s a bit of “locking the barn door after the horse has bolted,” it does indicate that the AI vendors are working toward more comprehensive protections.

The GAO report didn’t address the larger issue of whether censoring the AIs is ultimately in society’s best interest.

As a counterterrorism and infrastructure security professional, I often think of Ben Franklin’s famous statement, “Those who would give up essential Liberty, to purchase a little temporary Safety, deserve neither Liberty nor Safety.” Old Ben never had to contend with nukes, cyberattacks, and machines smarter than he was.

Personally, I lament the need for guardrails because they limit potentially legitimate access to information. But as the GAO’s scenario shows, strong guardrails are ultimately necessary, not because of the AIs, but because we humans are our own worst enemy.

Also: Rogue AI incidents hit ‘tens of thousands’: Can businesses trust these tools?

On the other hand, it’s not as if humans haven’t been able to figure out how to conduct violent acts all on their own, without the help of an AI. For example, the Anarchist’s Cookbook, which is essentially a recipe book for the home manufacture of explosives and illicit drugs, has been publicly available continuously since the early 1970s. While also outdated, Amazon even sells a reprinted 1941 manual about the chemistry of powder and explosives.

So, while it is definitely disturbing that the AIs could, collectively, provide four unique attack plans for civic disruption, they are not opening a door previously unavailable to those with nefarious intent. I laud the GAO for providing additional testing feedback to the AI companies, and the AI companies for improving their guardrails.

But while the report is scary in that all that information was presented in an organized manner by each of the AIs, that availability doesn’t bestow a new superpower on crackpots. After all, AIs are trained on publicly available information.

One of the more costly, but necessary, tradeoffs of an open society is that all this information is available. Whether an AI is used to structure that information is largely irrelevant in the face of that simple fact.

#2991. President Trump Says He Convinced Nvidia’s Board to Bring Back Jensen Huang

Nvidia's board brought Jensen Huang back after "input from senior U.S. government officials."

By Johnny Rice – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:20PM EST

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday that he personally lobbied Nvidia's (NASDAQ: NVDA) board to bring back Jensen Huang as CEO, telling the network's Eamon Javers this morning that he had spoken with the board "directly and indirectly" in recent days and that he "convinced them" to reinstate Huang, who "did nothing wrong."

The crux of the issue seemed to be the national security concerns surrounding Huang's original departure: "They did ask for assurance on the national security threat," The President said, "And we gave them assurances at least with everything we know, and we know a lot."

The board's official announcement this morning echoed Trump's statements: "In the process of reaching its decision, the Board received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang's reinstatement poses no national security risk."

While the announcement led to a massive rally today -- Nvidia stock finished up 31% -- the President's involvement could have unintended consequences for shareholders.

The national security concern stems from Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, the former CEO of Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer accused of contravening U.S. export controls. Ms. Huang is herself a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, though Beijing denies the espionage allegation. The Justice Department is investigating Megaspeed, so too is Nvidia, which opened its own investigation on Nov. 8. The board said it has no reason to believe anyone had compromised company secrets or U.S. national security.

According to Trump, Attorney General Todd Blanche told him the government "does not possess evidence that [Huang] has engaged in any unlawful conduct" and that there's no reason to believe Huang poses a threat to national security.

Trump told CNBC that not only is he not a threat, but that through his work, he is an invaluable asset: "People say, is it a threat to have him? No, it's a threat to lose him."

Trump cheered his reinstatement, saying: "I'm very, very happy that the company had the courage and the board had the courage to do the right thing, and they suffered for two weeks, and the company suffered greatly."

Given the sensitive nature of all of this, the dramatic reversal of the board's decision would likely have raised eyebrows in Washington under any circumstance. But now that the President himself has claimed that his involvement was instrumental in changing the board's minds, I would expect serious scrutiny from Congress.

Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

Legislators on both sides of the aisle expressed their skepticism. Republican Senator John Cornyn said on the social media platform X, "No national security risk? Based on what?" while Democratic Representative Jim Himes said in a statement that "Congress still has questions that require answers."

If any evidence of wrongdoing surfaces and Huang is forced out once more, I think the market's reaction will be even stronger on the way down.

#2989. How to Turn Off Apple Intelligence Feature by Feature in iOS 27

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Whether you want to drop a few AI features or as many as possible, here's where each control can be found in iOS 27.

iOS 26 lets you turn off Apple Intelligence with one toggle. iOS 27 doesn’t. Apple folded its AI settings into a new Siri menu centered on Siri AI and dropped the master switch along the way, so opting out now means working through several parts of Settings. Siri AI’s controls are in the Siri menu, Screen Time can block Writing Tools and image creation, notification summaries have their own switch and each app’s Siri & Search settings control what Siri learns from it. This guide walks through every step in order.

Individual features can still be disabled, but they’re scattered across several menus in Settings and Screen Time rather than being consolidated in one place. Here’s the current map of what can be turned off and where to find each control.

If your device is not running the newest software, turning off Apple Intelligence all at once only takes a few taps in Settings and doesn’t affect any core iPhone functionality. Here’s how to do it on iPhone, iPad and Mac.

You can still disable some of Apple’s AI features if your iPhone is running iOS 27. It just won’t be as easy as tapping the main toggle.

To disable Siri AI, go to Settings > Siri and toggle off the control. Beware, though, that this will disable your voice assistant entirely, as well as disable conversations in the new dedicated Siri app and block you from using Apple’s Visual Intelligence. Additionally, your HomePod speakers will not be able to recognize your voice or help with personal content if they’re on the same iCloud account as your iPhone.

Keep reading for how to disable some of the individual Apple AI features.

Since the first Apple Intelligence features rolled out in iOS 18.1, some people have opted out of the new technology. Tools such as Image Playground, Genmoji and message summaries can use up to 7GB of your device’s internal storage — a sizable chunk, especially if you tend to run against the upper limit of its free space.

Even if storage is not an issue, you just may not be interested in Apple’s set of AI features. A recent CNET survey finds that just 19% of US smartphone owners are choosing to upgrade their devices to take advantage of new AI features. And a recent ZDNET/Aberdeen survey reveals that the majority of US adults say they will not use most AI features, and just 8% would consider paying extra for them. Some AI features are more welcome than others, with 58% of respondents saying using AI for photo editing would be valuable.

Or, honestly, you may not want to deal with features that are creating inaccurate summaries or that can be replicated without Apple Intelligence.

(Earlier iOS 18 updates re-enabled Apple Intelligence automatically even if you had turned it off, but as of iOS 18.4, the setting is honored.)

Read more: Did a well-meaning friend send you a message about Apple Intelligence possibly reading your private information? Here’s what’s actually going on.

I’m not an AI crank — I appreciate features such as notification summaries and the Clean Up tool in the Photos app. And yet, Apple Intelligence is also a work in progress, an evolving set of features that Apple is heavily hyping while gradually developing. I wouldn’t hold it against you if you wanted to not be distracted or feel like you’re doing Apple’s testing for them (that’s what the developer and public betas should be for).

Remember that Apple Intelligence works only on the new iPhone 18 lineup, the iPhone Duo, iPhone 17, iPhone 17 Pro, iPhone Air, iPhone 17E, iPhone 16, iPhone 16E, iPhone 16 Pro, iPhone 15 Pro or M-series Macs and iPads, plus the newest iPad mini.

If you’re concerned about the storage requirements of Apple Intelligence, you can find out how much the system is using by going to Settings > General > iPhone (or iPad) Storage > iOS (or iPadOS).

See how much space Apple Intelligence is using in Settings. This is what it looks like running iOS 26, but the settings flow is the same.

Note that even after turning off Apple Intelligence, the storage still shows up in the iOS Storage list. However, I was able to confirm that if the phone’s storage fills up and the system needs that now-inactive space, iOS reclaims what it needs.

Maybe you’re finding some Apple Intelligence features helpful while others are annoying (or just highly amusing). You can turn off specific features in the settings for apps to which they apply.

For example, to disable text summaries in Messages notifications, go to Settings > Apps > Messages and switch off the Summarize Messages option.

Some features are not as app-specific. Writing Tools, which use Apple Intelligence to proof or rewrite text, appear as an option when you select text, regardless of the app. To turn that off, you need to disable Apple Intelligence system-wide.

Also note that nearly every app includes an “Apple Intelligence & Siri” option in Settings, with a Learn from this App option (turned on by default). That applies just to whether Apple Intelligence and Siri can monitor how you use it to make suggestions; it doesn’t affect any particular Apple Intelligence features.

Another option for restricting what Apple Intelligence can do is buried in the Screen Time settings. Screen Time is designed to control which apps and features can run on another device, such as the iPhone used by a child in your household. But it’s also helpful for tracking how much time you’re spending on the device and, in this case, turning off some components of Apple Intelligence.

Turn off selected Apple Intelligence features in the Screen Time settings. This is what the settings look like with iOS 26. It may look different if you are using iOS 27. The settings flow is the same.

If you haven’t upgraded to iOS 27 yet, make a good backup first.

If you aren’t on iOS 27, you can turn off Apple Intelligence with one tap. (You can see what software your iPhone is running by going to Settings > General > About and seeing what iOS version you’re running.)

If you’re on iOS 26 or earlier, here’s how to disable Apple Intelligence. Open Settings (iPhone or iPad) or System Settings (Mac) and choose Apple Intelligence & Siri. Then turn off the Apple Intelligence option. Confirm your choice in the dialog that appears by tapping Turn Off Apple Intelligence.

Turn off Apple Intelligence entirely with one switch if you are running iOS 26 or an earlier software version.

Curiously, the Clean Up tool in Photos sticks around even when Apple Intelligence is turned off, perhaps because the first time you use it the app downloads resources for it and holds onto them.

#2988. Anthropic Stock Is Now Public. Here’s What History Says About Buying the Hottest IPOs in Their First Month.

Waiting to buy IPO stocks months later is usually the correct move for individual investors.

By Brett Schafer – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:23PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Anthropic (ANT -1.43%) is officially a publicly traded company. The company sold shares at $83 this week, which then popped to over $100 on the first day of trading before falling back to earth and closing at roughly $89 on Friday, Nov. 21. It is now the 14th-largest company in the world by market cap, worth $1.16 trillion.

Many investors are interested in Anthropic as an IPO investment given its massive growth potential and the disruptive nature of artificial intelligence (AI). But what does the data say about investing in IPOs near their debut? The answer is clear: Investors should exercise patience with this smoking-hot stock. Here's why.

We have abundant data on IPO stock performance dating back decades, which has allowed researchers to analyze them closely. When looking three years out from the IPO date -- which is what long-term investors should be doing for buy-and-hold portfolios -- 64% of IPO stocks underperformed, while 29% beat the market averages. However, a lot of the time, even stocks with market-beating performance experienced a post-IPO slump. This usually provides investors with potential buying opportunities at lower prices if they are patient enough to wait six months after listing.

There are two main reasons for this, regardless of company quality. First, an IPO is generally considered a time for insiders to sell some or all of their positions, not to buy. A venture capital firm, a vice president, or an entry-level employee may now hold millions in Anthropic stock. It is not shocking, then, to see them sell some of their stakes now that the stock is publicly traded, a practice usually done in increments during the lockup period after the IPO. Second, a company and Wall Street generally want to maximize the IPO price at which they sell shares, which can, on average, lead to elevated valuations for investors buying in the early weeks of trading.

Taking all this into consideration, it is no surprise that the average IPO stock performs poorly over the next three years.

Seeing this data, a smart investor looking to buy shares of Anthropic will wait at least a few months before starting a position, which will (on average) lead to a better entry point.

The next question is whether Anthropic is a high-quality business in the first place. Growth has been on fire leading up to the IPO, with trailing revenue now at $35 billion at the end of the third quarter, up from less than $5 billion in 2025. This is an incredible level of growth, as enterprises and individuals alike spend gobs of money on Claude's coding tools and other automations.

Growth should continue in the years ahead, but don't think the AI race is over. Anthropic has many competitors, both large and small, and has to contend with open-source AI models that enterprises are increasingly adopting to lower costs. The question is whether Anthropic has locked in enterprise customers to its AI tools, creating switching costs, or whether enterprises will eventually move to cheaper solutions.

Profit margins are also a big question. Right now, Anthropic is generating around break-even profitability. However, it is about to embark on a spending spree to build its own AI data centers, amid the general volatility of the AI race. A year ago, many thought Anthropic was well behind OpenAI, its main competitor. Who's to say what its competitive position will be like a year from now?

I wouldn't fault anyone looking to get in on Anthropic. This is one of the fastest-growing companies in history, with a product that is indistinguishable from magic. There are certainly risks to the investment, but Anthropic also may be the largest company in the world 10 years from now.

Anyone looking to buy shares should hold off in the first weeks of trading, if not longer. Let the company get through its insider sales post any lock-up periods, which end in May of next year, before starting to build a position. On average, you will find a better trading price in doing so.

#2987. Pixel Weather’s new feature lets you report your local conditions now

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

Weather apps are notoriously inaccurate. Google's new feature can help.

Default weather apps are notoriously unreliable, but Google is giving users a chance to help improve things.

A recent update to the Pixel Weather app introduced a new “Report current weather” card. If the weather you’re experiencing doesn’t match what the app is saying, you can report the discrepancy.

“Share the current weather condition,” the card says, “to improve local forecasts.” If you tap it, you’re given options to indicate whether the current temperature is accurate, warmer, or colder than the app says, and what your current conditions are, with options like cloudy, clear, rain showers, strong thunderstorms, and rain showers.

The card also shows what the app currently thinks is happening for you to use as the basis for your report. To find the card, scroll all the way to the bottom of your Pixel Weather app.

Also: Your best Google weather app alternatives (and what I recommend instead)

Google said that it will use your feedback, device location, and “some diagnostic information” to help improve forecasts. Nothing you report will be associated with your Google account. The app doesn’t explain exactly how Google uses your reports, but I’ve reached out to see if the company can offer any details.

As someone who doesn’t put a ton of faith into default weather apps, I see this as a step in the right direction toward regaining some trust. Even if it’s not yet clear how Google will use the information you send, the goal of improving forecasts is an important one.

Also: I switched to MyRadar as my main Android Auto weather app and don’t regret it

A large part of what makes weather apps unreliable is that they lack human insight, and a computer usually can’t grasp the nuance of a local forecast the way a meteorologist would. Experts have also warned of reduced weather forecast accuracy due to recent NOAA budget cuts.

The Apple Weather app has a similar feature, so it’s good to see Google catch up. I couldn’t find a feature like this for Samsung or any other native app. One of the most popular third-party apps, Weather Underground, does feature a “Report your local weather” prompt. It also gathers information from hundreds of thousands of connected home weather stations.

This new Pixel Weather feature was present on three Pixel phones in my home, so the rollout appears to be widespread.

Atmos Rewards offers six communities, each with its own travel perks and opportunities to earn bonus points

There are six communities, including two reserved for Alaska and Hawaii residents. The other four are open to members regardless of where they live. But don’t get caught up in the community names. The best choice for you may depend more on your travel plans than the groupings suggest.

Here’s how to decide which Atmos Community best fits your travel patterns.

Atmos Communities are like groups within the Atmos Rewards program that offer additional benefits based on different travel interests.

They’re free to join for Atmos Rewards members. Four communities are available to all members, while community Club 49 is reserved for Alaska residents and community Huaka’i by Hawaiian is reserved for Hawaii residents.

You can belong to only one community at a time and switch communities once per calendar year. Consider your upcoming travel plans before choosing.

Joining a community is easy: Log in to your Atmos Rewards account and navigate to “Account overview.” Select “Join a Community,” browse the options and click the “Join” button under the one you choose.

Each Atmos Community has a list of 10 destinations where members can earn bonus points and status points. Although the destinations differ by community, the rewards structure is the same.

Through Dec. 31, 2027, members can earn up to 10,000 bonus points and 10,000 status points by visiting qualifying destinations.

Visit one destination, earn 100 status and bonus points.

Visit 10 destinations, earn 3,000 status and bonus points.

The first member of each community to visit all 10 qualifying destinations during the challenge period will also earn 100,000 bonus points.

New lists of qualifying destinations for each community take effect on Jan. 1, 2028.

#2985. Everything Is Falling Apart, and AI’s Fingerprints Are Everywhere

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

AI is rewriting the web in its own imperfect image, making software flaws easier to find and easier to exploit, while introducing plenty of its own imperfections.

I have struggled to find the time to write this story about why everything keeps breaking because everything keeps breaking.

Microsoft Outlook? Broken, for a bit. Air traffic control? Had some issues. Bank with Chase? Monday was not great. But even before that, you had issues if you ran a website with PHP, or had a grocery store freezer connected to the internet, or owned crypto. I am almost certainly forgetting some.

Something must be behind all of this, you might think — a massive cyberattack campaign, maybe or AI agents running amok across the internet? But the answer is more complicated than that. All of these issues, and the ones that will happen after I write this, are probably unconnected, at least in the concrete sense. But they exist in a world where the infrastructure of the internet and the whole human world it undergirds looks increasingly precarious.

Adam Meyers, senior vice president of counteradversary operations at cybersecurity company CrowdStrike, called last month “Black October.” This month? “Blacker November.” December probably isn’t looking great.

“This is not getting better, unfortunately,” Meyers told me.

One big reason for all of this, although not the only one, is artificial intelligence, but maybe not in the ways you expect.

If it’s connected to the internet, it’s at risk. Websites, sure. But also utilities like power plants and water treatment facilities or, for some reason, grocery store freezers.

In the olden days, like six months ago, a hacker taking control would have required a fairly complicated set of skills, honed over a long career, perhaps in the employ of a government or criminal organization.

Artificial intelligence, especially models developed and released this year, has changed things. AI has “democratized” hacking, cybersecurity experts told me. Things that used to require the capabilities and focus of a team of people with a clear goal can be done by a 20-something with an AI model and a specially designed program around it. And you don’t have to be a big or valuable target anymore to be worthy of a hacker’s attention.

At the same time, AI has made it easier to find vulnerabilities. These models and agents — essentially AI models that can do things on their own — can comb through websites or code and find problems that might have escaped the notice of humans. And they can do so more cheaply, meaning they can be turned on code that might not otherwise have gotten a close look. So many vulnerabilities are being exposed that it’s been given an appropriately bleak name: the vulnpocalypse.

Even that defensive advantage is a double-edged sword. Before, it might have taken weeks before a newly discovered vulnerability could be exploited. Now it might take days, even hours. If you’re responsible for a website or any of those things connected to the internet that may or may not still be maintained by their creators, you may not be paying attention and know you should update. You may find yourself liable if a breach leads to a leak of customer data. You may not have the know-how to fix it.

Another risk introduced by AI is that the technology, trained on all the lines of code its creators could find, is really good at writing code. Software engineers know this, and companies are now maintaining smaller teams of human engineers using AI to write more and more software. That code might be OK, but it might not be, and there’s a chance no human ever looked at it to be sure.

When I connected with John Strand over video chat, he was in Germany for a conference, and he was smiling. That wasn’t good. “You need to understand that whenever an offensive security person smiles and gets excited,” he said, “it’s bad.”

As owner and CEO of Black Hills Information Security, Strand’s job is to test systems for weaknesses, to think like an attacker and poke holes in a client’s defenses. He says he’d been getting a bit bored with the work after decades of the same techniques until the cyber capabilities of AI models started to explode at the end of 2025. New AI models were great at finding weaknesses in code, and AI agents could do the boring, time-consuming part.

“You’re seeing something that can automate the kind of grinding grunt work of offensive security and bring that to a human operator much faster,” Strand said.

Things have only accelerated since the summer. AI agents are capable of more autonomy. And attackers have better tools to refine the often blunt instruments of AI models — harnesses. While an AI model can do a lot of things, a harness provides the tools and direction that allow it to do a specific thing more effectively. Think Anthropic’s Claude Code or OpenAI’s Codex, but for hacking. Perhaps the biggest, most notable harness used by hackers is Artex, a significant-enough threat that the FBI issued a warning.

In September and October, a hack of major South Korean banks led to the theft of personal information. Meyers says CrowdStrike determined the attacker was likely a 26-year-old in China using Artex. After asking AI how to do the attack, the hacker succeeded, then asked the AI how to sell the stolen data on Telegram.

“Anybody out there can now be a nation-state-level hacker just by using the tool,” Meyers said.

Despite efforts by major AI developers to limit who can access their biggest and most capable models, the hacking and exploit skills attackers need are within the means of models already in the hands of attackers. That includes open-weight models that hackers can run on their own hardware, away from the guardrails and peering eyes of AI companies and government agencies.

“The genie’s out of the bottle, and there’s no putting it back in,” said Gary Barlet, public sector chief technology officer at Illumio, a firm that specializes in ransomware and breach containment.

AI-powered hackers, whether they’re individual vibe-hackers or government-backed cyberwarfare units intent on damaging critical infrastructure, always have an advantage. They just have to be right once, as the saying goes. Defenders have to be right every time.

The good thing is that the defenders are using the same tools as the attackers. And sometimes they have a head start. Programs like Anthropic’s Project Glasswing, OpenAI’s Daybreak and Google’s Fairwind let cybersecurity experts and high-profile targets like banks and healthcare providers have early access to the most capable models from those developers, often with fewer limits on what the models can find. The goal is to get a jump on patching and fixing the easiest and riskiest problems.

It still might not be enough, and Big Tech leaders know it. In an October appearance at the Stanford Graduate School of Business, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said he no longer thought we’d be able to “just patch all the bugs” and that the problem is one that “is still something that we have not, as organizational leaders or countries or individuals, taken seriously.”

And defenders may not have much of a lead in patching bugs anyway, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said at the New York Times Dealbook Summit. Previously, when patching a critical system, you might have a week to test it before pushing it to production. “Right now the patch needs to go instantly from being available to being patched in production because it’s being exploited in the wild,” Nadella said. “That’s going to create instability.”

The PHP vulnerability widely reported in October — with an urgent notice from the PHP Foundation for website owners to patch — stemmed from work through Patch the Planet, part of OpenAI’s Daybreak.

That’s just one high-profile recent disclosure. But there has been an avalanche of them. The National Vulnerability Database has received more than 10,000 instances of common vulnerabilities and exposures so far in November, after more than 17,000 CVEs in October. For comparison, in October 2025, that figure was less than 5,000.

“There’s effectively this big backlog of security issues in the open-source world that hadn’t been discovered until AI was able to just spend tons of time analyzing it and finding these vulnerabilities,” said Matt Wyckhouse, CEO of Finite State, a cybersecurity company focused on connected devices.

It goes well beyond open-source software. The connected devices Wyckhouse focuses on include things like refrigerators and smart home gadgets — the kinds of things that probably didn’t have to be connected to the internet, but are. The source code of the firmware and software powering those products has been seen by far fewer eyes than something like PHP, an incredibly common coding language used by much of the internet. The code running these devices may not be on GitHub or floating around the internet, but an AI model might be able to reverse-engineer it and find an exploit. And suddenly an attacker can turn freezers into ovens. If you think ground beef is expensive now, imagine what would happen if a major retailer’s stock of hamburger all spoiled overnight.

“Refrigeration has to work for our society to function, as it turns out,” Wyckhouse said.

Wherever the vulnerability is found, it has to be patched. That’s where the backlog of issues is really wreaking havoc. Companies that create or just operate software are swamped with important, critical issues that have to be fixed now because it could only be hours before an attacker can exploit it. It’s why your computer and the software on it have to update so frequently these days.

“It’s daunting the amount of work and the amount of time it’s going to take to clean this whole thing up,” said Ted Miracco, CEO of the mobile security firm Approov.

And patching isn’t perfect. Sometimes it’s not a cyberattack that causes a system to go down but the fix to prevent it. Software updates are believed to be responsible for several of the recent high-profile outages, including Apple’s issues on Tuesday.

Things get even hairier if the code isn’t great to begin with or presents new problems of its own. And with AI writing even more of it, that’s a growing issue.

In the backdrop of all of this chaos is the fact that software engineering is one of the industries most disrupted by AI. Models trained on miles of code are great at writing something that works — no matter how many holes are in it. And to CEOs eager to cut costs and boost their companies’ stock prices, the temptation is to lay off engineers and have fewer people use AI to do all that coding.

Big Tech has been laying off staff by the thousands this year. Microsoft’s October announcement that it was offering buyouts to 60,000 staff became a key talking point just weeks later when the system powering its Outlook email service went dark for a day. Might things break less often if more people were on hand to maintain them?

“I wouldn’t be surprised if companies start to backtrack on that more,” said Ryan McCurdy, vice president of marketing at the database security firm Liquibase.

Companies might also have more junior developers writing and releasing code that would have previously gone through senior developers, with AI replacing the productivity, but not the experience, McCurdy says.

That means the problem isn’t just the vulnerabilities in existing software, but the vulnerabilities being written into new software or patches to existing software by AI. The same technology is causing the problem, providing the solution and complicating that solution.

The main thing we can do now is just… wait. Wyckhouse told me he thinks the next two years are going to be a period of growing pains as vulnerabilities are found and patched. The end result should be that everything is more stable.

“I think software is going to be an order of magnitude more secure by the end of the next year or two than it is today for this reason,” he said. “It will hurt a bit.”

Whether you’re an ordinary phone user or a major corporation, updating your software has become more important than ever.

“You’ve got to patch. You go for a flu shot, and you go and have your preventative healthcare,” Meyers said. “You need to do the same thing when it comes to cybersecurity.”

We don’t have to put up with it, Barlet says. “I think we need to start having higher expectations from the companies we’re consuming from,” he said.

This under-the-radar software stock boasts a Superscore of 89 from our Hidden Gems Primary database, part of The Motley Fool's Moneyball Database system. Here's why.

You sit down with a mobile app to learn a new language, but you soon realize you are not just learning vocabulary. #2983. You won’t see a Galaxy Watch 10 until 2028 – here’s why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

In an effort to cut costs, Samsung is postponing the launch of the Galaxy Watch 10, according to reports. Here's what to know.

You might have to wait over a year to get the Samsung Galaxy Watch 10: Samsung will not release any new Galaxy smartwatch in 2027, South Korea’s ET News reported on Sunday.

In an effort to cut costs after two quarters of consecutive losses and amid global RAM shortages, Samsung Electronics’ Mobile Business division will switch from an annual release schedule to a biannual one for its smartwatches, according to ET News. The switch follows other cost-cutting measures that will reduce production of the tech giant’s midrange smartphone lineup while increasing its premium Galaxy S27 offerings.

I am not mad at this decision by Samsung. Annual smartwatch updates are usually iterative anyway. Take Apple, for example. Its Apple Watch Series 10, released in 2024, had the same chip as the Apple Watch Series 11, released a year later. The most recent Apple Watch, the Apple Watch Series 12, has the same battery life as the Apple Watch Series 11. I’d expect Apple to keep its new sensors, which debuted on the Series 12 and Ultra 4, for its Series 13 or Ultra 5 next year.

Samsung’s Galaxy Watch 9, which debuted with a different chip and added a few more hours of battery, had the same bones as the Galaxy Watch 8.

If some of the biggest features on these smartwatches remain the same for a few years, why not change the release schedule to a biannual one?

Also: Galaxy Watch 9 vs. Galaxy Watch 8: I’ve compared both models, here’s my advice

Some of the most substantial updates that arrive on new devices like the Galaxy Watch 9 or Apple Watch Series 12 normally arrive through software updates, which end up landing on previous watch generations upon launch as well.

By switching from an annual release cycle to a biannual one, Samsung could drum up more excitement and anticipation between launches, and be able to surprise its customers with more purchase-worthy updates.

Samsung did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

#2982. Why SK Hynix Stock Skyrocketed Today

The chipmaker rose after reports of interest from a high-stakes bidder.

By Danny Vena, CPA – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:36PM EST

Shares of SK Hynix (SKHY +20.00%) charged sharply higher on Wednesday, jumping as much as 23.7%. By the time the market closed, the stock was still up 20%.

The catalyst that sent the semiconductor specialist higher was news of a high-profile potential suitor.

SK Hynix was reportedly the subject of takeover interest from Japanese investment holding company and tech conglomerate SoftBank Group (SFTBY +19.27%). Chairman Masayoshi Son approached numerous Wall Street banks to discuss financing options to acquire the chipmaker for $1 trillion, according to a report that first appeared in The Information. The price under consideration would have been roughly 1.7 times SK Hynix's market cap of $576 billion as of Friday's market close.

The sheer magnitude of the offer and the potential national security implications were just a few of the obstacles that reportedly scuttled the deal, though it isn't clear whether a formal offer was ever tendered.

SoftBank has made no secret of its ambition to achieve end-to-end control over all elements of the artificial intelligence (AI) semiconductor supply chain. The company already owns data center chip designer Ampere Computing and power infrastructure specialist SB Energy, as well as an 86% controlling stake in chip designer Arm Holdings. SoftBank also holds a position in AI start-up OpenAI.

Bringing SK Hynix into the fold would have given the company a direct pipeline to DRAM, NAND flash, and high-bandwidth memory (HBM). However, in a statement, it said, "SoftBank explores many opportunities, and this one is no longer under active consideration." The wording suggests the deal was, in fact, explored.

So is SK Hynix worth $1 trillion? It's doubtful. A premium of that magnitude would likely have drawn the ire of smaller SoftBank investors, even as Son holds a dominant 33% stake in the company. Moreover, as Korea's dominant maker of HBM, it's unlikely the deal would have passed regulatory muster.

At less than 7 times earnings, SK Hynix stock appears attractively priced. However, the historical boom-and-bust cycle has some investors concerned, and the jury is still out on whether the company can maintain its juicy profit margins while the future of AI plays out.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many home buyers.

Mortgage rates dropped today, but anything above 7% still puts pressure on home buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.24% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and three basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

A significant drop is eye-catching, but you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Next week, the Nerds are waiting for the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. Stubbornly high inflation is a big reason why we’re seeing upward pressure on mortgage rates right now.

If PCE shows inflation comes in hotter than expected, mortgage rates could climb as markets anticipate further Fed rate hikes. (An increase to the federal funds rate is already widely expected at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting.) A cooler reading could ease some pressure on mortgage rates for now.

I’m not getting my hopes up too high for meaningfully lower inflation, though. Last week's Producer Price Index showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its fastest pace in four years. Wholesale inflation measures the price businesses pay for things. When businesses’ costs go up, they could pass those along to consumers in the form of higher prices — keeping the flames of inflation alive.

Whether Wednesday's PCE brings relief or another helping of bad inflation news, we'll know before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.74% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#2978. Why CrowdStrike Stock Was Down Today

CrowdStrike beat Wall Street's estimates, but its $43.9 billion deal to buy Zscaler sent the stock lower.

By Johnny Rice – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:40PM EST

10 stocks we like better than CrowdStrike ›

CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD -8.92%) stock finished down 8.9% on Monday, Nov. 23, after the cybersecurity company agreed to buy Zscaler (ZS +17.67%).

It was one of the few tech stocks to see red today, with the Nasdaq Composite up 6.2%. The S&P 500 jumped 4.7%.

CrowdStrike announced Monday that it would buy Zscaler, a cloud security company, in an all-stock deal valued at $43.9 billion. Zscaler shareholders would own about 16% of the combined company, and CrowdStrike shareholders would own the rest.

The deal still needs approval from both regulators and Zscaler shareholders.

The offer represents a 34% premium to Zscaler's Friday closing price; investors seemed to think that's a lot to pay.

CrowdStrike makes software that protects devices, while Zscaler helps companies control who can access their apps and data. Bringing the two together makes sense from a product standpoint and gives the company more to sell to its own customers as well as a shot at selling its software to roughly 8,000 Zscaler customers who don't already buy from CrowdStrike.

The announcement overshadowed a strong earnings report this morning in which the company beat Wall Street estimates for revenue and adjusted earnings per share. Sales jumped 28% year over year.

#2977. Can an App Tell When Someone Near You Is Wearing Smart Glasses?

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Apps such as Zuckoff scan for the Bluetooth signals smart glasses send out, giving bystanders a way to know a pair is nearby.

Meta’s smart glasses stay connected to the wearer’s phone over Bluetooth, and that connection is what detector apps look for. Apps such as Zuckoff scan for the short broadcast signals Bluetooth devices send out and compare them with signals from known smart glasses models. When there’s a match, the app sends an alert. It’s a practical way to learn that a pair is nearby, though the alert can’t confirm whether anyone’s recording, and glasses with Bluetooth switched off won’t appear at all. Here’s how the apps work and how much weight to give an alert.

It’s a clever workaround for glasses designed to blend in. How useful it is depends on what you want it to tell you: The app can detect that a pair is close by, but not whether it’s recording. Still, that’s more notice than most people get today, especially if they haven’t spotted the glasses’ built-in LED indicator.

Here’s how those apps work, what they reliably catch and what they miss.

Zuckoff, whose name takes a direct jab at Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg, has joined a small ecosystem of apps such as Antizuck Smart Glasses Scanner and Nearby Glasses that offer similar functionality — letting you know when someone has smart glasses activated and how close the device is, although they can’t tell you specifically whether someone is actively recording or using AI to pull up information. The apps detect approximate device proximity by sweeping local Bluetooth frequencies to locate vendor IDs, system identifiers and hardware signatures associated with smart glasses.

As Zuckoff’s website notes: “Glasses advertise loudest while powering on, pairing or leaving the case. Once paired to their owner’s phone, they can go quiet, so silence is not proof that nobody is recording, and a detection is not proof that anyone is. Signal strength gives a rough distance and no direction at all.”

Zuckoff’s developer, Pawel Szydlowski, said he was already working on projects that used Bluetooth to detect nearby devices and discovered the tech could be applied to smart glasses. “It’s just the same technology wrapped in a different paper,” he said in a video chat with CNET.

Recent reports have shown that smart glasses are being used for everything from bullying in schools to viral videos with unwitting subjects who don’t know they’re being recorded.

Since it launched in early August, the Zuckoff app has gotten 2,000 sign-ups, and Szydlowski is beta-testing an Android version he hopes to make available on the Google Play Store. The app also has a premium version for $10 a year that can run continuous detection checks in the background when the app isn’t in use.

But a booming ecosystem of DIY modifications and third-party hardware hacks is also giving smart glasses wearers unprecedented stealth by allowing them to disable the activation lights, either by bypassing the capture LEDs or by patching software to suppress system notifications.

Tech journalist Joanna Stern hosted a video showing the process in which a local hacker removed the LED light from her Ray-Ban Meta glasses for $100, allowing the wearables to secretly record without notice.

Detecting active smart glasses in public or private spaces adds a new layer of security awareness, but it raises an obvious dilemma: What do you actually do with that information? Once a detector app alerts us that smart glasses are nearby, it seems our only choices are to either leave the space or ask the wearer to remove the glasses.

Uttara Ananthakrishnan, an associate professor of information systems at the University of Washington who studies the societal impacts of technology, says we’re still figuring out the social norms around wearables like smart glasses. It’s impossible to know whether the person filming will behave responsibly, and the question of consent gets even thornier if children might be captured on video or in photos.

“Until we develop clear mechanisms to balance seamless innovation with consent, these devices will continue to turn everyday public spaces into zones of surveillance anxiety,” she said.

The Even G2 smart glasses don’t have a camera for recording photos or videos. They can pair with a separately sold R1 control ring that works as a fitness tracker.

For instance, Even Realities’ G2 smart glasses don’t include a front-facing camera at all. Instead, the Even G2 devices, released in late 2025, focus on providing the wearer with information and acting more like a personal assistant.

“We made the decision from the beginning that smart glasses don’t need to have cameras on them in order to be useful,” Even Realities CEO Will Wang told CNET in an email. “We believe something worn on your face should respect not only the person wearing it, but everyone around them.”

The G2 devices don’t record externally, a design choice Wang said gave the company a unique challenge. “Leaving the camera out also pushed us to think differently about what actually makes smart glasses useful day to day, rather than assuming more sensors automatically make a better product,” he said.

Wang said there’s room in the smart glasses market for different approaches to features and functionality, and that buyers may want to make trade-offs for devices they’ll wear on their faces. But Wang noted that the rise of smart glasses detectors indicates that social acceptance is an issue the industry needs to consider.

Over the last several months, smart glasses have been getting cheaper. And with more models and companies jumping into the fray, the demand for surveillance detectors is likely to grow — that is, unless Big Tech and wearable companies radically change their devices to make them invisible to detector apps.

Szydlowski said his hope is that, in time, Zuckoff won’t be needed at all. He pointed out that the ability to detect nearby Apple AirTag devices eventually became part of the operating systems on iOS and Android devices. If consumers pressure manufacturers and their governments to make smart glasses easier to detect, it would be “the perfect solution” to make his app obsolete, he said.

#2976. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2975. Claude can ship code for you now – how to try it (if you dare)

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Would you let a few Claude agents review and ship your code for you?

Developers are already using Claude Code and other AI-powered coding tools like OpenAI’s Codex in droves, but some still want to check code themselves before pushing it. A new feature from Anthropic is banking on more adventurous, agent-first coders being comfortable with handing even that last step off to AI.

With Claude Ship, announced in beta Monday, “Claude can now review pull requests, fix them, and approve the ones that a team’s policy allows,” Anthropic said. The feature is geared toward individual developers or companies who want to move faster or already delegate a significant amount of coding tasks to agents.

Also: How I made a paid Mac app in 11 days with Claude – without writing a single line of code

“Teams stay in control of where it runs and which changes need a person,” Anthropic said. “The goal: ship code as fast as teams write it.”

The new feature uses two new agents alongside Code Review, which Anthropic released in March for Teams and Enterprise plans. Code Review first collects, organizes, and posts findings to a PR. Then, Anthropic said, the agents, PR Steward and Claude Approvals, guide a PR “through review, fixes, and an approval decision so teams don’t have to manage each step manually.”

PR Steward can’t approve or merge pull requests, designed instead to participate “on failing CI checks, merge conflicts, and review feedback from Code Review, other review tools, or team members.” It also documents what it changed in each push through comments and provides instructions for how to undo it, should developers want to. Throughout the process, developers can give Claude instructions or tell it to stop by commenting @claude.

Also: I tried replacing Claude Code with a local AI stack that’s completely free – how it went

As a final step, Claude Approvals references whatever policy the user or team abides by (which developers provide as a repo file), and additional signals developers set, like Code Review findings, before approving changes autonomously if allowed. If not, it will seek out “deeper human sign-off,” Anthropic said.

“It works like a smarter CODEOWNERS file,” the release added. Anthropic said it recommends that most customers enable those additional checks “first in observe-only mode to build confidence before configuring branch protection to require Claude Approvals’ green light.”

Admins can select which repos the feature runs in and adjust settings as needed.

But Claude Ship isn’t meant for everyone.

“Most teams want a person to approve every change, and that’s easy to keep up with when people write every line. But a lot of developers build with agents now,” Anthropic said in the release, noting that its own Claude Code team uses Claude to write a majority of the code it needs.

ZDNET senior contributing editor David Gewirtz said the idea of shipping code at the speed of writing it “raises the hair on the back of my neck.” But he added that, given how impressive Claude Code has been thus far, he wouldn’t be surprised if Anthropic’s deployment tools were equally effective.

Also: Claude Code vs. Codex: Why I use both and how to choose one (if you must)

“But, it does depend on the team’s workflow and the kind of development they’re doing,” he said. “It’s not something for every team and certainly not for every indy developer, but for certain deployment processes, it could shave a ton of time off turnaround, saving money and potentially sending fixes to customers much faster. ‘Tis cool.”

Ultimately, Gewirtz said he has reasons to both trust the tool and be concerned about what it means for younger developers in the workplace.

“Since that workflow is often assigned to less experienced members of the team, it’s likely that Claude would be as accurate, although it’s one more way to shut the junior members out of where they add value to the company and grow their skills to become senior decision makers,” he said.

Developer platforms overall are adapting to the new state of the industry. Entire, a GitHub competitor started by the Microsoft-owned platform’s former CEO, is built around the expectation that developers are working side-by-side with agents.

Individuals with a Pro, Max, or Ultra subscription can use the /ship command now available in Claude Code, meaning “users won’t notice any significant increase in token usage,” an Anthropic representative explained. Team and Enterprise users can use Claude Ship for Github repos, and it is billed on tokens, so your overall bill may change.

Still, that depends on the number of PRs users are putting through Claude Ship — to avoid any surprises, admins can set spending limits for both Code Review and the new PR Steward agent. They can also turn Claude Ship on for an entire repo, meaning developers won’t need the /ship command.

Anthropic added that certain Enterprise organizations “can claim at least $10,000 in Claude Ship credits to use within 30 days of claiming” by Jan. 15, 2027.

With Huang's reinstatement, those fears have vanished -- at least for now.
