#2973. Nothing’s AI Earbuds Will Serve as an All-in-One Conversation Recorder

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

While most AI-powered peripherals record only phone calls or in-person chats, the Ear Note is designed to capture every conversation.

Phone-maker Nothing Technology is breaking away from its typical retro-inspired designs with a new pair of AI-powered earbuds, as reported earlier by Bloomberg. The company confirmed via an X post that its Ear Note wireless earbuds will be available starting Dec. 8.

The Ear Note earbuds are designed primarily to maximize the recording functionality. While other AI earbuds have already hit the market, the Ear Note is the first to record both phone calls and in-person conversations.

Nothing shared that the Ear Note will work with multiple popular AI agents, but the full list of compatible software has not yet been released. The Ear Note works in tandem with an app that stores recordings from the earbuds and case, creating transcriptions and summaries similar to those created by the popular Otter AI app.

The Nothing brand has developed a cult following for combining lifestyle and fashion trends in its suite of smartphones, headphones, speakers and watches.

The full product lineup is built with modern hardware, but mimics warm, nearly retrofuturist design sensibilities. The blocky Ear Note charging case continues this trend while taking the company’s tech in a new direction.

In the lead-up to the Ear Note’s reveal, Nothing CEO Carl Pei described the earbuds in an X post as “a tool, but one built with the personality and care that have come to define a Nothing product.”

In a Bloomberg interview, Pei indicated that Nothing isn’t trying to create AI agents or software, but that the company has a “really interesting opportunity to be the hardware layer to AI.”

It’s currently unclear how much the Ear Note earbuds and case will cost when they’re released next month. According to Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman, the company hasn’t settled on a price just yet. The most expensive earbuds currently available on the Nothing store cost $149.

Whether you’re covering your eyes, plugging your ears or slapping a timepiece on your wrist, there’s now an ever-present option to record the world around you. Multiple companies have created their own versions of smart glasses, earbuds and watches with augmented reality capabilities.

Now that AI is the flavor du jour, these existing features are being enhanced by new ways to scan and interact with the surrounding environment. That means a lot of visual and auditory data is being recorded by wearable devices, and very rarely with the consent of all parties involved.

The most immediate risk factors are associated with video recording – public distrust of smart glasses is so widespread that they’ve colloquially been coined “pervert glasses,” as folks fear they fundamentally break an unspoken social contract by obscuring public recording from other people.

But audio recording, enabled by Apple’s latest smartwatches, the Amazon Bee, Plaud earbuds and the new Ear Note, is also raising concerns, not least because many states require all-party consent before you can record conversations.

The Ear Note’s ability to record every bit of audio, from phone calls to in-person conversations, means it will be handling more sensitive information than other AI-powered earbuds. The company will need to face new challenges to ensure security measures are in place to prevent private chats from leaking.

Pei called wearables with recording functionality a sensitive space, and said the design team was “extra careful to not take any risks.” The Ear Note earbuds and case are largely controlled by user commands, including tactile button presses that begin the recording process.

#2972. College Scholarship Breakdown: Types, Sponsors and Eligibility at a Glance

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships vary in what they pay for and who can apply. Understanding the differences can save you time during your application process.

Scholarships have become a standard part of applying for college. You can learn about scholarships in a variety of ways, but looking through an online database of available scholarships — the most common way — can be overwhelming.

Before you go searching for a scholarship, it's important to understand the types of scholarships available and how to identify the ones that give you the best chance of winning, which can save you time and potentially earn you more funding. Here’s what you need to know about scholarships to decide which ones are best for you.

Scholarships are often funded by three groups: private organizations, institutions like universities, and government agencies. None is better than the other when it comes to awarding funds.

Nonprofit organizations and clubs that are looking for students either majoring in or researching certain fields, or who have experience volunteering in a specific field.

Companies that want to support all students or only fund those majoring in a field that relates to the companies’ industries.

Financial institutions, such as banks or credit unions, that want to support their community or their members.

Individuals or families that are funding private scholarships, usually in memory of a loved one.

Universities and colleges that are offering funds to students who attend their campuses.

Research organizations that want to fund students who will research in their areas of interest and further their causes.

These scholarships generally are provided by local, state or federal government agencies for a variety of reasons. Despite being funded by a government agency, the scholarships do not require you to pay back the money — unlike federal student loans.

Some scholarships pay for anything related to college expenses, including tuition, room and board, books and supplies, and more. Other scholarships limit what you can pay for with the funds, such as only tuition or tuition along with books. It’s important to look at this restriction when applying for scholarships to ensure the funds will go toward what you need to cover.

Merit scholarships require you to show excellence or achievements in your schoolwork, through talents and abilities, and even leadership or service work. Because these types of awards can be highly competitive, merit scholarships might require more than just achievements, such as volunteer or leadership experience. This means merit-based scholarships can overlap with other types of scholarship categories. Here are some examples of merit-based scholarships:

Merit scholarships often require you to have earned a minimum grade point average (GPA). The minimum GPA depends on the scholarship but often ranges from 2.0 to 4.0, with more competitive scholarships usually requiring higher GPAs. Scholarships with lower GPA minimums are more likely to consider additional factors, such as financial need.

Some merit scholarships might require a minimum ACT or SAT score instead of a GPA — or in addition to a minimum GPA.

Merit scholarships might also require applicants to have participated in activities outside of their schoolwork. This is common for scholarships that require membership in a certain group or organization. For example, membership in agricultural clubs, such as 4-H or FFA, and scouting groups, such as the National Eagle Scout Association, appear in scholarship requirements.

Some scholarships emphasize volunteering or community service and only consider applicants who have that experience. Applications vary in how detailed their requirements can be. Some scholarships want only a general description of where and how you’ve volunteered, and others might require experience with a service project that shows more leadership experience or time volunteering with a certain type of organization.

Scholarships that are awarded to students who can’t afford college are called need-based scholarships.

Unmet financial need is the difference between what it will cost you to attend college and what you can afford to pay. Because calculating how much you can afford is a complicated process, many scholarships refer to your FAFSA to document your financial need.

Some scholarship requirements may be as straightforward as demonstrating that you have financial need, but some scholarships might have income limits. However, income restrictions are less common.

Athletic scholarships are for — you guessed it — athletes. They are usually offered by colleges and universities to recruit athletes onto their collegiate teams and can be competitive. Which sports have scholarships available and the scholarship amounts vary by college.

Even though the main criterion for an athletic scholarship is being good at your sport, you’ll likely still need to show that you can keep up with your schoolwork. Most athletic scholarships require students to earn a minimum GPA or test scores to remain eligible while they’re in college.

Some scholarships are designated for students majoring in a general field, such as science or education, but others can be for very specific areas of study. For example, a scholarship for students majoring in a natural resource field could include majors in biology, ecology, wildlife biology, conservation education, forestry, soils and hydrology. However, a stricter scholarship might state that students have to major in more specific topics, such as biochemistry or microbial ecology.

Scholarships are available for active and former members of the military and members of their families. In addition to federal funds available for veterans to attend college, private organizations and some universities have scholarships for veterans to offset the cost of a degree.

Which family members can apply for funds depends on the individual scholarship. For example, some scholarships extend to children or spouses of veterans, but some go as far in a family tree as great-grandchildren.

Veteran- and military-focused scholarships might require that applicants or their family members enlisted in a certain branch, served during a particular time or conflict, or meet a certain rank or status such as receiving disability. Scholarships might also require that the applicant be a wartime veteran or family member of a wartime veteran; not all veterans meet this status.

Scholarships are also awarded to people who identify with a certain group. Common examples of identity-based scholarships include those for people who have disabilities, belong to certain ethnic or religious groups, identify as LGBTQIA+ or are first-generation students. Some identity-based scholarships require applicants to meet several criteria, such as belonging to more than one identity group.

Outside of what would be considered standard DEI groups, there are scholarships for applicants who identify with less common groups, such as veganism, homelessness and model railroad builders.

The best scholarships for you depend on your situation. For example, if you are a skilled athlete with a good academic record, going for an athletic scholarship might give you the most funding. On the other hand, if you’re going to research a topic that isn’t common, looking for scholarships that support your research topic might be your best bet because competition could be lower.

Good tactics to figure out which scholarships are worth your time include:

Think of the skills, talents, experience and identities that are unique to you. For example, being a child of a veteran immediately qualifies you for scholarships with fewer applicants than scholarships open to everyone. Likewise, if you embrace a certain ethnicity, identify with a specific group or have experience with a type of work, you might qualify for scholarships that appreciate and recognize those attributes more than the average scholarship.

Apply if you think you're eligible. Don’t apply to just a few scholarships and hope you get them. Instead, apply for as many scholarships as you can and for which you’re eligible. This increases your chances of receiving funds. Even small scholarships can make a big impact when you add several of them together.

Understand how you can use the money. If you already have a scholarship that covers only books, applying for the same type of scholarship isn’t helpful if you don’t need the extra funds. Instead, look for scholarships that cover the costs you have remaining.

Not sure where to look? Our sister site Scholarships360.org provides students with vetted scholarship opportunities. That would be a good place to start.

#2971. Retirees: Is Now the Time to Ditch S&P 500 Funds?

The recent Nvidia scandal underscores just how vulnerable the broad index can be to tech stocks.

By David Jagielski, CPA – Nov 23, 2026 at 6:13PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vanguard S&P 500 ETF ›

The stock market has been volatile in recent weeks, with the scandal involving Nvidia and its CEO, Jensen Huang, resigning and then coming back within a few weeks, highlighting just how erratic tracking the S&P 500 can be. Last week, amid the Nvidia sell-off, the broad index fell to below 7,000 -- its lowest point since April. Now, with Nvidia's stock jumping on the news of Huang being reinstated as CEO, the index added more than 300 points in a single day.

The wild swings due to tech are something investors have to come to terms with if they track the index via funds such as the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +4.46%). While over the long run it can be a good way to invest in the markets, the exposure the index has to tech these days makes it a riskier-than-normal option.

For risk-averse investors and retirees, it begs the question: is investing in S&P 500 index funds still a good move right now?

Through the S&P 500, investors gain access to the largest, most valuable companies on U.S. markets. But these days, that means a lot of tech exposure, as it's been tech stocks that have been rising feverishly due to artificial intelligence (AI) in recent years.

Nvidia, Apple, Microsoft, Amazon, and Alphabet are the largest holdings in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. Just those five holdings alone account for close to 30% of the Vanguard S&P 500's portfolio. And that's just among the largest holdings. There are also many companies that indirectly have significant exposure to AI, including energy companies involved in data center build-outs.

Tracking the S&P 500 makes sense for the long haul. But during a period of five years or less, a lot can happen. If a crash occurs, it can take a long time for the market to recover, time that retirees can't spare.

For most long-term investors, tracking the S&P 500 still makes a lot of sense, even if there might be a potential crash or correction on the horizon.

Retirees, however, may be better off considering dividend-oriented funds to track instead, which don't have nearly as much exposure to tech. Some funds don't have any exposure at all. During shorter periods, those types of investments can offer much less volatility and be safer options to hang on to.

Google's rejected patent application was for a TV service that made videos based on your viewing habits.

According to the website Patentlyze, the application is for a system designed to compile your video or TV watch preferences using genres and titles you watch on services such as YouTube, plus context from your IP address and demographic data such as your age. 10 stocks we like better than Cava Group ›

#2967. Applied Digital Stock Surged 20% on a Hyperscaler Deal. What to Know About Its $41 Billion in Leases

The data center developer says it now has more than $41 billion in contracted revenue. Its filings show how much of that has actually started and how much it has borrowed to build.

By Jonathan Ponciano – Nov 23, 2026 at 6:37PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Applied Digital ›

Applied Digital (APLD +20.28%) shares jumped as much as 31% on Monday after the company said it signed a new 150-megawatt, 18-year lease with a "tier-one investment-grade hyperscaler." The company said the lease brings its contracted base-term revenue above $41 billion. The stock ultimately gave back some of that gain but still closed up 20% at $13.82.

The jump came one trading day after Applied Digital hit its lowest close of the past year, $11.49 on Friday. Even after Monday's gain, the stock sits 72% below its 52-week high of $49.65 on May 28, and it's down 44% this year.

Applied Digital builds data centers and rents them to companies that need power and space for artificial intelligence computing. A lease of this kind is a promise of rent once a building is finished and handed over.

The company's quarterly report filed Oct. 7, before Monday's lease, listed $35.7 billion in minimum contracted lease payments. Of that, $468 million is due through May 2027, the end of the company's fiscal year. Payments rise to $1.58 billion the following year and $2.36 billion the year after. Another $26.6 billion is due after May 2031.

The reason is largely construction. As of the 10-Q's filing, Applied Digital had 250 megawatts of AI data center capacity running at its Polaris Forge 1 campus in Ellendale, North Dakota, out of 1,410 megawatts under contract. Its other buildings are scheduled to come online between late 2026 and 2028. A 210-megawatt building leased in June to another investment-grade hyperscaler isn't due until the first half of 2028.

The company's annual report spells out what happens if building falls behind schedule: "Under certain circumstances, lessees may have the right to terminate applicable leases if there are significant delays in construction." It adds that Applied Digital "may not be able to release the applicable data center in a timely or profitable manner."

The company's debt terms already account for that possibility. Under the terms of the 7% notes due 2031, issued by an Applied Digital subsidiary, if that subsidiary receives certain termination fees from a tenant, it generally must use them to pay off the notes, with exceptions if a replacement tenant is found.

The construction boom is happening as the firm loads up with debt. Applied Digital's long-term debt rose to $6.26 billion at Aug. 31 from $4.96 billion three months earlier. Interest expense for the quarter was $77.4 million, up from $8 million a year before. Meanwhile, revenue grew to $341.9 million from $80.9 million, but the company posted an operating loss of $62.4 million. It ended the quarter with $2.95 billion in cash.

This combination is common for data center stocks in a building phase, but it also means the company has little room for delays, since late buildings both push back revenue and give tenants a possible way out.

The new lease adds another investment-grade tenant, and its 18-year term is longer than the roughly 15-year term on the June lease, but the risk lies in execution. Applied Digital has to finish several large buildings on schedule, keep financing them, and hand them over before tenants' patience runs out.

The tenants' own spending is a second question. In a commentary published Monday, LPL Financial Chief Equity Strategist Jeffrey Buchbinder noted that the six largest U.S. hyperscalers are expected to generate negative free cash flow in 2026 and 2027, based on Bloomberg estimates. He also wrote that political headwinds will likely slow data center construction, "though probably not until 2028," and that rising financing costs could hurt project feasibility. Several of Applied Digital's buildings are due that year.

For investors weighing AI stocks tied to the build-out, Applied Digital's next quarterly report should show whether its 2027 construction targets are still on track. Until most of that capacity is in operation, the $41 billion figure reflects the rent the company expects to collect if construction stays on schedule.

#2966. SpaceX Sued by 2 States Over Grok’s Sexualized Images of Women and Children

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

People used Grok to generate millions of "spicy mode" images.

Oregon and Virginia on Monday filed lawsuits against SpaceX, alleging that Elon Musk’s company violated consumer protection laws, months after people used Grok Imagine to generate millions of sexualized images of women and children.

Grok Imagine is an image- and video-generation tool developed by SpaceXSI, a division of SpaceX. Shortly after launching last summer, the tool added a “spicy mode,” which enabled people to generate images and videos showing partial nudity and adult-themed videos. Grok Imagine is integrated into Grok, which is Musk’s AI chatbot.

In the lawsuits, separate but jointly filed, Virginia and Oregon allege that the sexualized images — including child sexual abuse material — were generated without the knowledge and consent of the women and children shown in the images and videos.

Read more: After Rampant AI-Powered Abuse, Grok Doubles Down

Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones said in a press release that SpaceX misled people into thinking that their images and data would be safe.

“When xAI [now renamed SpaceXSI] allowed Grok’s creators to sideline consent to turn a profit, they prioritized growth and engagement over safety, and they deceived the public about it,” Jones said. “Today’s filings are the first step towards holding this company accountable.”

Donna Rice Hughes, CEO of child internet safety organization Enough Is Enough, praised Oregon and Virginia for taking action against SpaceX.

“It is abhorrent that women and children are preyed upon in this manner and even more repugnant that xAI’s Grok ‘spicy mode’ has not kept up with bad actors who abuse their terms of service,” Hughes told CNET, adding that Musk’s “team can find ways to prevent criminal misuse of Grok, and at the very least, disable Grok’s ‘spicy mode.'”

Grok Imagine launched on Aug. 5, 2025, and within months, it was used to generate millions of sexualized images, which were posted on the social media platform X, which is also owned by Musk’s SpaceX.

Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield alleged SpaceX “knowingly designed” Grok Imagine and promoted spicy mode’s lack of safeguards as a feature, not a bug.

“It’s no surprise that Grok and X became a haven for perpetrators using AI to create and share child sexual abuse material,” Rayfield said in a statement. “The lack of needed safety measures was considered a feature, not a bug, which they promoted as ‘spicy mode.’”

During a 9-day span starting in late December, Grok posted about 3 million sexualized images, according to an analysis from the Center for Countering Digital Hate.

In a statement posted Monday on X, SpaceX said that people using Grok to generate sexualized images and videos violated the platform’s terms of service and worked “aggressively to circumvent our safeguards.” The company said it tightened protections after the misuse and continues to test and adjust them as needed. SpaceX also said it notifies law enforcement authorities “when someone tries to break our safeguards and generate this content.”

Earlier this year, several minor Jane Does filed a proposed class action lawsuit against xAI after people used their photos to generate child sexual abuse material using Grok via spicy mode. That case is still ongoing.

In July, a victim’s family also filed suit after photographer Russell Bloodworth in Arkansas allegedly used spicy mode to generate explicit photos of children. xAI went on the offensive, suing Bloodworth and South Carolina man Terry Wayne Harwood, who is also accused of using Grok to generate child sexual abuse material. The company seeks damages for expenses incurred in fighting lawsuits and for reputational harm.

SpaceX and xAI have faced lawsuits for other things aside from illicit image generation. The companies were sued for allegedly sharing sensitive data and for allegedly causing users to be hospitalized for psychosis after harmful chat conversations on Grok.

Monday’s lawsuits against SpaceX are among nearly 400 cases brought against 129 AI companies in the US, according to the AI Lawsuit Tracker. Many Big Tech companies have been sued for allegedly harming the mental health of adults and children. In August, Meta agreed to pay $18 billion to settle a lawsuit brought by US states alleging the company intentionally designed Instagram and other platforms to be addictive to children.

Companies such as ByteDance — owner of TikTok — Snap, Google and others face more than 2,400 federal lawsuits alleging that their platforms harm the mental health of children.

#2965. Dreading Holiday Money Talks With Family? Here's What to Say

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

How to prepare before you gather with loved ones, what to say, and what to do next.

Holiday gatherings have a way of stirring up difficult and downright uncomfortable conversations — and not just about politics or cousin Derek’s love life.

Relatives and friends may also bring up their money troubles and financial plans. That might involve asking you to lend money or your credit.

It’s easy to feel obligated or pressured to help out. But it’s important to establish boundaries so you don’t end up making a decision you’ll regret.

If you suspect a money request is coming your way, think carefully about what you’re willing — and able — to offer. You might choose a specific dollar limit or to give no financial help at all.

“Only lend what you're truly OK losing,” says Alvin Carlos, a certified financial planner at District Capital Management in Washington, D.C. “Look at your budget and decide what you could hand over and never see again.”

Choose the language that best fits your situation. Adapt your responses as needed to guide the conversation in a direction that works for you.

“I understand that you need help. I just need to review my own finances before I can give you an answer. I’ll get back to you by [day].”

If the request comes up at a holiday or family gathering:

“I’d rather discuss this privately. Let’s find another time to talk.”

“Here's what I’m comfortable with, and here's what I have to protect to be financially safe, mentally safe, and to continue to be there for you in the ways that you need me.”Or, if you want to be more direct:

Sometimes, there’s an unspoken expectation to give money because of how much loved ones think you earn, says Ashley Agnew, a certified financial therapist in Boston.

Getting ahead of it before there's a tipping point can help with tricky dynamics, she says.

A recent Edward Jones report found that only about one-third of financial caregiving arrangements were planned and discussed in advance. The report also found that nearly half of financial caregivers (49%) make financial sacrifices, such as reducing or stopping saving, or tapping into their retirement account.

Factor your financial health, your values and emotional well-being into your decision:

Review your income, expenses and savings. If you provide financial help and don’t get the money back, would you struggle to pay bills or reach financial goals? Prioritize building an emergency fund, saving for retirement and paying off high-interest debt first, Carlos says.

Consider your relationship. Would you feel resentful if you aren’t repaid? How do you think your friend or relative would react if you decline to help? Could it damage your relationship, and if so, is it worth it?

Understand the credit risks. Co-signing or allowing someone else to use your credit can leave you responsible for payments and expose you to potential credit damage.

Know the tax implications. If you’re considering a substantial gift or loan, check with a tax professional about any reporting requirements and tax consequences.

Consult your partner. If you share finances, make sure to include your partner in your decision-making process, Agnew says. If you have a parent asking for financial assistance, for example, but your cultural view is different from your spouse's, that’s a big opportunity for a rift.

Aim to be clear and direct. When in doubt, keep your answer concise, Agnew says.

“Keeping it short and sweet will communicate that you've made a decision, not that you've requested a negotiation.”

Depending on where you left the conversation, you might have more steps to take.

Stick to your boundaries and promises. Did you offer to contribute money? Pay as agreed. Did you say no? Don’t let your friend's or family member’s persistence, or the opinions of other friends and relatives, change your mind.

If you offered to provide nonfinancial support, Carlos recommends pointing your loved one to local government and nonprofit resources. That might include emergency rent assistance, cheap or free financial advice, food banks and career services.

Document the details and set the plan in motion. If you agreed to loan money, send your relative a quick text recapping the terms, Carlos says. He also suggests setting up a simple repayment system together, such as a recurring Zelle or Venmo transfer.

“Fewer reminders from you mean less tension,” he says.

Get outside help, if necessary. Despite your best efforts, it can be difficult to handle the situation on your own. If you’re feeling lingering stress or your family can’t reach a resolution, you might want to bring in some help, Agnew says.

She recommends working with a financial therapist or advisor, or using a financial wellness app.

#2964. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2963. Nvidia's Stock Surges More Than 30% on Jensen Huang's Return. Will It Get Back to $200?

On Monday, the company announced that Huang is back as CEO, after resigning just a few weeks earlier.

By David Jagielski, CPA – Nov 23, 2026 at 6:40PM EST

What a turnaround. In just a single day, Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%) stock jumped more than 30%. The tech company announced that it was reinstating Jensen Huang as CEO, who had resigned earlier in the month after multiple extramarital affairs came to light. It also comes after the board said it wasn't considering bringing him back.

Investors emphatically gave the move their stamp of approval, with the stock skyrocketing on the day, back to around a $4 trillion valuation. It is, however, still down more than 30% from its peak. Given the excitement around Nvidia's stock right now, could it be heading back to $200?

With the huge rally on Monday, Nvidia's stock is back up to around the levels it was at a few weeks ago, when Huang initially resigned. However, even before the scandal came to light, the stock was already falling from its highs; a month ago, the tech stock was trading at around $210.

The company's board initially said it would not take Huang back as CEO. However, by Monday, it unanimously voted to reinstate him. A big part of the reason appears to be due to the government's input. U.S. President Donald Trump has spoken positively of Huang and believed it was in the company's best interests to have him back, and says he convinced the board to take him back, saying, "I made the deal."

While there's one less question mark around Nvidia's business right now, there are still others looming, including what will happen involving Congress. Huang is scheduled to meet with Congress on Dec. 1, facing questions about a relationship with a possible Chinese spy.

Trump, however, says he gave Nvidia's board assurances about national security: "We gave them assurances at least with everything we know, and we know a lot." On the surface, it would appear that issues relating to national security may not be significant enough to warrant any further concern from the company's board.

Given how volatile the stock has been and the excitement around it, I wouldn't be surprised to see Nvidia shares get back up to $200 in the near future. However, the broader market risk of rising interest rates still looms, which could impact the business and its growth prospects in the longer run.

For now, the market appears to be focused on the short term; however, this can expose the stock to significant volatility. Investors should tread carefully because while Nvidia's hot today, if market sentiment sours next month due to rising interest rates, things could change quickly.

#2962. Uber Riders Can Now Request to Have Their Trips Recorded

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

In four cities, Uber riders can request rides with drivers who use the Record My Ride feature.

Starting this week, Uber riders in select markets will be able to specify that they want their trip recorded on video. The feature, which is being introduced in Baltimore, Indianapolis, St. Louis, and Raleigh, North Carolina, adds a new Recorded Rides choice to the app alongside other familiar ride options like UberX and UberXL. Uber says that the new feature gives “greater confidence that a recording will be there if something doesn’t go as planned.”

After a series of rider safety investigations and sexual assault lawsuit settlements, Uber began looking for ways to boost passenger safety and confidence in the service. The ridesharing platform first tested in-app trip audio recording in late 2019, then piloted the Record My Ride feature in 2022, before finally rolling out the video recording function in earnest, first in San Francisco in 2023, then nationally across the US in 2024.

According to Uber, the knowledge that a trip is being recorded gives both riders and drivers peace of mind.

While drivers can opt out of using the feature in their settings, more than half of its active drivers in the US choose to record their trips in the Record My Ride feature.

Starting this week, Uber riders in those four cities will be able to request a recorded ride during the hailing process in the Uber app and, when possible, be automatically matched with a driver using the feature. Choosing a recorded ride doesn’t affect the fare.

Record My Ride has been available to Uber’s drivers, though not passengers, for a few years now. The feature uses the front-facing camera on the driver’s phone to capture trip audio and video.

“Uber can only review recordings uploaded as part of a safety report or insurance claim,” a representative told CNET.

Not selecting a Recorded Ride doesn’t guarantee that your trip won’t be recorded in some way. Many Uber drivers choose to install separate dash cams in their vehicles, often with multiple cameras recording into the cabin. Uber offers drivers the option to register their dash cam with the service, but this is separate from the pilot.

“Drivers can use a separate dash cam, provided they comply with local laws,” an Uber representative told CNET in an email, “but Recorded Rides specifically matches riders with drivers recording through Record My Ride.”

Uber says it’s exploring additional ways to use recordings to keep its riders and drivers safe. It will soon begin proactively activating the Record My Ride feature on drivers’ devices during trips that may benefit from additional oversight, such as late-night or trips involving teenagers. Uber is also looking into automatically turning on video recordings “when trips are not proceeding as expected,” such as when a trip goes significantly off the projected course or if a crash is detected.

Uber says it’s also looking into testing the automatic activation of audio recording on riders’ phones during nighttime trips. Passengers would have to consent to this, which would require the app to use their phone’s microphone.

#2961. The Midterms Put Data Center Permitting Back in Play. Here Are 2 Industrial Stocks That Win if Power Moves Behind the Meter.

Bloom Energy and Caterpillar have sold off sharply along with the AI sector, but shifting electricity trends could support long-term business success.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 23, 2026 at 7:11PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Bloom Energy ›

Artificial intelligence (AI) is likely to be a world-changing technology, but it uses a huge amount of electricity. AI power demand has been so high that it has driven up electricity costs for everyone, hitting the pocketbooks of everyday people even as inflation is running hot. That's resulted in pushback, with data centers playing a prominent role in the recent midterm elections. That could actually be a net positive for companies like Bloom Energy (BE +18.09%) and Caterpillar (CAT +7.17%). Here's why.

The current administration in Washington has been very supportive of artificial intelligence. With the House and Senate changing hands next year following big wins in November's election, support for AI could waver. There are already states that have put up roadblocks to further data center construction. One of the biggest problems is electricity, with a push for data centers to supply their own power to avoid straining the grid.

That, however, could be a huge win for companies like Bloom Energy and Caterpillar. Bloom Energy makes factory-built hydrogen fuel cells that can be easily deployed where they are needed to provide off-grid power. Caterpillar is best known for making the heavy construction equipment used to build data centers, but it also makes generators used to provide off-grid power.

If data centers have to bring their own power, Bloom Energy and Cat are primed to help. Notably, Bloom Energy entered 2026 with a $6 billion product backlog, up 140% year over year. That backlog has likely grown throughout the year (it only reports its backlog annually) and could get even bigger in the future. The company, which has lost money for years, is likely to turn profitable in 2026.

Caterpillar ended the third quarter with a record $78 billion backlog, up 96% year over year. While not all of that backlog is tied to its power systems, the bigger takeaway is that demand remains very strong across all its products. That said, management noted that bringing up manufacturing capacity in its power division remains a primary goal because demand is so strong.

The shifting political winds in Washington could increase the already strong demand that Bloom Energy and Cat are seeing. But investors need to distinguish between the stocks and the businesses here. AI-related stocks, including both of these companies, have been volatile of late. If you own them or are considering buying them, you need to approach them with a long-term mindset. Another option is to add them to your wish list and be ready to pounce if there is a deep AI-driven bear market.

#2960. The Nasdaq Just Had Its Best Day Since April 2025. Anthropic Stock Fell Anyway.

Anthropic stock slipped on the Nasdaq Composite's best day since April 2025.

By Johnny Rice – Nov 23, 2026 at 7:22PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

For a while this morning, Anthropic (ANT -1.43%) looked like it would soar today after news of Jensen Huang's dramatic return to Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%) broke. Shares of the newly public company opened up 5.4%.

But by the closing bell, Anthropic was down 1.4%. Not a bad day per se, but consider the gains from the rest of the tech market:

Just two trading days after its debut, in what was the year's biggest gain for the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +6.51%), Anthropic stock inched lower. Why did the Claude maker's stock miss out on most of the marketwide rally?

Nvidia's board announced this morning that it was bringing Huang back as CEO, president, and board member. That's just under a week after Board Chair Harvey Jones told investors that Huang's return was not on the table. The official announcement said that the board had "received input from senior levels of the United States government," giving it confidence that "Mr. Huang's reinstatement poses no national security risk."

The government input was, in fact, from the most senior of senior levels: the U.S. President. Trump told CNBC that he spoke with the board "directly and indirectly" to advocate for Huang's return: "I convinced them." Trump reassured the board that he did not pose a national security threat after Attorney General Todd Blanche assured the President that investigators had no evidence to suggest wrongdoing.

Whatever the full reasoning behind that led to the board's decision, the move -- a shock to many -- breathed life into an ailing AI trade that was in serious need of good news; the Nasdaq  turned red for 2026 on Friday after entering a correction earlier in the month.

A few reasons: first, Reuters reported that President Trump -- Vice President JD Vance, too -- intervened in part because they saw Huang as crucial to keeping American companies competitive in open-weight AI -- a market in which China leads.

Open-weight models are offered to the public -- often for free -- to download, tweak, adapt, and run however and wherever they see fit.

That stands in stark contrast to the closed models of Anthropic and rival OpenAI, which offer comparatively little in the way of customization and, most importantly, run in the cloud and can only be accessed through the company. That ensures they get paid every time you use their models. To state the obvious, they do not get paid if a company opts for an open-weight model. That makes them a potential threat to Anthropic's growth -- a pretty substantial one.

Nvidia, on the other hand, gets paid no matter what models get used, so long as the servers powering the model use its chips. More open-weight models mean more potential customers. That's why Jensen Huang has been an advocate, making several major investments in open-weight developers.

Whether or not Huang is at the helm, open-weight models pose a genuine threat to Anthropic's business. It is one of the core reasons I would avoid the stock. At the moment, Anthropic's moat -- the thing keeping customers paying Anthropic and not its competitors -- comes almost entirely from having the best models.

Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

That's not a very strong one in my opinion, and over time, I see Anthropic struggling to keep customers from switching workloads over to cheaper open-weight alternatives.

#2959. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2958. Mortgage Rates Today, Monday, November 23: Decidedly Higher

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Surging tech stocks aren't great news for bonds, or for mortgage rates.

Mortgage rates have had some substantial day-over-day swings lately, and today, unfortunately it's an upward swing.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.38% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points higher than yesterday and four basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Recently news about big tech has often pulled rates down. Investor concerns about AI profitability keep cropping up, and when they do, tech stocks and bonds lose their appeal and Treasuries have been picking up the slack. Mortgage rates are generally benchmarked to the yield on the 10-year Treasury, and Treasury prices and yields move inversely. More demand for the 10YT drives prices up, and sends yields (and thus, mortgage rates) down.

Today's the opposite, though. Investors are having a bout of tech exuberance as Jensen Huang got reinstated as Nvidia CEO, and lots of excitement about tech stocks and bonds means less enthusiasm for Treasuries.

Here's the thing, though: Markets are fickle. They also move fast. And they aren't the only influence on mortgage rates.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context. For more, keep reading below the charts.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Lately, we've seen two narratives influencing mortgage rates battling it out; depending on which one's stronger, daily rates move higher or lower. Up top I talked about how shifting perceptions of big tech have been driving mortgage rates (and yes, today, pushing them higher, though usually it's pulled rates down.) When rates are moving higher, more often it's been thanks to inflation — and while it's a lightweight work week, we're about to get heavyweight inflation news.

The latest Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index is due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely.

The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but its decisions influence the entire economy. That includes mortgage lenders, who tend to adjust rates ahead of its decisions. Raising the federal funds rate (the short-term borrowing rate the central bankers actually set) is the Fed's main tactic for managing inflation, so you can guess how things are likely to go at the Fed's December meeting. Markets already feel fairly certain about a quarter-percent rate hike.

We already know how PCE's likely to go, since it's the last of the three big inflation measures to be released. We've already gotten last month's Consumer Price Index and Producer Price Index. October's CPI came in worse than predicted, showing widespread price increases. (When most of the increase is driven by one area, it's easier to be optimistic that inflation can be contained.) And October’s PPI showed wholesale inflation climbed at its fastest pace in almost four years. PPI measures the price businesses pay for things, so it's often a preview of what consumers could face once those costs get passed along.

Predictions for October PCE aren't great, so at this point the hope is that the numbers aren't even worse than expected. And again, PCE's out Wednesday morning. We'll know whether we're getting seconds (actually, thirds) of bad October inflation news well before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

And speaking of: Markets will be closed on Thanksgiving, and Black Friday's a half day, so we'll all have a bit of a break this week. Mortgage rates don't move much when the markets aren't moving. I won't be here to talk to you about mortgage rates, either, 'cause NerdWallet gives us Thursday and Friday off. Happy Thanksgiving!

P.S.: I don't have to ask if anyone's playing Grand Theft Auto 'cause c'mon, of course. But did anyone else perk up when Lu asked about home loans? Yeah, that was probably just me. Jason and Lucia would have to change a lot about their lifestyle to qualify (we know they're working on building credit, but having all your income be cash you can't really document is not going to work for a lender). But hey, I'm rooting for them.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you could start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.88% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#2957. If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, Warren Buffett Says This Is the Best Move Investors Should Make

With stock market valuations approaching historic extremes, Warren Buffett's investing advice offers a simple strategy for navigating a potential bubble.

By Adam Spatacco – Nov 23, 2026 at 8:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

Despite stubborn inflation, rising interest rates, geopolitical conflicts, and considerable economic uncertainty, the S&P 500 (^GSPC +4.73%), Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +6.51%), and Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI +3.01%) have delivered impressive returns over the last four years. However, this rapid appreciation of stocks has also raised concerns that a bubble could be forming.

Those fears are intensifying as artificial intelligence (AI) stocks retreat from record highs. Both the Nasdaq and the S&P 500 recently entered correction territory, although both indexes are rebounding sharply as Jensen Huang returns to the CEO position of Nvidia.

Fortunately, legendary investor Warren Buffett has a simple strategy for navigating periods of market turbulence.

Between Nov. 30, 2022 -- the day ChatGPT was launched to the public -- and Nov. 20, 2026, the S&P 500 advanced approximately 69%, equivalent to an annualized gain of roughly 14%. The Nasdaq performed even better, climbing roughly 97%, or 18% annually.

Much of this growth was driven by the AI revolution. Companies designing AI chips, providing cloud infrastructure, and building enterprise software have attracted outsize interest from growth investors. Meanwhile, demand for data centers is creating opportunities throughout the economy. Utilities, energy producers, industrial equipment manufacturers, and construction companies are increasingly benefiting from the infrastructure spending required to support AI adoption.

However, enthusiasm surrounding AI is also pushing stock market valuations toward historically expensive levels. One revealing indicator is the Shiller cyclically adjusted price-to-earnings (CAPE) ratio, which measures the S&P 500's price relative to its average inflation-adjusted earnings over the last 10 years.

Unlike a traditional price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio, CAPE readings smooth out short-term fluctuations in earnings. This makes it useful for assessing long-term valuations. Historically, the metric has averaged 17.7, meaning investors have generally paid around $18 for every dollar of normalized earnings.

Currently, the CAPE ratio sits around 41 -- dangerously close to its highest level in history. That places valuations at more than twice their historical average and well above the threshold that has occasionally preceded major market drawdowns.

For perspective, the CAPE ratio exceeded 30 before the 1929 stock market crash. During this period, the Dow plunged 89% through 1932. Meanwhile, the CAPE ratio reached an all-time high of 44 during the dot-com era. Once the bubble burst, the Nasdaq collapsed 77% between March 2000 and October 2002 while the S&P 500 declined approximately 49%.

These examples underscore why elevated valuations deserve attention. With that said, the CAPE ratio is not a reliable indicator of what the market will do in the near term. Moreover, readings between 30 and 40 do not automatically mean a crash is imminent. Rather, elevated CAPE ratios have historically been associated with subdued returns in the immediate years following a crash.

Although bubbles can produce stomach-churning losses in the short term, history demonstrates the resilience of the stock market. The S&P 500 has endured the Great Depression, multiple recessions, wars, financial crises, and numerous presidential administrations while continuing to generate long-term wealth.

Consider what happened to investors who bought stocks right at the height of the dot-com bubble. The Nasdaq peaked at approximately 5,048 in March 2000 before collapsing nearly 77% over the following two and a half years. Nevertheless, the index eventually recovered and surpassed its previous record in 2015.

As of this writing (Nov. 23), the Nasdaq stands near 24,142, representing 386% appreciation from its dot-com era peak. That's respectable even for someone who invested at one of the worst possible moments in modern market history.

The S&P 500 tells a similar story. Its March 2000 peak was 1,527, compared with roughly 7,224 as of this writing. That represents a gain of 375% despite the dot-com collapse, the 2008 financial crisis, and the COVID-19 crash.

The lesson here is straightforward: Even investors who bought stocks at historically expensive valuations eventually generated multibagger gains by remaining invested.

In Berkshire Hathaway's shareholder letter from 2016, Buffett acknowledged that market declines were inevitable. But the "Oracle of Omaha" also emphasized that no one can reliably forecast when they will occur. Buffett observed:

Buffett's investment philosophy emphasizes remaining invested in productive businesses rather than repeatedly moving between stocks and cash based on market predictions. Investors who sell during downturns face two difficult decisions: determining when to exit and deciding when to reinvest.

Getting either decision wrong often has consequences. Stock market recoveries tend to begin when economic conditions still appear bleak, meaning investors who wait for uncertainty to disappear end up missing out on gains.

For investors with a long-term time horizon, a prudent approach involves maintaining a diversified portfolio, holding an appropriate cash reserve, and continuing to invest through periods of volatility. Cash will provide the financial flexibility to buy the dip in quality companies when market sell-offs create compelling opportunities.

At the end of the day, building wealth requires staying invested through market volatility rather than trying to avoid every downturn.

#2956. Mint Mobile Discounts for November 2026

Thanks to commercials starring actor (and former part-owner) Ryan Reynolds, Mint Mobile has become one of the most popular mobile carriers. According to J.D. Power, Mint ranks highest in overall customer satisfaction among wireless carriers. Its prepaid cell phone plans, with no contracts or early termination fees, appeal to many customers, especially those with basic needs. Mint operates on parent company T-Mobile’s network, which has the best 5G coverage among the big three operators. You can bring your own unlocked, compatible device, or purchase a new phone from Mint, including the latest Google Pixel 11, Apple iPhone 18 Pro, or Samsung Galaxy S26 FE.

Mint doesn’t always offer extra discounts in the form of codes or coupons, but its low-cost plans with minimal monthly commitment appeal to groups that need budget phone service, such as seniors on a fixed income or young adults transitioning off their parents’ service. However, it runs promotions that are automatically applied at checkout. Right now, Mint is running an anniversary special that gives you up to 71% off its six-month plans. We like that Mint Mobile makes its plans easy to understand, thanks to a nutritional-value-style breakdown of pricing and fees. If you’ve been looking to switch carriers and have your eye on Mint, you should definitely consider these Mint Mobile promos when you decide.

While Mint Mobile doesn’t offer a specific discount for customers age 55 and older, its low-cost standard plans should appeal to anyone on a budget or who simply wants to pay less for phone service. For seniors who don’t need much data per month, the 6-GB plan is plenty for casual browsing, streaming, app downloads, and emailing. Plus, it includes unlimited talk and texting, which may be more valuable to this age group than doomscrolling on social media.

The 6-GB plan costs $15 a month for three months, plus taxes and fees. Mint plans never require a contract, but you must pay the full $45 upfront. The plan renews for another three months, but the price increases to $25 a month, so it’s $75 plus taxes and fees. It’s still affordable compared with legacy carriers. You don’t have to deal with monthly charges, and there’s no commitment after the three-month term if you wish to cancel. You can switch to a different plan for potential greater savings. Note that Mint doesn’t refund unused months if you cancel outside the seven-day cancellation period (seven days after activation).

To save more, and if their budgets allow, seniors should consider Mint Mobile’s longer-term plans. While they cost more upfront, they save more in the long run. For example, Mint Mobile is currently running an anniversary special on its six-month plans. Seniors can lock in the 6-GB, 17-GB, 23-GB, or unlimited plan for $10 a month for six months. Afterward, the price increases to only $20 a month if you renew the 6-GB plan for another six months, or you can switch to a 3- or 12-month plan. Note the varying upfront costs required.

For the best savings, consider the 12-month plan. It has a higher upfront fee of $180, but it breaks down to $15 a month. Unlike the three-month plan, it stays at $15 after it renews (subject to potential price increases, which are very possible given the nature of subscription services). You pay more at first, but you save even more over time.

Perhaps the best feature for seniors is Mint Mobile’s customer service, staffed by live agents by phone during select hours or available 24/7 via online chat. While many companies are increasingly moving toward AI-based systems, having humans who can help guide customers through issues is refreshing.

Like seniors, Mint Mobile doesn’t offer plans or discounts for kids. Instead, parents can choose a low-cost option that fits their children’s mobile habits. If they rely mostly on Wi-Fi at home, school, or the library, they may not need a high-data plan. If they binge on streaming entertainment and are constantly on social media, Mint offers 17 GB, 23 GB, or unlimited data plans at $20, $25, or $30 per month.

As mentioned above, the different monthly terms (3, 6, and 12 months) require different upfront payments, and prices may also increase after the initial entry price. The best approach is for parents to determine their child’s needs, then browse Mint’s plans that fit their budget. It can get costly as you move up in data and monthly tiers, plus the higher upfront costs, but parents could still save money in the long run compared with a legacy carrier, and without a contract. If additional children also need a phone plan, parents should look at family plans offered by other service providers. Mint Mobile doesn’t offer a traditional family plan, but you can sign up for multiple plans and mix and match based on your needs. Again, do some price comparison to see if you would actually save money.

Something Mint Mobile doesn’t offer is a smartwatch plan. Instead of phones, a popular trend is for parents to give their kids a cellular-enabled Apple Watch. So if this is what you’re looking for, sadly, Mint Mobile won’t work for this purpose.

Mint offers home internet from its parent, T-Mobile. Like its phone plans, Mint offers Home Minternet packages in 3-, 6-, or 12-month terms, each with an upfront payment. Prices start at $40 per month for a three-month term ($120 upfront).

Right now, Mint Mobile is offering a 12-month plan for $45 per month ($540 upfront) that bundles 12 months of unlimited data—a $300 savings compared with buying both separately. Since home internet can easily cost double what Mint charges, this could be a significant cost-saver for those with modest internet needs that T-Mobile’s 5G network can handle.

#2955. Infleqtion: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Its $560 million cash pile funds a manufacturing push, but whether computing revenue can grow fast enough to lift operating leverage remains an open question.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 23, 2026 at 8:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Infleqtion ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 16, 2026, Infleqtion (INFQ +6.80%) announced that it achieved 30 logical qubits -- error-corrected quantum processing units -- ahead of its 2026 target. Demonstrating hardware efficiency early suggests the company can scale its systems faster and at lower capital cost than competitors. Three takeaways from management's comments stand out for shareholders.

Logical qubits are the error-resistant processing units required for commercial quantum applications. Management delivered 30 logical qubits using just 80 physical qubits -- a ratio under three physical qubits per logical qubit -- which shows its neutral-atom hardware, controlled by light lasers, requires less physical equipment to perform complex calculations.

Commercial interest is converting into hard commitments as system sales expand globally. Total remaining performance obligations -- unbilled contract value committed under signed agreements -- reached $51.2 million in the quarter, laying a firm foundation for computing sales to expand in 2027.

Quantum sensing platforms generate steady cash flow while quantum computing technology matures. A $20 million expansion from NASA to an existing total gravity-sensing contract illustrates how government agencies rely on the company's precise atomic measurements for space and defense missions.

Demand for neutral-atom architecture is building across commercial and defense markets, backed by $560 million in cash and short-term investments. Management is prioritizing manufacturing expansion and system delivery timelines as customer engagement accelerates.

Scaling hardware shipments remains essential for generating operating leverage as computing revenue expands alongside established sensing operations.
