#2954. I Rate Buy Now, Pay Later Apps; Here’s My Holiday Advice

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

“Buy now, pay later” can help stretch your holiday budget further, but pick your BNPL app carefully and avoid loan stacking.

Head of Content, Personal & Student Loans

As Americans compile their shopping lists ahead of the holiday season, I’ve been compiling my own kind of list — a list of 25 data points, gathered across six “buy now, pay later” (BNPL) apps, fed into a rubric of five categories and 22 subcategories, to produce a star rating for each app.

It’s a big list. I check it twice (or 10 times).

By the end, I know more about these apps than anyone should, and I’m eager to bestow my knowledge on the next unsuspecting person who utters “Klarna” in my vicinity.

I should have plenty of opportunities this year. Nearly 2 in 5 holiday spenders (38%) plan to use BNPL, according to NerdWallet’s holiday spending report. Concerningly, the same report found that a higher share of BNPL users expect they won’t be able to make their payments in full and on time compared to credit card users.

Using BNPL for holiday shopping isn’t inherently bad, but there are risks, including sneaky fees and the ease of loan stacking across many providers.

Here’s how to minimize those risks and set yourself up for success this holiday season.

Brenton Harrison, a certified financial planner in Nashville, says many of his clients are turning to buy now, pay later in the “messy middle of life,” when everyday expenses are high and budgets are tight. This is especially true during the holidays.

“They have kids they're providing for, or they’re trying to go travel and see family,” Harrison says. “When those expenses pile up, it can be very attractive to be able to spread out payments.”

Since BNPL divides your total purchase into smaller installments, typically due every two weeks, it feels easier on your cash flow than having to pay all at once.

While Harrison is open to his clients using BNPL, he emphasizes how important it is to understand the terms before opting in. BNPL apps only require a soft credit pull and a short application. When you can apply for a loan in just a few clicks, it’s easy to miss when payments are due or whether they’ll include interest.

But if clients understand the terms and are certain they can make the payments, Harrison says BNPL's set repayment schedule can be a better option than more open-ended financing.

“With a credit card, [a holiday purchase] might be something that they buy over and over and over again, because that interest just continues to accrue over time, and they never pay it off,” he says.

Not all BNPL apps are created equal. I measure the top apps across multiple categories, but I weigh affordability the highest. My thinking is if you’re already strapped for cash, it’s a bad idea to pay extra just to break up the payments.

There are providers, like Affirm and PayPal, that don’t charge interest with a common pay-in-four term (your total purchase, divided into four installments). They also charge no fees, including no late fees for missed payments.

Compare that to apps, like Sezzle and Zip, that may charge an automatic service or origination fee with your first installment, as well as late fees.

Long-term BNPL financing, which can stretch months or even years, usually includes interest, expressed as an annual percentage rate or APR. This is a more expensive payment option than a zero-interest pay-in-four.

Pay close attention to your checkout screen to see what you’re offered. Are the installment amounts equal or is the first installment higher than the others? That may mean there’s a fee. Do you see an APR listed? That means you’re paying interest.

Harrison points out that just because a store partners with a BNPL company doesn’t mean that’s your only option. Download a different BNPL app, search for the store and see if you get access to better terms, like a 0% offer.

Taking out multiple loans with different BNPL providers is known as “loan stacking,” and it’s risky.

Because you’re juggling different payment amounts due on different days, it’s hard to keep track of what’s coming out of your bank account. This is especially true if you set up automatic payments, which most BNPL apps require. If you overdraw your checking account, your bank will likely charge a fee.

According to research published in August by the Federal Reserve, 11% percent of BNPL users had a BNPL payment trigger an overdraft or nonsufficient funds fee from their bank in the prior year. People with less savings were even more likely to pay fees.

Before applying for multiple BNPL loans, look at a typical month of expenses and ask if there’s extra room in your budget for those payments, Harrison says.

“If you’re just assuming that because you’re spreading it out that you’ll be able to pay it, that's where I think people can get in trouble,” he says. “They set it and forget it, and they don't realize, ‘Oh, I am in no better position to pay that next month than I am this month, or I have three of these going at the same time, and collectively, it's more than I can manage.’”

Most apps send payment reminders, so you can potentially reschedule the payment or change your payment method.

Still, it’s best to avoid the headache to begin with. If you’re feeling pressured to spend more this holiday season, communicate your limits clearly with loved ones, Harrison says, and avoid any loans you can’t comfortably repay.

“Think about the purchase itself, especially around the holidays,” he says. “There's a big difference between something that I can pay off over three months and something that I should have never been buying in the first place.”

#2953. CFO Colette Kress Just Made This Subtle Shift That Could Save Nvidia Shareholders

Nvidia may now take a more deliberate approach to customer financing.

By Will Healy – Nov 23, 2026 at 8:06PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%) continues to impress shareholders. Even though large companies tend to grow slowly, Nvidia has challenged that assertion by delivering triple-digit revenue growth, much as it did in the third quarter of fiscal 2027 (ended Oct. 25).

Unfortunately for shareholders, CEO Jensen Huang financed much of this growth through "circular financing," in which the company finances customers to sell products.

CFO Colette Kress, who was briefly interim CEO, questioned this strategy. She reined in much of this financial support for other companies' artificial intelligence (AI) projects. Assuming Huang follows her lead, here's how this approach could save Nvidia shareholders.

In recent months, investors have become more concerned about how Nvidia generated its revenue growth.

Nvidia was to provide financial backing for an OpenAI data center in Ohio. Huang wanted to backstop $250 billion of OpenAI purchases, but Kress stepped in to cut that commitment to $105 billion.

Although Kress has rejected the "circular financing" label, as interim CEO, she stated on the third-quarter 2027 earnings call that Nvidia would be "disciplined and selective" in its support for the neoclouds, which will likely reassure those investors who questioned Huang's prior approach.

This is critical for investors because analysts often view such financing as consistent with the end stage of a boom.

They have good reason to worry. Cisco Systems (CSCO +6.80%) was at the center of the dot-com boom when it backstopped several of its customers. In 2001, Cisco famously had to write down $2.25 billion in one quarter when several of the customers it backed declared bankruptcy. That write-down contributed to a stock price decline so profound that it took 26 years for its stock to return to its 2000 high.

With "everything Kress told us," the company carries around $118 billion in guarantees, several orders of magnitude higher than Cisco's write-down. Still, if projects fall behind schedule, the company will now revoke guarantees, a move that could shield it from painful write-offs later.

Moreover, Nvidia may not be vulnerable to a slump as severe as Cisco's. Nvidia's 18 P/E ratio as of Nov. 23 is a tiny fraction of the 222 P/E ratio that Cisco reached in early 2000, suggesting less downside if an AI bust occurs.

Furthermore, Kress's approach did not appear to meaningfully slow Nvidia, even in the short term. For fiscal Q4, it guided for $130 billion in revenue at the midpoint, a 91% year-over-year increase if that forecast holds. While that is slightly less than fiscal Q3's 103% revenue increase, it may be a small price to pay to mitigate the specter of massive write-downs later.

Kress's move to rein in customer financing could alleviate a significant worry about the stock, assuming Huang follows his advice now that he has returned as CEO.

Indeed, the possibility of bankruptcies among major customers leaves Nvidia financially exposed, and that vulnerability could hurt the stock.

Nonetheless, Kress's decision to be "disciplined and selective" about customer financing addresses a key concern with Nvidia's current business approach. While it may mean somewhat slower growth, the prospect of reduced financial exposure should an AI bust occur is likely a wise move in the long run.

#2952. Etihad’s New Credit Cards Are Primarily for Status Chasers

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The new cards offer perks like faster paths to status and elevated earning rates when purchasing tickets with partners.

Etihad Guest, the loyalty program of Abu Dhabi-based Etihad Airways, is expanding its U.S. presence by introducing two new credit cards. Most people won’t get much value from these cards, but they can make sense for Etihad frequent flyers who want elite status.

The two cards are the Etihad Guest Visa Signature® Card ($99 annual fee) and the Etihad Guest Premier Visa Signature® Card ($499 annual fee). Issued by the First National Bank of Omaha, both offer faster pathways to earning and maintaining elite status and the ability to earn extra miles on Etihad purchases.

They also offer extra miles on international transactions (neither has foreign transaction fees) and purchases with Etihad’s partner airlines, which include U.S. carriers like American Airlines and JetBlue Airways.

Here’s what you need to know about the cards.

The Etihad Guest Visa Signature® Card comes with a $99 annual fee, and it earns Etihad Guest miles at the following rates:

3 miles per $1 spent on Etihad purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on Etihad partner airline purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on non-U.S. purchases.

1 mile per $1 spent on all other U.S. purchases.

Welcome offer: Through Feb. 3, 2027, the card is offering the following welcome offer for new applicants: Earn up to 40,000 welcome Etihad Guest Miles, including 10,000 miles after the first purchase and an additional 30,000 miles after spending $3,000 within the first three billing cycles after account opening.

Elite status benefits: You’ll also get Etihad Silver status after your first Etihad purchase within 90 days of opening your account. Unlike most airlines, that entry-level status is enough to get you into the business class lounge when traveling through Etihad’s hub at Abu Dhabi’s Zayed International Airport.

You can retain that status by spending $20,000 a year on the card, or $3,000 a year on Etihad purchases.

The Etihad Guest Premier Visa Signature® Card comes with a $499 annual fee, and it earns Etihad Guest miles at the following rates:

5 miles per $1 spent on Etihad purchases.

3 miles per $1 spent on Etihad partner airline purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on non-U.S. purchases.

1 mile per $1 spent on all other U.S. purchases.

The only differences in rewards between the premium and standard cards are increased earning rates for eligible airline purchases.

Welcome offer: Through Feb. 3, 2027, the premium card offers a higher welcome offer compared to the standard card: Earn up to 60,000 welcome Etihad Guest Miles, including 20,000 miles after the first purchase and 40,000 miles after spending $7,000 within the first three billing cycles after account opening.

Elite status benefits: The premium card offers a faster track to elite status: automatic Silver status upon card approval and Gold status after your first Etihad purchase within 90 days of opening your account.

To maintain Gold status, you’ll need to spend $40,000 a year on the card, or $8,000 on Etihad purchases. Otherwise, you’ll have Silver status.

Wi-Fi benefit: After your first Etihad purchase, you’ll get 3,000 Etihad Guest miles, enough to cover Wi-Fi costs on a long-haul Etihad flight. You’ll then get those miles again after each cardholder anniversary.

Travel benefits: The card offers a statement credit for Global Entry or TSA PreCheck and select travel protections. If you already have a premium travel card, you might already have access to these perks.

The card also includes a Priority Pass lounge access membership. However, an Etihad spokesperson told NerdWallet that it’s the entry-level membership tier, so you’ll need to pay for yourself and guests each time you visit a Priority Pass lounge. Most premium credit cards in the U.S. that offer Priority Pass allow complimentary visits for at least the cardholder.

The Etihad cards make the most sense for Etihad’s frequent flyers who are flying to or connecting through Abu Dhabi several times a year.

That’s because the standard card’s accelerated pathway to Etihad Guest’s Silver status tier can unlock the ability to choose business class lounge access in Abu Dhabi as a status perk.

With the premium card, you get a fast track to Gold. If you select lounge access as a Gold status perk, you can not only bring a guest on the same flight into the lounge, but also access Etihad’s worldwide lounge network. That includes Etihad’s own lounges in New York and Washington, D.C., as well as most partner lounges at other Etihad destinations.

Bear in mind that while Etihad has many partnerships with other airlines, it’s not part of an alliance, so you don’t get lounge benefits when traveling on partners. You’ll need to be flying on an Etihad-operated flight to get into the lounge.

If you primarily fly with other carriers, then Etihad status will have limited upside for you.

However, the Priority Pass lounge membership that comes with the premium card does not have that restriction. You can access Priority Pass lounges while flying on any airline, although you’ll need to pay an additional per-person $35 fee each visit.

The earning rates on the Etihad cards are lackluster for everyday spenders. The bonus categories are for airline purchases with Etihad and its partners, as well as international spending.

That means your day-to-day purchases at home, like groceries, dining out and gas, earn only 1 mile per $1 spent.

Plus, there are more ways to earn Etihad Guest miles than spending on an Etihad credit card. For example, you can transfer points to Etihad Guest from Bilt Rewards, Capital One and Citi ThankYou.

Those programs offer cards that typically have better benefits and bonus categories, and they offer flexibility on how you can use your points and miles. With an Etihad card, you can only earn Etihad Guest miles, which expire after 18 months without qualifying flight activity. (If you’re not traveling on Etihad or its partners at least once every 18 months, then you definitely should not get the cards.)

One of the most interesting perks of the new cards is the bonus category for spending with Etihad partner airlines — 2 miles per $1 for the standard card and 3 miles per $1 for the premium version. Typically, airline credit cards reward customers only for spending on the airline, not partners.

There are better cards available, though. Some offer as many or even more points per $1 on flights purchased directly from airlines.

If you want the card solely to earn more Etihad Guest miles, then the answer for most people is no.

While you can earn more miles for purchases with Etihad and partner airlines, the earning rates aren’t attractive enough to justify the annual fees, especially when compared with existing cards with currencies that transfer to Etihad Guest.

Status chasers could benefit from the fast track to elite status — but you would need to be a fairly frequent Etihad flyer to really take advantage of your elite benefits.

You want a travel credit card that prioritizes what’s important to you. Here are some of the best travel credit cards of 2026:

Flexibility, point transfers and a large bonus: Chase Sapphire Preferred® Card

No annual fee: Wells Fargo Autograph® Card

Flat-rate travel rewards: Capital One Venture Rewards Credit Card

Bonus travel rewards and high-end perks: Chase Sapphire Reserve®

Luxury perks: American Express Platinum Card®

Business travelers: Ink Business Preferred® Credit Card

#2951. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2947. What Is a Reconditioning Fee on a Used Car?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Used-car dealers may charge you a reconditioning fee to cover their costs of getting cars ready for sale.

A reconditioning fee is an added dealer charge you might find when buying a used car. It’s intended to cover any costs the dealer incurs to make sure a used car is showroom-ready, from mechanical inspections to detailing.

The reconditioning process varies between dealerships, but typically includes these steps:

A visual exterior inspection. An auto tech does a quick walk-around the vehicle and notes any corrosion, damage or wear.

An interior inspection. The engine and auto interior are examined and tested, and any issues are documented.

A test drive. The car is taken out for a drive to check that it runs well and that the radio, brakes, A/C and other components are all functioning properly.

Service and repair. Any interior or exterior mechanical, functional or cosmetic issues that were found are repaired, including paint, dents, oil changes and major engine work.

Inspection. Once repairs are completed, the car is inspected again to make sure everything looks and functions as it should.

Detailing. This includes deep interior, engine and exterior cleaning as well as any necessary leather refurbishing, bumper repair or wheel restoration.

Reconditioning fees are legal, but that doesn’t mean you have to pay them. Dealers can charge whatever they want for a reconditioning fee, and it’s not uncommon to find them ranging from around $500 to over $2,000.

The good news: Reconditioning fees, like most dealer extras, can be negotiable. Ask the dealer for a breakdown of the reconditioning fee to see what kind of work was done to prepare the car for sale. If the work was minor but the fee is high — for example, if they’re asking for $1,995 but replaced the floor mats only — that’s a good sign it’s a bogus charge.

However, when it comes time for negotiation, don’t just haggle over the reconditioning fee or other individual fees. The best way to negotiate is to focus on the out-the-door price, or the total amount you’re paying for the car. This simplifies the negotiation process and keeps you focused on the true price of your car.

All dealers invest some amount in reconditioning, but those costs are often built into the dealer’s asking price for a vehicle, rather than as a separate charge.

Some dealers opt to add a reconditioning fee to the bill because it allows them to advertise a lower asking price online, which doesn’t include fees. It’s a tactic to get you into their dealership, since the asking price is lower than that of other dealerships.

However, once you arrive, you may find that the out-the-door price is the same — or more — compared with what other dealers are asking.

Some car-buying experts recommend avoiding dealerships that charge reconditioning fees altogether. But remember that the out-the-door price is what matters at the end of the day, so it’s worth comparing prices at multiple dealerships to find the best deal.

Reddit is an online forum where users share their thoughts in “threads” on various topics. The popular site includes plenty of discussion on financial subjects like car buying, so we sifted through Reddit threads to get a sense of how users feel about reconditioning fees. People post anonymously, so we cannot confirm their individual experiences or circumstances.

Reddit users are generally skeptical toward reconditioning fees. Many users view these fees as a bait-and-switch tactic: dealers advertise an attractive price online and then tack on the fee at the dealership, inflating the actual out-the-door cost well above what was listed. Car buyers frequently share stories of being hit with reconditioning fees ranging from a few hundred dollars up to $2,000 or more, often with little or no evidence of what work was actually done.

Car-buying experts on Reddit point out that most dealerships already build reconditioning costs into the sticker price, so if the fee is listed separately, the dealership may be double-dipping. Several users suggest that car buyers ask their dealer for an itemized breakdown of the work done, but most users recommend negotiating the total out-the-door price rather than individual fees.

#2946. 2 Asset Manager Stocks to Buy That Each Oversee More Than $1 Trillion

BlackRock and Blackstone offer buyers very different setups on growth, valuation, and risk.

By Rick Orford – Nov 23, 2026 at 8:38PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Blackstone ›

BlackRock (BLK +3.88%) and Blackstone (BX +4.57%) each oversee more than $1 trillion in assets, making them two of the largest asset management firms worldwide. But with one trading closer to its 52-week high and the other down sharply this year, the question is: Which stock offers the more attractive opportunity for buyers?

BlackRock offers exposure to one of the most diversified businesses in asset management. With $15.5 trillion in assets under management (AUM) at the end of the third quarter, up 15% from the previous year, the company has built an enormous platform spanning index funds, exchange-traded funds (ETFs), and private markets.

On top of that, the company sees broad demand, attracting massive inflows across equities, fixed-income investments, alternative investments, and cash. For example, its iShares ETF business remains an important growth engine, while its active investment strategies attract capital from investors seeking opportunities beyond passive index investing.

Its third-quarter performance further strengthened the bull case in favor of the stock. Its third-quarter revenue rose 19% year over year, while adjusted earnings per share (EPS) climbed 38%. Looking ahead, its ability to attract new money while expanding its technology and private markets businesses gives it several avenues for continued earnings growth.

The main drawback is valuation. So far this year, BlackRock stock is up about 5% and trades at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio based on estimated earnings performance of about 18, which means investors are paying about $18 for every $1 of expected earnings. That leaves less room for error if market conditions deteriorate or inflows slow down.

Another company has also built its business around alternative assets, giving investors exposure to private equity, real estate, and infrastructure.

Blackstone has also established itself as one of the biggest names in alternative-asset management, with $1.4 trillion in AUM as of the third quarter. More broadly, its latest results suggest that investor demand remains healthy despite a challenging backdrop for private markets. Specifically, the company attracted $63.3 billion in new money, bringing its trailing 12-month (TTM) inflows to $271.6 billion.

Beyond inflows, one encouraging development is the growth of its perpetual capital business, which reached $562 billion. These investment vehicles are designed to provide capital without the fixed fund life and fundraising cycles associated with traditional private equity funds. This gives Blackstone a larger pool of relatively stable capital that can support more predictable fee revenue over time.

Blackstone is also benefiting from several major investment themes, particularly artificial intelligence (AI), energy infrastructure, and private credit. After all, building data centers, expanding electricity capacity, and financing AI infrastructure requires enormous amounts of capital. And Blackstone has established investment platforms across all these themes, allowing it to participate in the biggest opportunities of the next decade.

Blackstone shares have fallen almost 27% this year, but that has made its valuation more reasonable, with the stock trading at a forward P/E of about 16. Looking ahead, if transaction activity improves and fundraising accelerates, the stock could recover well.

Overall, both BlackRock and Blackstone offer exposure to the growing asset management industry, but their valuations and growth drivers present different opportunities for investors.

BlackRock offers scale, diversification, and strong earnings growth, making it appealing to investors seeking steady long-term growth, although its premium valuation limits the margin for error. Blackstone offers exposure to private markets and AI infrastructure at a considerably lower price, potentially giving investors greater upside if the company recovers. In other words, these giants appeal to different buyers: BlackRock is steadier, Blackstone is riskier. But each looks like a buy.

#2945. How to Invest in Humanoid Robots: 3 Stocks to Watch

Humanoid robots are on the verge of becoming mainstream, and these three stocks are positioned to benefit.

By Marc Guberti – Nov 23, 2026 at 9:31PM EST

Humanoid robots grab people's attention as they walk on sidewalks. Granted, it's a rare sight since this technology is in its early stages, but there is plenty of excitement about the future.

While many start-ups are creating their own humanoid robots, it may be better for investors to invest in the companies selling shovels during a gold rush. Once hints of humanoid robotics leaders become more apparent and finances improve, it may be worth considering smaller companies.

For now, these three tech giants look well-positioned for the humanoid robot boom.

Tesla (TSLA +11.46%) is the most direct, established American company for exposure to humanoid robots. The company has been developing its Optimus robot segment for several years and has sufficient revenue and profits to ensure financial sustainability.

Revenue reached $28 billion in Q3, which was roughly flat year over year. The company's "Services and other revenue" component was the only part of the business to grow, suggesting that Tesla's future growth will have to come from robots.

Net income wasn't as exciting, down 43% year over year, but $787 million in pure profit is still more impressive than humanoid robot start-up companies. While Tesla has been a volatile growth stock, it's actually one of the least-risky ways to gain exposure to humanoid robots. Tesla anticipates delivering orders in late 2027.

Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%) isn't specifically a robotics stock, but its artificial intelligence chips are foundational for robotics. As demand for humanoid robots soars, Nvidia chips will gain even more traction. Grand View Research projects a 38.2% CAGR for the humanoid robot market through 2033, indicating the type of momentum Nvidia can ride.

While high demand for humanoid robots is critical to Tesla's long-term investment thesis, Nvidia continues to perform well in current times. Revenue surged 103% year over year in the company's fiscal 2027 third quarter, and its impressive guidance shows it is taking steps to gain market share.

Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress highlighted that the company's roadmap is on track and that demand is still booming. That's the type of news investors want to hear as 2026 comes to a close, and it further silences concerns about an AI bubble.

Meanwhile, AI stocks have been trending downward for several weeks. Fundamentals are still growing, but the best explanation may be concerns about higher rates and uncertainty with oil, even as the commodity's price continues to trend downward.

In any event, this sharp correction has produced an enticing valuation for Nvidia, making it cheaper than most S&P 500 (^GSPC +4.73%) stocks and putting it deep into value-stock territory.

Micron (MU +19.04%) plays a similar role to Nvidia in humanoid robots. They require vast amounts of memory to interact with the world and learn from their training, and Micron's chips fill in the gap.

Its technology will be part of the humanoid robot bedrock, and, like Nvidia, Micron isn't waiting for these robots to become mainstream before delivering compelling financial results.

Micron anticipates $61.5 billion in fiscal 2027 first-quarter revenue. That's more than a 4x increase from the $13.64 billion Micron generated in the same quarter last year. Micron has established a good reputation for beating the high end of its guidance throughout the AI boom, which makes a 5x growth rate possible.

Multi-year customer deals highlight the sustainability of the AI boom, but even with these bullish catalysts, Micron has been caught in the recent negative sentiment of the AI trade. It has a P/E ratio below 10, which offers an attractive entry point for investors who can wait out the current volatility.

#2944. Quaker Chemical: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Structural cost cuts and Asian market pivots insulate earnings growth even as traditional automotive demand weakens globally.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 23, 2026 at 9:36PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Quaker Chemical ›

#2943. What Are Repositioning Cruises?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Repositioning cruises move ships between seasonal destinations, often with longer sailings, fewer port stops and lower fares.

A repositioning cruise is a one-way sailing that moves a ship from one region to another. For example, many ships sail in the Mediterranean Sea during the summer and move to the Caribbean Sea for the winter, and that trip between the two is a repositioning cruise.

Repositioning cruises are most common in spring (roughly March to May) and fall (roughly September to November), though they can occur at other times of year.

Because ships are crossing oceans, these trips often include many days at sea. For some travelers, that’s a chance to relax and enjoy the ship’s onboard amenities. Sometimes, these sailings can also be cheaper than traditional cruises.

One key consideration for a repositioning cruise is that the embarkation and disembarkation ports will be different. This means you generally can’t buy a standard round-trip flight to and from the same city. Instead, you’ll need transportation to where the cruise starts and another way home from where it ends, which usually means two one-way flights (or one open-jaw ticket) unless you live near one of the ports. While these one-way sailings may be more affordable than traditional cruises, airfare costs may be higher.

This could be a great opportunity to redeem miles for your flight.

Another feature that sets these cruises apart is that stops along the way tend to include ports that aren’t traditionally part of regular itineraries. These might include islands like Bermuda, the Azores or the Canary Islands, and on some northern routes, Greenland.

Just keep in mind that the main purpose of these sailings is to get to a new region for the next season’s itineraries rather than make daily port stops. So if you’re someone who wants to visit as many places as possible on a cruise, a repositioning sailing may not be the ideal choice.

Repositioning cruises are often longer than standard cruises, commonly running two to three weeks compared with about a week for a typical sailing, which may appeal to travelers looking for an extended vacation. The experience is often more relaxed, with many days at sea and less of a rush to book shore excursions or visit important sites when off the ship.

If there are particular ships with certain amenities — like tattoo parlors or massive waterparks — that you really want to experience, keep in mind that not all ships reposition between regions. Some stay in certain regions year-round.

For ships that do change locations for the season, there are plenty of onboard activities to fill the time. You can enjoy shows, food and wine tastings, organized activities and time at the spa, pool or casino.

Many repositioning crossings are relatively smooth, and modern ships use stabilizers to reduce movement. But since these itineraries cross open ocean rather than staying closer to land, there can be more motion, especially on North Atlantic crossings in fall and winter.

If you tend to get seasick, this could be a dealbreaker. Even if a repositioning sailing is smooth, temperatures can be chilly on the open ocean, especially on northerly or spring and fall transatlantic routes, with strong winds that may make suntanning by the pool less appealing.

You can book one of these sailings in the same way you would book a traditional cruise. But if you’re searching for dates and itineraries on a cruise line’s website, they may be listed as transatlantic, transpacific or Panama Canal sailings rather than repositioning cruises, though some lines do use the term "repositioning." There’s even a website that specializes in rounding up these cruises: RepoCruises.com.

Keep an eye out for themed sailings that focus on topics like wine, theater or music, where fellow guests on the ship may share similar interests. Cruise lines often schedule them on repositioning cruises to spur demand.

Here are some repositioning cruises for 2027. They often take place during shoulder seasons, between the most popular cruising times in places like the Mediterranean or Caribbean.

This journey repositions the ship from Miami, where it sails on Caribbean itineraries, to Seattle, where it will visit ports in Alaska during the spring and summer months. Departing April 8, 2027, the 18-night cruise includes stops at various ports in Colombia, Panama and Mexico, as well as San Diego and Los Angeles, before arriving in Seattle on April 26.

Regent Seven Seas Cruises' all-suite luxury ship Seven Seas Mariner will sail between New York and Lisbon, making stops in Canada, Morocco and the Portuguese islands of Madeira and the Azores.

Departing April 29, 2027, the 16-night journey ends in Lisbon, Portugal, as the ship repositions to Europe, where it will sail various itineraries, including Mediterranean sailings later in the summer season.

Departing Seattle on Sept. 28, 2027, Norwegian Jade will sail to Yokohama, Japan, with stops in Alaska and northern Japan. This is a way to travel across the Pacific with less jet lag, since the journey takes place over several days, with ship clocks typically adjusted gradually (often an hour at a time) during the sailing. Note that the voyage also crosses the International Date Line.

If Greenland has been on your bucket list, this 16-night sailing is a way to get up close and personal with its natural scenery. Departing Reykjavik on Aug. 25, 2027, Azamara Quest will sail from Iceland to the U.S. It will make stops in Greenland, Canada and New England before reaching the Big Apple.

Departing Bergen, Norway, on June 11, 2027, this 28-night sailing aboard Viking Vela sails around Iceland's coast, makes two stops in Greenland and crosses the Atlantic before reaching New York.

#2942. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

#2941. Anthropic Stock Fell About 20% From Its First Trade in Its First 2 Days. Here's the Price at Which I'd Buy It.

The Claude maker's shares got within 5 cents of their IPO price on Friday. My number is lower.

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 23, 2026 at 9:47PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Shares of Claude maker Anthropic (ANT -1.43%) had a rough welcome to the public market. The stock's first trade on Thursday, Nov. 19, came at $111.50. It was around 34% over the $83 a share the company set for its initial public offering (IPO) a day earlier.

By the close on Friday, Nov. 20, shares had sunk to $88.91 -- around 20% under the first trade. They even dipped to $83.05 in Friday's session, just 5 cents over the IPO price, on a day the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +6.51%) lost around 5%.

Even after the drop, Anthropic's valuation is about $1.19 trillion. That means investors are paying around 34 times sales, based on the $35 billion or so in revenue the company took in over the 12 months through September.

At what price would the stock be worth buying? I'd put it at $75 a share, around 16% below Friday's close and about 10% under the IPO price. Here's how I get there.

Showing how fast its Claude artificial intelligence (AI) models have caught on with firms, Anthropic's third-quarter revenue climbed 1,127% year over year to around $17.0 billion. The company's revenue for 2025 was around $4.6 billion. However, its sequential growth slowed sharply in the last quarter. Anthropic's revenue climbed around 132% from the previous quarter in the first quarter of 2026 and about 144% in the second quarter, but only about 47% in the third.

Sure, 47% growth in one quarter is still impressive for a company this size. But it's well below the 144% a quarter earlier, and I think the slower pace is the better guide to the year ahead.

Anthropic's third-quarter operating loss was $567 million, versus operating income of $974 million in the second quarter.

The company said the swing was largely due to $5.0 billion of compute bought under short-term commitments, which now costs much more than compute under its multi-year deals. Anthropic also took $2.0 billion of non-cash charges linked to its donation matching program.

Compute is Anthropic's largest operating cost. Its compute and infrastructure expenses fell to 48% of revenue in the second quarter, down from 72% in the first, before rising back to 58% in the third. In other words, the improvement that took the company to an operating profit in Q2 partly reversed a quarter later.

Also, as of and after Sept. 30, Anthropic had committed to around $900.9 billion in spending, almost all of it on compute and infrastructure. Around $70.7 billion of that is due over the following 12 months.

True, Anthropic can afford a lot. Including the offering, it had around $245 billion in cash and short-term investments on a pro forma basis. But a dollar of Anthropic's revenue arguably isn't worth as much as a dollar of revenue at a software-as-a-service business when more than half of it goes straight back out to pay for compute.

Say Anthropic's revenue rises around 16% each quarter on average in the next year, about a third of its third-quarter rate. Revenue for the next four quarters would then total about $100 billion.

That sounds like a fair assumption to me, but it isn't a given. Anthropic said in its prospectus that it expects its revenue growth rate to fall over time. And its customer usage has tended to slip around the holidays.

Given how much of every revenue dollar goes to compute, I'd want to pay no more than about 10 times that forward revenue. At Friday's close, investors were paying about 17.5 times sales based on Anthropic's annualized third-quarter revenue. At 10 times sales, Anthropic's market cap might be around $1 trillion.

Split $1 trillion across the 13.4 billion or so shares outstanding after the offering, and you get around $75 a share. And I wouldn't go above that, because the share count might rise when employee stock awards vest and convertible notes convert.

There's also more stock headed to the market. Up to 315 million shares owned by Anthropic's non-founder directors, officers, employees, and consultants became eligible for sale on the first trading day.

Anthropic is arguably one of the most impressive companies to go public in years. But at Friday's close of around $89 a share, buyers were paying for fast growth to last and for compute costs to drop back down.

At $75 or below, I'd buy shares -- maybe in stages. Above that, I'm happy to wait.

#2940. SpaceX Stock Is Down 39% From Its High. Here's What Has to Happen Before I'd Buy.

SpaceX's business is thriving -- but something needs to happen before it becomes a buy in my book.

By Steven Porrello – Nov 23, 2026 at 10:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

It's a bit of a riddle: A stock loses nearly 40% of its value, yet still looks expensive by traditional metrics. If that rings a bell, you're likely thinking of the predicament facing investors interested in Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +4.35%), better known as SpaceX.

SpaceX shares have dropped to $136, a roughly 39% decline from an all-time high of about $225. Even after that considerable decline, SpaceX still carries a sizable valuation -- about $1.9 trillion at this writing.

What would it take for me to buy SpaceX stock? Has its recent decline brought it any closer to that point?

SpaceX is a behemoth of a business. Three-headed, fast-growing, and formidable, it operates across three segments: rockets and spacecraft (Space), a global communications network (Starlink), and artificial intelligence (Super Intelligence).

Most people would recognize the first category. Rockets and spacecraft are, in fact, SpaceX's bread and butter, but they're not the business's breadwinner -- at least, not by revenue. That distinction belongs to its AI division, which it calls Super Intelligence. The business generated an impressive $7.6 billion in revenue in the third quarter alone. Connectivity comes second, with $5.2 billion, while rockets and spacecraft contributed about $1.8 billion.

Revenue growth is rarely a thumbs-down, but it's even sweeter when the company keeps more of those extra dollars. Here, too, SpaceX gets a gold star: It reported $4.4 billion in net income for Q3, up substantially from a $1.1 billion loss the previous year. That's a great turnaround for a company investing so heavily in its future -- so why am I not investing in SpaceX today?

Previously, my concern about SpaceX stock was its valuation. That is still my concern; the stock trades at roughly 60 times trailing sales, more than 16 times the S&P 500 (^GSPC +4.73%). Now there's another thing holding me back -- the cost of its growth.

During the first nine months of 2026, SpaceX generated about $13 billion in operating cash flow but spent a staggering $48 billion on capital expenditures, roughly $40 billion of which was on its AI business. That works out to about $35 billion in negative free cash flow for the year -- so far. Management is expecting to spend another $35 billion to $40 billion in the fourth quarter alone.

Obviously, it isn't a bad thing to spend aggressively to grow a business -- and SpaceX has three of them. What worries me is the risks that come with spending billions on AI infrastructure, where the company faces some pretty deep-pocketed competitors. They include Microsoft (MSFT +1.65%), Amazon (AMZN +3.47%), and Alphabet (GOOGL +6.34%) (GOOG +5.88%), just to name the big ones. True, SpaceX has shown that it can compete in this market, but whether it can create the same strong competitive advantages that have made it dominant in rockets and satellite communications remains to be seen.

With tens of billions being thrown at AI, I want some proof that the investment will pay off. Indeed, at a lower valuation, I might be willing to take that risk, but at 60 times trailing sales -- no thanks. I'm fine letting this one play out for now.

#2939. Nvidia Just Built Agent Security Into Its Chips. What Does That Mean for CrowdStrike?

A brief mention on Nvidia’s earnings call hints at a bigger role for CrowdStrike in securing AI agents.

By Patrick Sanders – Nov 23, 2026 at 10:17PM EST

10 stocks we like better than CrowdStrike ›

With all the buzz surrounding Nvidia's (NVDA +31.48%) earnings report on Nov. 17 and the turmoil brought on by Jensen Huang's resignation and reinstatement, you may have overlooked something interesting unrelated to financial results.

In the company's earnings call with analysts, CFO Colette Kress discussed Nvidia's plans to secure artificial intelligence (AI) agents, and one of the companies she mentioned was cybersecurity specialist CrowdStrike (CRWD -8.92%).

That's a name familiar to Nvidia investors. In September, Huang called CrowdStrike Nvidia’s top cybersecurity partner. The companies were already collaborating on AI-powered security software.

Now that relationship is getting bigger. Nvidia is putting security controls directly into the computing infrastructure that runs AI agents. And CrowdStrike is helping to provide the protection.

AI agents are often much more powerful than the chatbots most people are familiar with, and they're getting more powerful every day. Thanks to agentic AI, agents can perform tasks, access databases, execute software, and interact with other systems.

That's useful if you want your agent to help you create a shopping list, schedule a doctor's appointment, or order a prescription refill.

But there are also obvious security concerns right now, as the U.S. has been hit with a variety of computer-related outages affecting banks, the airline industry, and government services. AI agents can be subverted to do harmful things, such as access confidential records or sensitive company data.

Nvidia wants to prevent that behavior before it causes damage. In October, the company introduced its OpenShell Reference Architecture, which combines software controls with security enforcement running on Nvidia's BlueField-4 data processing units.

OpenShell limits which files and tools an agent can access. Nvidia Sentry, running on BlueField-4, independently monitors agent behavior and enforces security policies at the hardware level. Nvidia says that Sentry can quarantine and stop an agent that attempts to operate outside its permitted boundaries in milliseconds.

Kress emphasized the technology during the recent earnings call and identified CrowdStrike among the companies working with Nvidia on OpenShell. It's part of a relationship between Nvidia and CrowdStrike that is developing rapidly.

In September, CrowdStrike introduced SafeMind, an AI cybersecurity system built using Nvidia's Nemotron models. By using offensive and defensive AI models to identify vulnerabilities and improve protection, SafeMind achieved a 29% higher detection rate and six times faster remediation than the comparison models in its evaluations, CrowdStrike said.

Then in October, CrowdStrike announced it was integrating Falcon Guardian, its AI detection and response product, with Nvidia OpenShell. It also announced plans to integrate with Nvidia Sentry on BlueField-4.

Nvidia's hardware provides another layer of enforcement -- CrowdStrike can supply the intelligence needed to recognize threats, while Nvidia's infrastructure can help enforce restrictions.

Businesses need cybersecurity protection for their employees, devices, cloud applications, and networks. And as they develop and deploy autonomous agents, those agents create additional activity that security teams need to monitor.

For example, an employee might interact with a handful of applications during the workday. An autonomous agent could execute hundreds of tasks, access multiple databases, and communicate with other agents without requiring constant human involvement.

That's a more efficient way to work, but it also creates a potentially enormous amount of activity for security companies to monitor to make sure someone isn't misusing an AI agent, or that the AI agent hasn't gone rogue.

CrowdStrike isn't simply using Nvidia's technology to improve its existing products anymore. It's working to become part of how Nvidia-powered AI agents are secured in the first place -- and, in doing so, to cement the company's position as one of Nvidia's leading security partners.

#2936. NASA Is Handing Out Moon Contracts Again. Intuitive Machines, Firefly, and Redwire All Got Paid. Only 1 Has the Balance Sheet to Keep Winning.

Not all NASA contractors are equally positioned to succeed.

By Jeff Siegel – Nov 23, 2026 at 10:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Firefly Aerospace ›

NASA is investing more in its lunar exploration program, and three publicly traded space companies in particular are positioned to benefit: Intuitive Machines (LUNR +11.11%), Firefly Aerospace (FLY +8.20%), and Redwire(RDW +13.63%).

On Nov. 7, NASA announced a new round of awards supporting its planned moon base, including lunar transportation, communications, and power infrastructure. All three companies have secured positions in NASA's expanding lunar program, but their financial situations are quite different. Let's take a closer look.

NASA recently selected eight companies, including Firefly and Intuitive Machines, to compete for contracts under its next commercial lunar payload services program, known as CLPS 2. The program has a maximum value of $10 billion over 10 years. NASA also increased the ceiling of its existing CLPS program from $2.6 billion to $4.2 billion.

Redwire was selected to provide power-generation technology for NASA's planned mini-communications and solar tower, which will support operations on the lunar surface. The value of that award wasn't disclosed.

These announcements follow approximately $590 million in lunar delivery contracts announced in June, including $144.2 million for Firefly and $148.3 million for Intuitive Machines. Of course, the $10 billion CLPS 2 figure represents a spending ceiling, not guaranteed revenue for participating companies. Individual contracts will still need to be awarded.

Intuitive Machines has been growing quickly. In Q3 2026, revenue reached $269.3 million, up from $52.4 million a year earlier. The company also generated $66.2 million in operating cash flow and approximately $33.5 million in free cash flow. It also finished September with $463.8 million in cash.

Those are encouraging numbers, especially for a company operating in an industry where developing spacecraft and launching missions can quickly burn through hundreds of millions of dollars. However, Intuitive Machines carries $336.9 million in long-term debt. That's a substantial obligation for a business still expanding its operations and investing in new technology.

Redwire manufactures spacecraft components, solar arrays, and other technologies used in space infrastructure.

In Q3 2026, revenue reached $109 million, an increase of 5.4% from the previous year. Contracted backlog climbed to a record $619.7 million, and the company also reported $488.9 million in cash, cash equivalents, and restricted cash against approximately $47 million in debt. That's a considerable improvement in its financial position.

But Redwire remains unprofitable. It reported a $23.2 million net loss in Q3 and an adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) loss of $5.3 million. Indeed, its growing backlog is encouraging, but converting those contracts into profitable operations remains a challenge.

Firefly's latest financial results make a compelling case for why it could be the strongest of these three companies. At the end of September, Firefly held $534.7 million in cash and short-term investments, compared with just $25.1 million in financial debt. That works out to approximately $510 million in net cash.

Firefly is also growing quickly. Third-quarter revenue jumped 259% year over year to $110.7 million, while backlog reached a record $1.7 billion. And unlike many companies competing for NASA contracts, Firefly has already demonstrated its ability to land a spacecraft on the Moon.

In March 2025, its Blue Ghost lander completed the first fully successful commercial lunar landing. Firefly is now preparing additional lunar missions while expanding its launch and national security operations. That gives it an established track record, a substantial backlog, and considerable financial resources to pursue additional contracts.

To be sure, Firefly does have a history of operating losses and negative operating cash flow. Its cash and short-term investments also declined from approximately $893 million at the end of 2025 to $535 million in September 2026.

Developing launch vehicles and spacecraft isn't cheap, and future missions will require substantial investment. And there's also no guarantee Firefly will secure a meaningful portion of NASA's new $10 billion program. Still, Firefly has the largest net cash position among these three companies, a $1.7 billion backlog, and a successful lunar landing already under its belt.

Intuitive Machines and Redwire have legitimate opportunities to benefit from NASA's expanding lunar ambitions. But based on their latest financial results, Firefly looks best positioned to compete for additional contracts without taking on substantial new debt.

That doesn't automatically make it the best stock to buy. Valuation and execution still count. But if I were choosing one of these three companies based on financial strength and its ability to pursue future lunar contracts, Firefly would be my pick.

#2933. A Stock Market Crash Could Be Life-Changing for Investors Who Own These 3 Artificial Intelligence (AI) Stocks

Lemmings is a term often used on Wall Street, and a stock market crash could show why it is important to avoid joining the crowd.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 23, 2026 at 11:15PM EST

Market volatility has ticked higher, with artificial intelligence (AI) stocks getting particularly hard hit of late. For example, Aehr Test Systems (AEHR +21.76%) and MongoDB (MDB +9.37%) are down 60% and 30%, respectively, over the past three months, as of this writing. Cisco Systems (CSCO +6.80%), a tech industry giant, is down about 18%. What's going on? There are two things happening here, and both could leave investors with long-term scars. Here's why these three AI stocks could be life-changing investments, but perhaps not in a good way.

Artificial intelligence as a technology is very likely to change the world. However, that doesn't mean every AI stock will be a long-term winner. You only have to look back at past technological revolutions to see the trend. Companies pile into a new technology, jockeying for a leadership position. Eventually, a small number of industry leaders emerge, and the other companies fall to the wayside.

Image source: OpenAI (AI-generated illustration).

Along the way, there are usually big swings on Wall Street. Every stock associated with the new technology gets investor attention early on, with many rising to shockingly high valuations. When it is clear that companies can't live up to investors' expectations, the entire group usually falls in unison. If the technology is big enough and Wall Street's enthusiasm great enough, the industry downturn can lead to a broader bear market.

Cisco is actually a poster child for this very dynamic. During the dot-com bubble, the stock rose dramatically. When that bubble burst, its stock fell dramatically. It took around 20 years for Cisco's shares to recover from that decline. If you had owned Cisco at the peak of the dot-com bubble, your entire thought process around investing probably changed. Some investors who got burned during that period may have even decided to stop investing altogether.

As it turned out, the internet did change the world. However, the key story is that the technology didn't make every dot-com stock a good investment. While Cisco survived, many internet stocks did not. And this is where the risk of an AI bubble comes in, since AI is the technology dejur. Cisco is again in play, having leaned into the sector by building data centers. Its stock rallied sharply because of this involvement, but it has also pulled back sharply. And the 18% decline over the last three months is modest compared to the drops in Aehr Test Systems and MongoDB. Aehr Test Systems provides testing equipment for computer chips. MongoDB makes database software. Like Cisco, they are both involved in AI.

However, the bigger issue is that even after the huge price declines, Aehr Test Systems has a price-to-earnings ratio of 890x, while MongoDB's P/E is 400x. Cisco Systems' P/E actually looks fairly attractive at 24x, but that's above its five-year average of 20x, and its price-to-sales and price-to-book ratios are also above their five-year averages, as well. In other words, even after the declines experienced so far, all three of these stocks could still have more room to fall.

It is entirely possible that AI stocks pull back and then head higher again, without the often disastrous industry shakeouts that have happened to new technologies in the past. History suggests that won't be the case, and eventually many investors will lose money by following the crowd, like lemmings, into expensive AI-related technology stocks.

For newer investors who haven't experienced a deep, prolonged bear market, it could be a life-changing event that leads them to abandon investing. Do not underestimate how emotionally devastating it can be to own stocks when an investment bubble bursts; consider diversifying now if you are heavily overweighted in AI stocks. Investing, despite bear markets, is still one of the most powerful wealth-building tools you have if you stick to it for the long term.

#2929. Innodata vs. IonQ: Is the Artificial Intelligence or Quantum Computing Stock the Better Buy for 2027?

Innodata's revenue grew nearly 48% last year, while IonQ's quantum hardware sales surged even faster, though its losses tell a very different story.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 24, 2026 at 12:02AM EST

Choosing between a data-focused AI provider and a quantum computing pioneer represents two very different paths for your portfolio. Innodata (INOD +14.54%) and IonQ (IONQ +12.65%) both operate at the cutting edge of innovation.

Innodata focuses on the data engineering required to fuel artificial intelligence, while IonQ builds the actual quantum hardware that could redefine processing power. Comparing these two helps highlight the trade-off between established AI software services and the high-stakes potential of quantum hardware in the tech stocks sector.

Innodata is a global data engineering enterprise that provides human expertise and evaluation frameworks to help businesses build and adopt generative AI solutions. The company serves five of the "Magnificent Seven" tech giants and various AI research labs. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, as one specific customer accounted for approximately 58% of total revenue in 2025.

In its 2025 fiscal year (FY), revenue reached $251.7 million, representing growth of 47.6% over the previous year. The company also reported a net income of $32.2 million, resulting in a net margin of 12.8%.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio stood at 2.7x, which measures a company's ability to cover short-term debts with its short-term assets. The debt-to-equity ratio, which compares total debt to shareholder equity, was zero, meaning it had no debt on its balance sheet.

Free cash flow, which is cash from operations minus capital expenditures, was $35.6 million. Note that stock-based compensation (SBC) represented 23.8% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

IonQ develops and delivers hardware solutions across quantum computing, networking, and sensing. It offers its technology through a quantum-computing-as-a-service model, partnering with major cloud platforms including Amazon (AMZN +3.47%), Microsoft (MSFT +1.65%), and Alphabet (GOOGL +6.34%) (GOOG +5.88%). These partnerships allow researchers and enterprises to access quantum processing power through familiar infrastructure.

In FY 2025, IonQ reported revenue of $130.0 million, which reflects a year-over-year growth rate of 201.9%. Despite this rapid expansion, the company is still in its early stages of financial development. It reported a net loss of $510.4 million for the year, resulting in a negative net margin of 392.6%.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio was 15.5x, indicating a strong position for meeting short-term obligations. Its debt-to-equity ratio was zero, showing no reliance on traditional debt for its operations. Free cash flow for the period was negative $299.6 million, as the company continues to invest heavily in the development of its quantum technology.

Innodata faces risks from its heavy customer concentration, although it is working to expand its clients. The company also deals with legal and regulatory challenges, including an ongoing securities class action and potential liability from a 2008 Philippine labor judgment. Additionally, it must navigate intense competition from large tech companies and operational risks associated with its facilities in the Philippines, India, and Sri Lanka.

IonQ is an early-stage company with a history of significant operating losses and an unproven business model. It faces operational risks related to achieving scalable quantum capabilities in a nascent technology and the integration of numerous acquisitions. Furthermore, the company depends on government contracts and faces intense global competition from well-capitalized incumbents like Alphabet and Microsoft, which are also developing quantum computers.

Innodata appears significantly more affordable for investors based on its P/S ratio multiple while IonQ does not have a Forward P/E ratio because it is not expected to be profitable any time soon.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Innodata offers immediate, profitable exposure to the commercial artificial intelligence boom, while IonQ represents a higher-risk, long-term bet on pure-play quantum computing. Both are tackling innovative technologies, but between the two, I would invest in Innodata.

For starters, Innodata's share price valuation is far more attractive. Moreover, it's positioning its business for increased resilience in 2027 by targeting physical AI, such as humanoid robots. Training data for robotics models requires custom video and movement information that is hard to come by, so Innodata is using motion capture to help robots learn to move like humans.

Innodata is also a profitable business growing rapidly. In the third quarter of 2026, it delivered 53% year-over-year revenue growth to $95.6 million, and net income of $10.3 million.

Although IonQ's revenue is rising quickly, the company remains deeply unprofitable. Its technology has yet to achieve commercial adoption as its quantum computers have not reached quantum advantage, the stage where a quantum machine is more efficient and cost-effective than a classical computer. It could be years before that happens, while Innodata is already delivering strong results.

#2924. Not Starlink. Not Starship. This SpaceX Business Could Be Worth $80 Billion on Its Own.

Space Exploration Technologies' fastest-growing business doesn't fly, orbit, or beam internet, and it might be worth $80 billion on its own.

By Micah Zimmerman – Nov 24, 2026 at 12:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

The best tenant I could ever imagine having is the one that paid on the first of every month without a reminder. Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +4.35%) has one that pays $1.25 billion a month.

That tenant is Anthropic (ANT -1.43%), which signed cloud services agreements in May to use about 325,000 Nvidia GPUs across SpaceX's Colossus and Colossus II data centers, at $1.25 billion per month through May 2029. A few weeks later, Alphabet's Google signed up for roughly 110,000 GPUs at $920 million a month from October 2026 through June 2029.

That first tenant also just got a lot richer. Anthropic closed its IPO on Nov. 20, with about $91.6 billion of the proceeds going to the company. A tenant with that much cash is exactly the kind a landlord hopes for.

Rockets get the headlines. Rent is quietly becoming one of the most valuable things SpaceX sells.

SpaceX's Colossus clusters train its own Grok models, and in the second quarter of 2026, the company began renting capacity to outside customers. By the third quarter, SI solutions and infrastructure revenue had jumped to $7 billion, and even excluding the newly acquired Cursor, it grew 192% sequentially, "driven primarily by the activation of new cloud services agreements," CFO Bret Johnsen said.

"The industry continues to demand ever more massive amounts of compute," president Gwynne Shotwell told investors, while Johnsen added that "at current pricing, new compute deployments are generating a payback of less than a year." A landlord who earns back the cost of the building in under a year is running a very good business.

Elon Musk was blunter, saying, "we could rent out our compute several times over if we said yes to everyone," and, after noting SpaceX ended September with 1.7 gigawatts of capacity, "I would be surprised if we brought on less than 13 gigawatts of compute by the end of next year." More buildings, in other words, and a waiting list of tenants.

Here's how I get to $80 billion. The closest public comparison is CoreWeave, a pure-play AI cloud that expects to exit 2026 at an annualized revenue run rate of $20 billion to $21 billion. Its shares have fallen 55% in three months, leaving it with a market cap of about $22 billion, plus about $47 billion of debt against $4 billion of cash.

The two disclosed SpaceX contracts total $2.17 billion a month. That's a conservative enterprise value because it ignores every other cloud customer and uses a multiple from one of the most hated stocks in cloud computing right now.

It also explains why the business gets overlooked. SpaceX's market cap is about $1.74 trillion, and the stock has dropped about 20% in the past month.

The risks are real, and they live in the fine print. SpaceX's cloud contracts can typically be terminated by either party on 90 days' notice, and Musk likes it that way, saying "our approach is to rent at the -- effectively the spot market rate as opposed to sign these long-term deals." That works beautifully while compute is scarce, but rent could reset quickly if the AI spending boom cools.

Concentration matters, too. A single SI customer accounted for 34.5% of SpaceX's total third-quarter revenue. If that customer walked, the rent roll would look very different.

The build-out isn't cheap, either. SpaceX has a commitment with an Apollo-managed entity for GPU finance leases expected to total about $40 billion. That's a lot of money tied to chips that could look dated quickly once newer, faster ones arrive, and a landlord still owes the mortgage even when a tenant leaves.

Still, I keep coming back to that first-of-the-month check. While investors argue over Starship's next flight, SpaceX is already collecting rent, and in a market this nervous about AI, cash coming in every month is worth more than another promise.
