#2918. Cybersecurity Practices That Can Create A False Sense Of Security

Organizations invest significant time and resources in cybersecurity measures designed to protect their systems, data and operations. However, even practices that seem responsible can create a false sense of security—especially when they’re treated as substitutes for more comprehensive protection and ongoing risk assessment.

For tech leaders, the challenge is recognizing when established security practices are effectively reducing risk and when they may be simply creating the appearance of preparedness. Here, Forbes Technology Council members highlight common cybersecurity practices and beliefs that can give organizations misplaced confidence and explain what to do instead.

“We have 24/7 monitoring” is not the same as, “We will catch an issue at 2 a.m.” This is truly a false sense of security unless your teams have fine-tuned alerts very well over time for your situations and context. That means TP/FP are identified, and actual signals are not lost in the high-volume alert noise. To test, inject a true alert at an inconvenient hour and see if ops is detecting it and acting upon it. Monitoring is mostly a comfort unless you make it a security control. - Pradeep Sandhu, Cywarden, Inc.

The fundamental assumption behind traditional cybersecurity is that all the technology in your security stack will behave exactly as designed, requiring little oversight. This could not be further from the truth. Constant governance and visibility into your security system is no longer an option. It is a necessity that can’t be overlooked or underestimated, and it’s a critical issue that must be addressed, especially as we navigate the uncharted territory of AI. - Kevin Lynch, Optiv

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Prioritizing security by a generic risk model describes the average organization, which does not exist. One bank had 362 vulnerabilities under attack in its network that global data never saw. A score trained on everyone is a statement about no one. - Michael Roytman, Empirical Security

As a CISO, I’ve sat through HiTrust, SOC 2 and PCI audits, and I value them. But a clean report is a snapshot of scoped controls and evidence chosen for an auditor to see, not proof we’d stop a real attacker. The danger is when “we passed” becomes the finish line and the board sees a green light. I treat audits as the floor, then measure what matters: real prevention, detection and remediation speed. - Lane Sullivan, Concentric AI

Many organizations mistake end-to-end encryption for security. Encryption answers one question: Can someone read the message in transit? It says nothing about who is on the other end or whether a record survives. Further, the same encryption that keeps attackers out can end up keeping the firm out, too. Security means governing the conversation at the source, with verified identities, managed access and a record the firm owns and can act on. - Dima Gutzeit, LeapXpert

Treating an approved business application as permanently trusted can create unchecked risk. Vendors now add AI agents and automation to software enterprises already use, giving new capabilities access to existing data, permissions and workflows. Security teams need to reassess what those applications can do as features change, not rely on the risk profile established when they were procured. - Ofer Klein, Reco

Software from an approved vendor, known repository, signed package or internal developer can still contain malicious or unintended behavior. Source and provenance are important, but they do not show what the finished code will actually do. Organizations should verify behavior before execution, not inherit trust from where the code came from. - Ken Ammon, CodeHunter

Early in my career, the problem was, “We don’t know what we have.” Today, most teams have dashboards full of alerts and findings, but visibility doesn’t equal security. If the data from your environment is not contextualized into actionable, cross-domain insights and proactive remediation workflows, you’ve just documented your exposure in more detail rather than successfully mitigating it. - Sivan Tehila, Onyxia Cyber

A common misconception is that if an email passes through a security gateway, it must be safe. While traditional gateways catch known threats, they’re less effective against AI-driven attacks that don’t rely on malicious links or attachments. These newer attacks look and sound legitimate, making them harder to spot. That’s why organizations must pay attention to communication patterns. - Eyal Benishti, IRONSCALES

A green dashboard does not truly mean your business is secure. No alerts may signal gaps in visibility, not an absence of threats. Organizations need security that connects signals across their environment, backed by experts who monitor, investigate and respond 24/7. Regularly test your defenses to confirm teams can detect suspicious activity and act quickly to contain threats before they do harm. - Neal Bradbury, Barracuda

One of the biggest misconceptions about open-source security is that finding vulnerabilities means you’re securing your software. Scanners and AI agents can flag thousands of risks, but without a clear remediation path, they create backlogs instead of protection. For end-of-life open-source software, the stakes are even higher. When official patches stop, vulnerabilities don’t. - Aaron Mitchell, HeroDevs

A dangerous belief is that more security tools automatically mean better security. In reality, too many disconnected tools can create blind spots, duplicate alerts and unclear ownership. A mature security posture is not defined by how much technology has been purchased but by how quickly the organization can detect, understand and contain a real threat. - Dr. Chiranjiv Roy, C5i.ai

Vendor security questionnaires feel thorough, but they capture a one-time self-assessment from direct suppliers and say little about the vendors behind them. Risk changes constantly and often sits several tiers deep, so treat these questionnaires as a starting point, then pair them with continuous monitoring of the extended sub-tier supplier network for proactive supplier risk mitigation. - Yardley Pohl, interos.ai

Poorly executed phishing tests can do far more to damage employee trust than they will ever benefit an organization’s cybersecurity capability. Activities that penalize individuals, don’t result in meaningful and engaged education, or become so frequent that they annoy and create resentment do little to strengthen your cyber defenses. - Kim Bozzella, Protiviti

I think relying too heavily on periodic security audits can create a false sense of confidence. They’re valuable, but technology changes quickly, especially as teams introduce new tools and AI into their workflows. I’ve learned that it’s important to keep reviewing how systems are actually being used, who has access and where new risks may be developing between audits. - Deniz Gezgin, Eightpoint

Mandatory password rotation is often mistaken for effective cybersecurity. Requiring employees to change passwords every 60 to 90 days can encourage predictable variations and credential reuse, potentially increasing security risks. Organizations should prioritize phishing-resistant multifactor authentication, compromised-credential detection and risk-based access controls over routine password expiration. - Gouri Sankar Dash, Tata Consultancy Services

Treating successful backups as proof of recovery can create false confidence. A backup job can finish successfully while a full restoration still fails or takes too long. Test recovery of a complete business workflow in an isolated environment, including its dependencies, and measure the time until people can actually use it. - Shahen Minasyan, Twin Peaks Inc. (TPI)

Access and authentication controls are paramount to any internal system, but with AI agents now working alongside humans, impersonation is becoming a growing threat. These tools often rely on being able to access the same files and systems you can access. It may be productive, but left unattended, it’s risky. Building in systemic limitations to what AI agents can do on your behalf is an important consideration. - Matt Wielbut, Openly

→ Baca artikel asli

#2916. Nvidia Stock Just Had Its Best Day in Over a Decade. I'd Still Buy It Now.

The AI chip giant's shares climbed around a third in one session. Is it still a bargain?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 24, 2026 at 12:43AM EST

Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%) said on Monday, Nov. 23, that it had brought back Jensen Huang as president and CEO, effective immediately. Shares surged on the news.

As I write, the stock's up about 34% for the day, at roughly $168. That'd be its largest one-day climb in over two decades, based on Yahoo Finance's daily closing prices. It'd also add about $1 trillion to Nvidia's market value in one session.

But the stock sits around 30% below Oct. 6, when it closed at a record $239.24. And I think Nvidia is still cheap for what it's expected to earn, especially with Huang back and sales rising faster than a year ago.

Huang stepped down on Nov. 11, after what Nvidia's board called personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct. Colette Kress, Nvidia's longtime chief financial officer, became interim CEO, and the board started looking for a permanent replacement.

Six days later, when Nvidia posted fiscal third-quarter results on Nov. 17, board chair Harvey Jones opened the earnings call by ruling out a return.

"The NVIDIA Board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO," Jones said.

The selling kept going. Shares lost around 28% between the Nov. 11 close and Friday, Nov. 20, when the stock dropped 14% in one day. This was Nvidia's seventh straight losing week, its longest weekly losing streak since 2008.

Five days after the call, the board reversed itself. Nvidia said its directors voted unanimously to reinstate Huang after a series of meetings with him. The board also said it got input from senior levels of the U.S. government before deciding.

Sure, not everything is settled. A committee of independent directors is still investigating, and Nvidia said it'll keep cooperating with ongoing government investigations and inquiries.

Highlighting how strong demand stayed through all this, Nvidia's revenue surged 103% year over year to $115.8 billion in its fiscal 2027 third quarter, which ended on Oct. 25, 2026. Sales grew 20% from the previous quarter, a pickup from 18% sequential growth in fiscal Q2. What's more, earnings per share on a non-GAAP (adjusted) basis gained 111% year over year to $2.68, so profits more than kept up with sales.

Management sees around $130 billion in revenue for fiscal Q4, a period with an extra week. Kress also said on the call that Vera Rubin, Nvidia's latest computing platform, pulled in almost $20 billion in its first quarter of production shipments. And she lifted Nvidia's outlook for next year, saying the company now expects fiscal 2028 revenue (the year ending in January 2028) to rise around 75%, up from its previous 70% forecast.

In other words, the stock's drop after Huang's resignation was arguably about who would lead Nvidia, not about demand for its chips. With Huang back, much of that worry could fade.

At roughly $168, Nvidia's stock price is about 10 times expected fiscal 2028 earnings, based on analysts' average estimate.

In comparison, shares of Advanced Micro Devices, one of Nvidia's top rivals in AI chips, cost about 26 times the company's expected 2027 earnings. Even after Monday's rally, then, investors are paying less than half as much for a dollar of Nvidia's expected profit.

Nvidia is also buying back lots of its own stock. It returned $28.1 billion to shareholders in repurchases and dividends in fiscal Q3. And Kress said it still plans to spend the $227 billion or so left on its buyback authorization by the end of fiscal 2028 -- over 5% of Nvidia's market value at Monday's price.

Yes, a price-to-earnings ratio this low only holds up if the earnings forecast does. Nvidia sees gross margin of 71% to 72% in fiscal Q4, down from around 75% in fiscal Q3. And the board's investigation might still find something investors don't like. But at around 10 times expected fiscal 2028 earnings, I'd say the stock already prices in a lot of bad news.

In the end, Nvidia gets back the co-founder who ran it for more than three decades, and it gets him back with sales still more than doubling year over year. I'd still buy the stock after Monday's jump, but I'd likely add shares bit by bit over the next few months, given how volatile the stock is. There’s no guarantee it doesn’t go lower again.

#2914. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2912. Prediction: 1 "Magnificent Seven" Stock That Will Lag the Market in 2027

Tesla is building some of the most exciting technology on Earth, but I think its stock is set up to trail the market next year.

By Micah Zimmerman – Nov 24, 2026 at 1:00AM EST

I've learned the hard way that a company can do amazing things and still be a frustrating stock to own for a year or two. That's exactly how I feel about Tesla (TSLA +11.46%) heading into 2027.

Tesla is already the laggard of the "Magnificent Seven" this year. Through Nov. 4, its stock was down about 14% in 2026, while the S&P 500 was up about 12%. This week didn't help. Shares were down roughly 6% intraday on Nov. 20, trading around $351.

To be clear, I'm not predicting a collapse. I'm predicting that Tesla will trail the S&P 500 next year. I don't think 2027 will fix what's holding it back, and here's why.

Tesla's core business is stalling. Last quarter, automotive revenue fell 3%, energy revenue fell 16%, and net income dropped 29% as spending on Optimus robots and AI surged. Tesla blamed lower average selling prices and tight battery supply, and it's facing tougher competition from Chinese EV makers like BYD and Xiaomi in Asia and Europe. Third-quarter deliveries slipped about 2% from a year ago, and the loss of the federal EV tax credit still stings.

To be fair, demand for the cars is real. CFO Vaibhav Taneja said Tesla "exited the quarter with our highest backlog in four years, with deliveries constrained primarily by production." Musk said Megapacks, Tesla's big batteries for the grid and data centers, are nearly sold out for 2027, and active Full Self-Driving subscriptions jumped 67% from a year earlier to 1.74 million.

The catch is the bill. Taneja warned that rising operating costs "would continue till we commercialize these new products," and that "commodity price increases and interest rate changes continue to add to our cost and create further headwinds." Musk said capital spending "will start to become more meaningful next year and then noticeable like in the tens of billions in '28."

Tesla's bull case is robotaxis and Optimus, and I'll admit the progress is impressive. Unsupervised robotaxi miles grew eightfold last quarter, and Cybercab rides started in Austin in September. Regulators are watching closely, though. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration opened an audit into how Tesla certified the Cybercab's compliance with federal safety standards.

But the timing matters for 2027. On robotaxis, Musk said, "I think it starts to become noticeable probably in Q2 and then probably a big deal by Q3 or Q4 of next year." On Optimus, he said there's "still quite a lot of work to do with regard to the AI and the robot for it to be truly useful for open-ended tasks."

Waiting is expensive at this price. Tesla trades at more than 400 times trailing earnings, so its price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio leaves almost no margin of safety if a launch slips.

This week showed how fragile AI-heavy stocks are right now. Jensen Huang's ouster at Nvidia, doubts about AI spending, and the threat of more Federal Reserve rate hikes all hit tech hard. Tesla sells itself as an AI and robotics company, so it wears those same worries.

There's one big thing that could prove me wrong. On Wednesday, Musk said Tesla and SpaceX would "probably" be better together, citing "so many areas of convergence." A merger could excite Musk's fans and send the stock soaring.

But it's far from certain. Musk has said a deal would need "a 50% vote of all shares, not just those who vote, but of all shareholders, and that's quite a high hurdle to try to achieve."

Tesla could still be a fine company to own for a decade, with its huge market cap backed by real technology. But for 2027, the gap between what Tesla is earning and what the stock is pricing in looks too wide to close in 12 months. That's why I think it lags.

#2905. Nvidia Has Incredible News for Artificial Intelligence (AI) Investors. Here Is My Top Stock to Buy Before It Is Too Late

The massive cloud backlog that Nvidia noted on its latest earnings call bodes well for this incredibly cheap AI infrastructure stock.

By Harsh Chauhan – Nov 24, 2026 at 2:07AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Dell Technologies ›

Artificial intelligence (AI) bellwether Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%) released results for the third quarter of fiscal 2027 on Nov. 17, and the company's numbers and guidance clearly suggest that the AI build-out will continue at an impressive pace.

Nvidia's revenue increased by 103% year over year to $115.8 billion, driven primarily by a 110% increase in data center revenue to $107.6 billion. Even better, Nvidia increased its fiscal 2028 revenue growth estimate from 70% to 75%. The company noted that demand for its chips is outstripping supply and said the trend could continue due to a huge backlog cloud computing providers are sitting on.

Specifically, Nvidia says that the cloud industry's backlog increased by $500 billion sequentially in the previous quarter to a whopping $2.5 trillion. As a result, Nvidia sees a "significant multiyear opportunity" for itself and its partners. That's why we will take a closer look at the prospects of Dell Technologies (DELL +20.46%), an Nvidia partner that could win big from the $2.5 trillion cloud backlog.

Dell Technologies' infrastructure solutions group (ISG) has been growing at an incredible pace, driven by phenomenal demand for the company's AI-optimized servers. Dell's ISG revenue increased by an impressive 89% year over year in the second quarter of fiscal 2027 to $31.8 billion. Its revenue from AI servers doubled year over year to $16.4 billion. What's more, traditional servers and networking revenue increased 122% from the year-ago period to $10.5 billion.

Dell's servers are used to mount chips produced by Nvidia. In fact, Dell claims that it was the first company to ship Nvidia's Vera Rubin chip systems. So, the company is a direct beneficiary of Nvidia's impressive growth. Also, Dell's server rack solutions are the backbone of AI data centers, helping hyperscalers and AI companies integrate a range of chips and cooling systems to run AI workloads seamlessly.

This puts Dell in a solid position to capitalize on the $2.5 trillion backlog mentioned earlier. Not surprisingly, the AI server market that Dell serves is poised for a big boom next year. Gartner estimates that AI server spending could surge by 81% in 2027 to $957 billion. Importantly, Dell already has a strong enough backlog that should help it capitalize on this massive opportunity.

The company's AI server backlog was $95 billion at the end of fiscal Q2. That should be enough for Dell to sustain its stunning AI revenue growth next year. Dell anticipates a 3x increase in AI server revenue to $74 billion in fiscal 2027. Its backlog already points to a nice improvement next year, though don't be surprised to see Dell's AI server revenue multiplying once again.

That's because Dell management says that its order pipeline is in "multiples of our backlog." As such, I won't be surprised to see Dell's revenue growth outpace analysts' expectations in fiscal 2028 (ending in January 2028). Specifically, analysts expect Dell's revenue to increase by 71% in fiscal 2027 to $194.3 billion, followed by a 20% increase in fiscal 2028 to $232.6 billion.

However, as the chart below shows, Dell's revenue estimate for fiscal 2028 has been rising, and I won't be surprised to see the trend continue.

DELL Revenue Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

Dell stock can be bought at an incredibly cheap valuation right now. The stock is trading at just 19 times earnings, which is incredibly cheap considering that its earnings per share are predicted to increase by 151% in the current fiscal year. What's more, the stock has a price-to-sales ratio of just 1.5, well below the tech-focused Nasdaq Composite index's price-to-sales ratio of 4.5.

These multiples clearly indicate that Dell is undervalued, and that makes this AI stock a screaming buy. For instance, if Dell's revenue increases to $233 billion in fiscal 2028 and it trades at even 2 times sales, its market cap will reach $466 billion. That's 119% higher than Dell's current market cap, suggesting that investors have the opportunity to buy a potential multibagger at dirt cheap levels.

#2898. Black Friday Comes to Tech Stocks: 3 Artificial Intelligence (AI) Names I’d Buy on Sale

Micron, Nvidia, and Broadcom have all been thrown in the bargain bin ahead of Black Friday.

By Geoffrey Seiler – Nov 24, 2026 at 2:34AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Micron Technology ›

Black Friday is right around the corner, and the market has been gracious enough to put a slew of tech stocks on sale before the holiday shopping season. With numerous bargains to choose from, these are the three tech stocks I'd be loading up on right now.

The market clearly does not believe in the Micron Technology (MU +19.04%) story, with the stock now trading at a forward P/E of under 3.5. The company has been delivering incredible growth, fueled by higher memory prices.

However, investors are clearly nervous that the memory market is about to peak, and the industry has a history of brutal downturns when this happens. That said, this time does truly look different. While the industry hasn't entirely lost its cyclicality, this cycle is also being fueled by a powerful AI build-out with secular tailwinds.

AI chips need to be packaged with a special form of DRAM (dynamic random access memory) called high bandwidth memory (HBM) that reduces latency and optimizes performance. HBM growth has been in lockstep with AI accelerator demand, but supply is struggling to keep up due to a dearth of extreme ultraviolet (EUV) lithography machines that are used to make both advanced logic chips and HBM. At the same time, HBM also requires three times the wafer capacity as conventional DRAM.

This dynamic will likely keep DRAM prices higher for much longer than your typical cycle, while Micron and others have locked up a significant share of their capacity in longer-term contracts. With cash piling up on its balance sheet and robust free cash flow, the stock is starting to look like a bargain.

Even after rallying following CEO Jensen Huang's return to the corner office on Monday, Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%)still trades at a forward P/E of just 10 times fiscal 2028 (ending January 2028) estimates from analysts. Huang played a huge role in Nvidia's success and his return puts the company in a good position moving forward.

Revenue is projected to grow a robust 75% next year, as the company maintains its leadership position. Importantly, Huang has transformed Nvidia from a simple maker of graphics processing units (GPUs) into a complete end-to-end AI infrastructure player. As such, it is now set to capture a much larger share of AI infrastructure spending per gigawatt of capacity for Vera Rubin customers than in past architecture generations. In addition, Nvidia has locked in a significant portion of future HBM supply and fab capacity, creating a wide supply-chain moat.

While Nvidia will lose some market share to ASICs and Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +6.85%), it still has a path to solid future growth and its future looks bright.

Broadcom (AVGO +3.00%) is another semiconductor stock that has fallen into the bargain bin ahead of Black Friday, trading at a forward P/E of just 11 times the fiscal 2028 (ending October) analyst consensus. This comes despite the company having some of the strongest growth prospects in the AI chip space.

Broadcom is the leader in ASIC technology, providing important IP (intellectual property) and services to help customers develop purpose-built, hardwired AI chips. It helped Alphabet (GOOGL +6.34%) (GOOG +5.88%) develop its highly successful Tensor Processing Units (TPUs), which are set to become Broadcom's biggest growth driver as it starts selling these custom chips to external customers. It's also helped OpenAI and Meta Platforms (META +6.55%) develop their own custom chips. Altogether, Broadcom has a clear line of sight to see its overall AI chip revenue quadruple from this past fiscal year to $230 billion in fiscal 2028.

In addition to its custom chip business, Broadcom is also a leader in data center networking, where its Tomahawk and Jericho chipsets hold strong positions in this fast-growing market. Combined, these two businesses should propel strong growth for the company in the coming years.

#2893. The Stock Market Just Entered a Correction. History Says Investors Who Make This 1 Move Right Now Will Win Out.

Stocks are plunging, but history says the right strategy can protect your portfolio.

By Katie Brockman – Nov 24, 2026 at 3:00AM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

It's been a tumultuous week for stocks. After many months of gains, the S&P 500 (^GSPC +4.73%), Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI +3.01%), and Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +6.51%) have all entered correction territory after falling more than 10% from their recent peaks.

It's a tough time to be an investor, and if you're feeling unnerved by all this volatility, you're not alone. The Fear and Greed Index, which measures investor sentiment on a scale from 0 to 100, currently sits at just 10 – firmly in the "extreme fear" category. The VIX Volatility Index (^VIX +0.60%) also reached its highest point since March 2025, signaling that investors expect more turbulence.

The promising news is that the market is no stranger to volatility, and history shows that one strategy stands above the rest when it comes to protecting your portfolio.

If history proves just one thing, it's that the best move investors can make is to stay in the market. That may sound counterintuitive as stock prices plunge. However, the market's short-term performance is unpredictable, and patient investors always win out.

The last several years have seen multiple instances of prices sinking only to rebound almost immediately. In March 2020, for example, the S&P 500 experienced its fastest crash in history after losing roughly one-third of its value in a matter of weeks. A few months later, it was reaching record highs.

Similar volatility occurred in April 2025 and March 2026, due to the "Liberation Day" tariffs and the beginning of the Iran war, respectively. In all three of these instances, many investors feared the worst and sold in a panic, only for prices to quickly bounce back.

Typically, the worst move investors can make is to sell after prices have already plunged. For one, that risks locking in losses by selling stocks for less than you paid for them. But also, if stocks immediately rebound, you may be forced to pay higher prices to reinvest in the stocks you just sold.

No two downturns are identical, so it's impossible to say whether the market will rebound just as quickly this time around. Volatility in the tech sector is the primary driver of this recent sell-off, and with persistent concerns about an AI bubble, there's always a chance stocks could continue to fall.

Even if that is the case – which, at this point, is still a big "if" – history still has good news for investors. The average bear market lasts around 340 days, according to analysis from Yardeni Research, and healthy stocks have always managed to come out the other side stronger than ever.

The last two decades have seen particularly brutal recessions, too. The dot-com bubble burst made history as the longest bear market on record, lasting 2.5 years, and the 2008 financial crisis was the most severe economic downturn since the Great Depression.

Yet since January 2000, the S&P 500 has earned total returns of close to 700%. In other words, if you'd invested $10,000 in an S&P 500 ETF or index fund immediately before two of the worst recessions in history, you'd have nearly $77,000 by today.

Since 1929, the S&P 500 has experienced 22 bear markets. That's roughly one bear market every four years, on average. Even if yet another historic downturn is coming, the market has proven time and again that it will bounce back – likely within a year or two.

The investors best positioned to reap the greatest rewards are those who avoid the temptation to sell at the first sign of trouble and instead use this opportunity to load up on quality stocks at discounted prices.

#2885. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2883. Tesla Stock Is on Track for Only Its 3rd Losing Year Since Its 2010 IPO. Here's What Happened After the Other 2.

Do the EV maker's past down years say anything about where the stock heads in 2027?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 24, 2026 at 3:34AM EST

Tesla (TSLA +11.46%) stock surged on Monday, Nov. 23, climbing with the broader market two days after the company started rolling out version 15 of its Full Self-Driving (Supervised) software to some users. Even so, at around $385 as of this writing, shares are about 14% under the $449.72 where they finished 2025.

The stock might still close the gap. But unless it rises about 17% before the year ends, 2026 will be only Tesla's third losing calendar year since it started trading in June 2010. (Its initial public offering was priced at $17 a share, and the stock ended that first year well above it.)

The other two were 2016, when shares dropped around 11%, and 2022, when they lost about 65%.

Both times, the next year was a good one for shareholders. Going by split-adjusted closing prices at the end of each calendar year, Tesla stock climbed around 46% in 2017 and about 102% in 2023.

That's an encouraging record. But two examples aren't a pattern, and I'd say what Tesla was like heading into those two rebounds counts more than the losing years themselves.

Tesla's business didn't exactly drive either recovery. In 2017, the year Model 3 deliveries started in July, sales surged 68% to $11.8 billion. But the net loss almost tripled, rising to $1.96 billion from $675 million in 2016.

2023 was similar in one way. Revenue climbed 19% to $96.8 billion, a big slowdown from 51% growth in 2022.

And operating income (business profit before interest and taxes) dropped 35% to $8.9 billion as price cuts on Tesla's vehicles squeezed its margins.

In other words, the stock rose in both years even as Tesla's operating results got worse.

Both started from a low price compared to sales. When 2016 ended, Tesla's market value was around $35 billion. Tesla took in $7 billion in revenue that year, up 73% over 2015, so shares traded at about 5 times sales. Six years later, the picture was much the same. At the end of 2022, Tesla was worth around $390 billion, or again about 5 times sales. Revenue that year was $81.5 billion, up 51%.

The 2022 reset is arguably the more striking of the two. Tesla's net income more than doubled that year to $12.6 billion. But the stock had started 2022 at over 200 times earnings, and it finished the year at around 34 times earnings.

In both cases, investors going into the next year were paying a modest price for a company whose sales were rising fast.

Tesla is headed toward 2027 with neither of those things going for it. Its revenue growth has stalled in recent months. Revenue climbed 16% year over year in the first three months of 2026 and 26% in the April-to-June period. In the third quarter, it was flat at around $28 billion.

Profits look even worse. Operating income came in at $718 million in the third quarter. That was better than the $398 million Tesla earned in the second quarter, though it was still 56% lower than a year earlier.

Over the last four reported quarters, net income totaled about $3.2 billion -- about a fourth of what Tesla earned in 2022.

The price, meanwhile, is far higher. At roughly $385 a share, Tesla trades at more than 13 times its sales over the last four quarters and more than 400 times earnings.

That price-to-sales ratio is about three times what it was when 2016 and 2022 ended.

To be fair, there's a story in play that looks like 2017. Then, investors were waiting on the Model 3. Now, they're waiting on Robotaxi and the self-driving software that just began rolling out, which might take years to show up in Tesla's profits. But the 2017 version of that story had a far lower price tag.

Two losing years are a small sample, of course, and Tesla stock can move fast, as Monday showed. Still, I'm doubtful that a losing 2026 would set the stock up like 2016 and 2022 did.

Both rebounds started from a price that seemed to assume little, but today's price assumes a lot. At over 400 times earnings, I still think Tesla stock is overvalued.

#2880. Jensen Huang Just Killed Hopes For a Fed Rate Pause. Will Kevin Warsh Crush the Stock Market Recovery on Dec. 9?

As the Jensen Huang saga comes to a close, investor attention could return to interest rates.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 24, 2026 at 3:45AM EST

Investors cheered as Jensen Huang returned to Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%), reinstated as CEO of the company he led for 33 years.

The Nasdaq Composite jumped 6.5%, and the S&P 500 gained 4.9% on Monday, as it seemed like the fears that crushed the stock market last week had suddenly vanished.

However, the return of Huang and the subsequent stock market surge bring other risks. With the stock market in free fall last week, investors were starting to hedge their bets that the Fed would raise rates at its next meeting on Dec. 9. According to the CME FedWatch Tool, bets that the Fed would hold interest rates steady grew last week as the market crashed, reaching an 11.4% chance of no change at the next meeting.

On Monday, after Huang's return, that probability fell to zero, and investors now see a 4.9% chance that the Fed hikes rates by 50 basis points. Some Fed Governors, like Christopher Waller, have argued for a 50-basis-point hike at the next meeting.

Fed Chair Kevin Warsh. Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.

The Fed has a dual mandate to maximize employment and keep prices stable, as reflected in its 2% inflation target.

In other words, the Fed isn't supposed to directly consider the stock market, but the stock market impacts both employment and inflation. It represents trillions of dollars of wealth, and when the stock market falls sharply, like it did last week, investors are less likely to spend money, and falling stock prices could put pressure on businesses as well, leading to layoffs.

The Fed monitors asset prices, including stock prices, but Fed Chair Kevin Warsh has made it clear that taming inflation is his top priority, as inflation has been above the Fed's target for five years.

Rate hikes tend to cool off the stock market as higher interest rates raise borrowing costs for businesses and also entice investors to rotate from stocks into bonds.

With expectations for a December rate hike now built in, the impact of a 25-basis-point hike should be mostly priced in to the market, though the probability of a rate hike could change after the October PCE report comes out on Wednesday.

Fed rate hikes, which are expected to continue into next year, are likely to exacerbate one of the biggest issues in the AI sector.

Concerns about rising yields on CoreWeave's bonds sparked the start of the sell-off in AI stocks last October, and that pressure remains. In fact, one of the issues surrounding Huang's departure was that he was behind many of the financial backstop arrangements that Nvidia had made with companies like CoreWeave. Those promises are propping up a significant portion of the AI sector, as Nvidia holds stakes in several AI stocks and start-ups and has complex financing arrangements with companies that buy its chips.

We don't know what the stock market will do in the two weeks between now and the Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting on Dec. 9, and if stocks continue to rally, the Fed will have more leeway to raise rates.

However, last week's plunge shows how jittery investors are and how fragile the AI boom has become, as prior to Huang's resignation, investors were already bailing on AI stocks, first over solvency concerns at CoreWeave and other neoclouds and then over the backlash against data centers in the midterm election.

Those issues haven't changed because Huang is back at Nvidia, though investors may be willing to put them aside for now.

If selling pressure returns to the AI sector, any rate hikes from the Fed will only add fuel to the fire, regardless of expectations or the impact on inflation.

#2877. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2875. SpaceX sued by Oregon and Virginia over AI deepfake porn, Grok safety claims

Lawsuits against Elon Musk's SpaceX are stacking up, with Virginia and Oregon becoming the first states to sue the company over issues with its Grok AI image generator.

Versions of Grok and SpaceX's social network X have enabled users to generate and distribute explicit deepfakes based on real photos or videos of children and other people who did not consent, according to research by the Internet Watch Foundation, the Center for Countering Digital Hate and others.

In separate complaints submitted to state courts as of Monday morning, Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield and Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones argued that SpaceX failed to put safeguards in place to stop Grok and X users from making and sharing harmful deepfake images, especially alleged AI-generated child sexual abuse material.

The complaints also allege that SpaceX has falsely represented its AI apps and social network (Grok and X) as generally safe, which the states see as a violation of Virginia's Consumer Protection Act or Oregon's Unlawful Trade Practices Act. The matter is particularly concerning because X allows minors 13 and older to access its content.

Rayfield and Jones both noted in their complaints that harms caused by non-consensual, sexualized images go beyond the victims depicted in the images.

In a statement emailed to CNBC by a spokesperson for SpaceXSI, and posted on the company's @Safety account on X, the company said, "When someone tries to break our safeguards and generate this content, we take appropriate measures, including actioning accounts and notifying law enforcement. Attempting to create this material is a serious crime, and the individuals who do it should be prosecuted to the fullest extent of the law. We will continue to work with the authorities via all available means to pursue these bad actors."

At an NYC Council hearing in October, a SpaceX AI policy leader, Gabriela Castillo Madrid, said the company had appropriate "safeguards" and "strict controls" in place around its AI products.

SpaceX was already facing multiple Grok- and X-related lawsuits, and government probes around the world, including by authorities in the U.K., Spain, Ireland, France and the state of California.

In private-sector lawsuits filed against SpaceX in the U.S. concerning Grok and AI-generated, explicit images, litigation is still in early stages and is tracked by The Center for Internet Technology Addiction.

When the city of Baltimore sued xAI, now owned and operated by SpaceX, the company argued that under Section 230, they were not liable for unwanted images. Section 230 is a federal law that largely shields online platforms from liability for users' content.

SpaceX also sued a user in a federal court in the Northern District of Texas for breach of contract after he allegedly used Grok to create and distribute deepfake explicit images, including depictions of minors. Lawyers for Musk's company argued that Grok should be seen as "a neutral tool, subject to user control," and that any image generation "is the result of the user's prompts and directions."

The new state suits add to legal pressure on SpaceX. Both Virginia and Oregon are seeking injunctions, civil penalties, disgorgement and legal costs, while Virginia is also seeking restitution for consumers from Musk's company.

Oregon submitted its complaint to the Multnomah County Circuit Court on Sunday evening, and Virginia submitted its complaint to the City of Richmond Circuit Court Monday morning. As of early Monday, the suits were pending the courts' acceptance.

State attorneys general have recently succeeded in compelling U.S. social media companies to change their products and pay for alleged past harms.

In August, for example, Meta settled a major federal case centered on allegations that the social media giant misrepresented the level of child mental health problems caused by its apps, Facebook and Instagram. Without admitting to wrongdoing, Meta agreed to pay states around $17 billion over a 10-year period and to make app changes that experts believe will help keep children safer online.

#2868. Tech stocks see monster rally after Jensen Huang returns as Nvidia CEO

Major tech stocks rebounded across the board Monday, sharply reversing Friday's steep sell-off when artificial intelligence stocks fell in the Nasdaq's worst week since 2020.

Shares of Nvidia surged 31.48% after the company announced Monday morning that Jensen Huang was reinstated as CEO. The semiconductor giant gained back nearly $1 trillion in market cap, posting its best day since May 2003.

Nvidia's surge and Huang's reinstatement followed a more than 40% slump in the stock over the past month. The company has been embattled as reports surfaced about Huang's multiple extramarital affairs — one of which was with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes to be a Chinese spy. Huang resigned as CEO of the company he founded on Nov. 12.

The Nasdaq Composite surged 6.51% Monday, for its best day since April 9, 2025. The Nasdaq slid 10% last week, with tech stocks having a rocky week as investors remained nervous about the valuation of some AI companies and business models. All three major averages ended last week in correction territory, and the Nasdaq turned negative for 2026.

Shares of Big Tech companies and other stocks related to the AI trade saw a lift Monday on the Nvidia news.

Shares of Alphabet surged 6.34%, Meta gained 6.55%, Amazon jumped 3.47% and Microsoft increased 1.65%. SpaceX added 4.35%, Arm Holdings surged 16.08% and AMD added 6.85%. Dell and Micron posted some of the sector's biggest gains of the day, gaining 20.46% and 19.04%, respectively. The iShares Semiconductor ETF gained 13.63%.

Neoclouds, which have seen a particularly choppy few months amid AI boom financing concerns, surged on Huang's reinstatement. Nebius soared 20.28%, CoreWeave gained 22.32% and Iren gained 23.86%.

Following a brief premarket surge, Anthropic shares posted a decline of 1.43% in its third day of trading. Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, before closing down 8.5% on Friday.

Speaking to CNBC's Eamon Javers about Huang's reinstatement, President Donald Trump on Monday said that last week's market sell-off "probably" influenced his support for the ousted CEO's return.

"Probably so, but the bigger impact was the long term. The bigger impact was, he's got other chips in his brain that nobody else knows about," Trump said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street."

Trump said he spoke to Nvidia's board directly and that he "convinced" the board to reinstate Huang, who the president said "did nothing wrong."

"That thing's like a rocket ship going in the wrong direction. Now it's a rocket ship going in the right direction, and it's taking the whole market with it," Trump said, referring to Nvidia's stock price.

"The board was in a bad position, but they were put in an easier position to do this when the stock lost 50% of its value or whatever the number was," the president said.

#2863. DNI Jay Clayton tapped as acting national cyber director

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton has been tapped to serve as acting national cyber director while the Trump administration's permanent nominee for the role awaits Senate confirmation, a White House official told CNBC on Monday.

Trump had already added artificial intelligence czar to Clayton's portfolio in October. The official asked not to be named because the pick hasn't been announced.

The White House last week announced that President Donald Trump had picked Kirsten Davies, the Pentagon's chief information officer, to lead the Office of the National Cyber Director.

The U.S. cyber director advises the president on cybersecurity and coordinates U.S. cyber policy across federal agencies. Federal law states that a person may not serve as an acting officer if the president has submitted that person's nomination to the Senate for full appointment and if that person served as first assistant to the office for less than 90 days.

Davies would replace Sean Cairncross, who was confirmed to the role in August 2025 and whose departure was first reported by Axios last week.

Trump, at a White House press conference on Friday, said he decided to replace Cairncross "because I didn't think that person was good enough."

#2860. More Americans pay this Obama-era investment tax every year — how to avoid it

Every year, more Americans pay an extra tax levy on their investment gains — but there's still time to reduce or avoid that bill for 2026, experts say.

Net investment income tax, or NIIT, is a 3.8% levy on investment income once earnings exceed $200,000 for single filers or $250,000 for married couples filing jointly. The tax was enacted in 2010 under President Barack Obama to help fund healthcare via the Affordable Care Act.

Unlike the federal income tax or capital gains brackets, the NIIT earnings limits don't adjust annually for inflation.

As a result, "more clients get hit every year," said certified financial planner Michael Walstedt, founding advisor of Reliant Wealth Advisory in Hoboken, New Jersey.

Roughly 5.6% of tax returns included NIIT for 2025, according to IRS data released in November for returns processed through mid-July 2026. That's up from about 3.7% for the same time frame in 2021.

As more investors pay NIIT, here are some key things to know.

NIIT applies when you have investment income and your earnings exceed the modified adjusted gross income, or MAGI, limits, according to certified financial planner Marianela Collado, CEO of Tobias Financial Advisors in Plantation, Florida.

"The trick is to keep an eye on both of these numbers," said Collado, who is also a certified public accountant and member of CNBC's Financial Advisor Council.

For the NIIT earnings limit, MAGI is typically the same as adjusted gross income, or AGI. One exception would be if you have foreign income, which changes the calculation. You can find last year's AGI on line 11a of your 2025 tax return.

Head of household (with qualifying person): $200,000

Qualifying widow(er) with dependent child: $250,000

NIIT applies to investment income, which includes interest, dividends, annuities, royalties, certain rents, and more. It can also include capital gains from a primary home sale, for profits that exceed the IRS exclusions. The exclusion is $250,000 for single filers or $500,000 for married couples.

The levy stacks on top of regular income tax rates, or capital gains tax — which can be 0%, 15% or 20%, depending on earnings. That means higher earners can pay up to 23.8% on investment income, including long-term capital gains and NIIT.

The NIIT calculation is 3.8% multiplied by the lesser of:

For example, if your MAGI is $30,000 above the NIIT earnings limit and you have $100,000 of investment income, you would pay 3.8% of $30,000, or $1,140.

Alternatively, if your MAGI is $120,000 above the threshold and you have $100,000 of investment income, you would owe 3.8% of $100,000, or $3,800.

As year-end approaches, investors should run projections to estimate their adjusted gross income, which can impact NIIT, among other tax consequences, experts say.

If you're above the NIIT limits, you could reduce AGI by boosting pretax 401(k) deferrals or health savings account contributions, according to Collado with Tobias Financial Advisors.

While employee 401(k) deferrals must happen by Dec. 31, you have until next year's tax deadline for health savings account contributions for 2026.

You might also try to avoid selling assets before year-end, which could further boost AGI, experts say. Of course, any current-year tax moves should consider your long-term financial goals.

Amid recent stock market volatility, investors may also consider so-called tax-loss harvesting, which uses losses to offset other portfolio gains, Walstedt with Reliant Wealth Advisory said.

If your losses exceed profits, you can subtract up to $3,000 from regular income. After that, you can carry remaining losses into future tax years.

However, if you're planning to reinvest the sale proceeds, you need to know the "wash sale rule," which blocks the tax-loss harvesting tax break for buying a "substantially identical" asset within 30 days before or after the sale.

#2857. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2853. ‘This establishes a bottom’: Jensen Huang’s return seen as likely to stop market sell-off, for now

The major averages of U.S. equities surged on Monday after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, giving investors some hope that the relentless selling pressure seen last week may subside — at least temporarily.

The relief rally came after the Dow Jones Industrial Average, S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite ended Friday's session in correction territory. The S&P alone tumbled more than 9% after Huang's departure from the chipmaker was initially announced on Nov. 12 through Friday.

That sell-off may be over — at least temporarily — according to Wall Street experts. But key questions linger around Nvidia and the artificial intelligence trade that has carried the bull market.

"I think the AI trade is back on," said Mark Gibbens, founder of Gibbens Capital Management. "I certainly believe this establishes a bottom and a floor in the AI trade and the recent market weakness."

He added that the news likely doesn't alleviate all concerns that have driven recent declines, including worries about data center approvals after the midterms and fears that the capital expenditures cycle may have peaked. But Gibbens thinks the "keystone" role that Nvidia plays in both financing the AI buildout and the technology's supply chain means a more stable picture of the chipmaker creates a brighter view of the overall market than before.

An encouraging sign that the correction may be over came even before Nvidia's Monday announcement, according to Art Hogan, chief market strategist at B. Riley Wealth Management. He said Friday's selling, which was indiscriminate and affected sectors beyond just AI-exposed companies, showed a bottom may have been building.

"Sometimes you just hit that crescendo where you know the market gets oversold, and I would argue that we'd be higher today, even without the Nvidia news," he said. Indeed, futures tied to the major stock indexes were broadly higher on Sunday night, before any news regarding Huang's return. "So I think that sort of signals we've likely found a place where we priced in, at least the near term, as much of the bad news that was giving markets jitters over the course of the last several weeks."

Strengthening the case for a bottom was that the technology sector's forward earnings valuation was cheaper than that of consumer staples, Hogan said. The S&P 500 consumer staples sector trades at a 20.5 multiple, per FactSet. Tech, meanwhile, trades at 16.3 times forward earnings. Hogan said that discrepancy doesn't square with the earnings outlook for the former sector.

Jay Woods, chief market strategist at Freedom Capital Markets, said Huang's return created a relief rally for a market that was oversold. But he's not sure the headline alone can get the market out of its funk.

"I think the rally will probably fizzle out because now people will pull back and still have the same questions we had before he was replaced," Woods said. Those questions will likely play out in congressional hearings, he speculated. "The market needed this jolt to get us back on our feet, but it is still a counter trend rally in an otherwise near-term downtrend."

Woods, though, said that the lows for the year are likely in, thanks to Friday's wash-out that spread to all sectors beyond those dependent on Nvidia's role in AI.

While investors celebrated Huang's return, the sell-off that accelerated after his departure started before worries about his position even began. That stemmed from concerns about financing the buildout, data center approvals after Democratic wins in the midterm elections and worries that hyperscaler capex may have already peaked or is about to.

That's why Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, is still nervous about the "picks and shovels" companies involved in the AI trade, a group he says includes Nvidia.

"We should enjoy this rally, and maybe it lasts a bit, but I think that picks and shovels trade is going to be solely dependent on what that rate of capex spend is going to be," he said. "That, at the end of the day, is the story that those companies are going to have to deal with outside of bounces like we're seeing now."

Traders at Goldman Sachs put it more succinctly.

"Not sure yet whether good or bad that the fate of the market is almost equal to the fate of one person. But hey ... We are not picky here," they said.

— CNBC's Michelle Fox and Deena Zaidi contributed reporting

#2850. Around the West: Lakers, Spurs continue reigns atop conference

As Thanksgiving approaches, I'm here for my weekly update about the rest of the Western Conference. Both the Los Angeles Lakers and San Antonio Spurs saw their lengthy winning streaks snapped last week but continue to sit atop the West at 14-3 with the third-seeded, 12-5 Minnesota Timberwolves two games back (and tied with two others). While two games separate second from third out West, two games also separate third from ninth, so the separation up top hasn't pervaded throughout the entire playoff landscape.

Net ratings are courtesy of Cleaning the Glass and exclude garbage time.

The Spurs dropped their first game of the month Tuesday against the Detroit Pistons but rebounded with a pair of wins over the Los Angeles Clippers and New Orleans Pelicans, including a 50-point explosion from Victor Wembanyama. That loss to Detroit did, however, provide a double whammy as Dylan Harper suffered a left hip rotator strain and will miss 1-to-3 weeks.

Despite losing a crunch-time affair on Thursday to the streaking New York Knicks (winners of four in a row), the Timberwolves authored an impressive week with three victories in four tries. They nabbed both legs of a back-to-back against the good and feisty Portland Trail Blazers then discarded the Memphis Grizzlies, 104-95, on Saturday for a shiny 3-1 record against three legitimate playoff contenders. That'll do, especially as Anthony Edwards continues to recover from knee surgery.

Among the early surprises of the NBA season, the Grizzlies are playing at 57-win pace through five weeks, despite their five-game winning streak being snapped Saturday by Minnesota. They're top eight in both offensive and defensive rating and are riding a rotation full of good, versatile NBA players to success thus far. It's a treat to watch.

A lean, two-game week saw the Rockets split their contests by beating the Indiana Pacers and dropping a tight one to the Denver Nuggets, which look increasingly potent as the year progresses. Fred VanVleet has played quite well this season in his return from a torn ACL, averaging 14.4 points, 5.7 assists (1.1 turnovers) and 1.1 steals while shooting 39.2% beyond the arc.

After losing to the Phoenix Suns to open the week, Denver notched a couple of mighty fine wins over the Rockets and Boston Celtics before surviving in overtime against the Chicago Bulls on Sunday. They've won seven of their past nine games following a 3-2 start.

Clutch time was rather unkind to these Blazers this past week. Portland lost both legs of a back-to-back set against the Timberwolves — one of which occurred in the final seconds — then handled business against the Milwaukee Bucks before losing to the New York Knicks at Madison Square Garden Saturday night courtesy of a Mikal Bridges game-winning three.

A forgiving stretch of games against the Utah Jazz, Los Angeles Clippers and Stephen Curry-less Golden State Warriors helped Dallas wash away the nasty taste of a brutal three-game losing streak a week and a half ago. The competition ramps up Monday night, though, when the Mavs travel to Denver to face a Nuggets team starting to find its way again.

Not much has come easy for the injury riddled Thunder this season and that was certainly the case during a 1-2 week. They endured close losses to the Atlanta Hawks and Miami Heat sandwiched around a crunch-time victory over the Pelicans on Friday night featuring a 40-point, eight-assist performance from Shai Gilgeous-Alexander.

The Suns scored their niftiest victory of the year last Monday when they defeated the Nuggets in Denver. They backed that up with two more wins to move above .500 for the first time this season and will look for a fourth consecutive victory Monday night against New Orleans.

With an 88-87 victory over the Celtics on Sunday night, led by 18 points, seven rebounds and two assists from Brandin Podziemski, Golden State extinguished its five-game losing streak — in which it lost by an average of 23.2 points — to secure its first win since Stephen Curry went down.

The drop-off from 11th to 12th in the West is severe. There are already 4.5 games separating the Warriors and Pelicans, which have lost nine of their past 10 games after a 4-4 start.

Despite going winless last week, the Clippers did receive some good news when it was announced Brandon Ingram will make his season debut Monday against the Houston Rockets. They'll hope he can invigorate their 24th-ranked offense, which is pretty starved for shot creation beyond Darius Garland.

The Jazz broke their 12-game losing streak with back-to-back wins over the Clippers and Lakers last week. The joy of success appears short-lived, though, as they ended the week with two more losses. They'll try to return to the win column Monday against the Lakers.
